\hypertarget{error-4-keepdim-forgotten}{%
\subsubsection{Error 4: Keepdim
forgotten}\label{error-4-keepdim-forgotten}}

In a positional API, forgetting \texttt{keepdims=True} is one of the
most common tensor bugs:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Bug: mean returns (batch,) not (batch, 1), broadcast is wrong}
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{# missing keepdim=True}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In Einlang, the broadcast requirement is derived from the coordinate
structure, not from a flag:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{mean{[}c{]}(x{[}b,\ c{]})} produces a result with coordinates
\texttt{\{b\}}. The subtraction expects \texttt{\{b,\ c\}}. The
coordinate sets differ: \texttt{\{b,\ c\}} vs \texttt{\{b\}}. The
missing coordinate is \texttt{c}. The compiler infers that the mean must
broadcast back over \texttt{c}. \texttt{keepdims=True} is not a flag the
programmer writes---it's a requirement the compiler derives from the
coordinate structure.

The four errors form a gradient. Error 1 is caught unconditionally (name
mismatch). Error 2 is caught if declarations are consistent (wrong
coordinate set). Error 3 is not caught by the compiler, but the bracket
makes the claim visible to the reader. Error 4 is eliminated
entirely---the compiler derives the keepdims requirement from the
coordinate sets, so the programmer cannot forget it.

\vspace{2em}

We have now covered the four primitives of tensor computation: naming (a
coordinate has an identity), permutation (coordinates move by name, not
position), reduction (the consumed coordinate is named in the bracket),
and broadcasting (the omitted coordinate is visible by its absence).
They all flow from a single idea: \textbf{the megaphone.} A tensor
speaks on some coordinates and stays silent on others. Reduction
silences a coordinate. Broadcast copies along one the tensor was already
silent on. Naming makes the coordinates audible. Permutation moves them
without changing who speaks.

In the next chapter, we begin composing these primitives into
functions---functions whose coordinate contracts are part of their type,
statically checked at every call site.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Find a broadcast in your code. Not one you intentionally
wrote---one that happened because a shape broadcast aligned. Can you
name the coordinate it copies along? Is the broadcast semantically
justified? If you cannot answer both questions, you have found a silence
that should be a claim.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-broadcast-self-audit-three-questions}{%
\subsubsection{The Broadcast Self-Audit: Three
Questions}\label{the-broadcast-self-audit-three-questions}}

Every broadcast in your code is a claim. The claim may be true or false.
But it is always a claim. Here are three questions to ask of any
broadcast you encounter, whether in Einlang or in PyTorch:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinate does the broadcast copy over?} Can you name
  it? If the answer is ``I don't know, the shapes just happened to
  align''---you have found a broadcast that should be explicit. Write
  down the coordinate name. Make the claim visible.
\item
  \textbf{Is the broadcast semantically justified?} Does it make sense
  for this value to be independent of that coordinate? If \texttt{bias}
  broadcasts over \texttt{batch}, does it make sense that bias doesn't
  vary per sample? If the answer is ``I'm not sure''---you have found a
  claim that needs verification. The broadcast might be correct, or it
  might be a bug waiting for the right input shape to expose it.
\item
  \textbf{Will the broadcast survive a refactoring?} If the dimension
  order changes, will this broadcast still copy over the right
  coordinate? If the answer is ``no''---the broadcast depends on
  position, not identity. It will break when the layout changes.
\end{enumerate}

These three questions are the broadcast self-audit. You will see them
again, formalized, in Chapter 4. For now, they are a reading habit.
Every time you see an implicit broadcast, ask the three questions. If
you can't answer all three with confidence, make the broadcast explicit.

Now, actually do this. Open a terminal. Go to your most recent
project---any project with tensor code. Search for patterns where
broadcasting happens implicitly: \texttt{x\ +\ bias},
\texttt{x\ *\ scale}, \texttt{x\ /\ temperature}, \texttt{x\ -\ mean}.
For each match, ask the three questions. Write down the answers.

You don't need to change the code. You don't need to add comments. You
just need to notice: which broadcasts in your code are claims you can
defend, and which are accidents of shape alignment? The accidents are
the ones that will break.

The megaphone model gives you a way to make every broadcast a defended
claim. A bracket that names the spoken coordinates. An omission that
names the silent ones. A coordinate set subtraction that verifies the
broadcast is consistent. The model is simple---a tensor speaks on some
coordinates and stays silent on others. The implications are not simple.
They extend through every operation in the rest of this book.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-3-names-as-contracts}{%
\section{Chapter 3 · Names as
Contracts}\label{chapter-3-names-as-contracts}}

\begin{quote}
``The art of programming is the art of organizing complexity.''

--- Edsger Dijkstra
\end{quote}

\emph{Combinations · Coordinate-aware functions}

\vspace{2em}

We have spent two chapters learning to name coordinates. We name them
when they survive, when they are consumed, when they are copied, and
when they are rearranged. Every operation so far has been a single
statement---a \texttt{let} declaration with brackets.

Real programs are not single statements. They are compositions. A
softmax is not one operation but three---a max reduction, a broadcast
subtraction, an exponentiation, a sum reduction, and a division. Each
step involves coordinates with distinct roles. Composing them naively,
with intermediate \texttt{let} bindings for every step, produces working
code. But it scatters the coordinate story across multiple lines, and it
gives the reader no way to see, at a glance, which coordinates are
structurally load-bearing and which are just passing through.

This chapter introduces the mechanism that turns scattered primitives
into a coherent composition: the \textbf{coordinate-aware function}.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-softmax-decomposition}{%
\subsection{The Softmax Decomposition}\label{the-softmax-decomposition}}

Softmax is the workhorse of classification. It takes a vector of logits
and returns a probability distribution:

\[\text{softmax}(x)_j = \frac{e^{x_j}}{\sum_k e^{x_k}}\]

Decomposed into primitives:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ m = max}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ e}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = exp(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - m);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ z = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(e}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ probs}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = e}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / z;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This works. Every coordinate is named. Every reduction states what it
consumes. But notice the subtle thing that happened: the stability scan
uses \texttt{j} as the scanned coordinate, and the normalization uses
\texttt{k}. These are the \emph{same} coordinate---the class
dimension---used in two different roles. The notation doesn't capture
that identity because the names are local to each statement.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-coordinate-aware-function}{%
\subsection{The Coordinate-Aware
Function}\label{the-coordinate-aware-function}}

What if we could package this pattern---``normalize over the coordinate
named in brackets''---as a single reusable operation, and make the
coordinate name part of its \textbf{contract}?

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ softmax[j](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = max}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ e}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = exp(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ z}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(e}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    e}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / z}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Let's read this carefully. It is the most important code block in the
book.

\texttt{fn\ softmax{[}j{]}}---the \texttt{j} in brackets after the
function name is a \textbf{coordinate parameter}. It is not a value. It
is not a number. It is a coordinate name that the caller supplies at the
call site.

\texttt{x:\ {[}f32;\ ..left,\ j,\ ..right{]}}---the parameter \texttt{x}
is a tensor whose shape includes the coordinate \texttt{j}, plus zero or
more leading coordinates (\texttt{..left}) and zero or more trailing
coordinates (\texttt{..right}). These are \textbf{packs}---they stand
for whatever coordinates surround \texttt{j} in the actual argument.

\texttt{-\textgreater{}\ {[}f32;\ ..left,\ j,\ ..right{]}}---the return
type has the same coordinate structure as the input. Normalization
preserves the shape.

Now the call:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = model(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ p}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The caller writes \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(...)}. The \texttt{class}
in brackets is not a comment. It is not a string. It is a coordinate
argument---the name of the dimension the function will normalize over.
That \texttt{logits} has a \texttt{class} coordinate is checked. If it
doesn't, the call is a compile error.

Compare to the standard API:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{p }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.softmax(logits, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{dim=-1} says ``the last one.'' If the last dimension is
\texttt{class}, this is correct. If upstream changes the dimension
order, \texttt{dim=-1} silently begins normalizing over \texttt{batch},
or \texttt{feature}, or whatever happens to be last. The code runs. The
output is a valid probability distribution---just over the wrong
coordinate.

Now compare to a different Einlang call:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ p}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This normalizes over \texttt{b}---the batch dimension. It is a
one-character bug (\texttt{b} instead of \texttt{class}). It is also a
compile error, because \texttt{softmax{[}b{]}} would attempt to consume
\texttt{b}, and the function signature says \texttt{b} should survive in
\texttt{..left}. The coordinate contract catches the error at the call
site.

Now pause and think about a different kind of failure. You write
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)} and it works. Three months later, a
colleague refactors the model. They rename the \texttt{class} coordinate
to \texttt{category}---a better name, more consistent with the rest of
the codebase. They update twenty-three files. They miss one: the call to
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}}. What happens?

The call is now \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}b,\ category{]})}.
The compiler checks: does \texttt{logits} have a \texttt{class}
coordinate? No---it has \texttt{batch} and \texttt{category}. The error
is not ``shape mismatch.'' It is not ``dimension 1 out of bounds.'' It
is: \textbf{\texttt{logits} has no coordinate named \texttt{class}.} The
error message names the missing coordinate. The fix is to change one
character in the brackets: \texttt{softmax{[}category{]}(...)}.

Now consider the same scenario with \texttt{dim}. The old code is
\texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=1)}. The colleague changes the coordinate
name, not the position. \texttt{dim=1} remains correct. The code
compiles. The code runs. Everything is fine---\emph{this time.} The
position didn't change, only the name did.

But what if the colleague had also changed the dimension order? What if
\texttt{category} moved to position 2? \texttt{dim=1} would silently
begin normalizing over the wrong coordinate. No error. No warning. The
loss goes down. The model deploys.

Here is the distinction in one sentence: \textbf{when positions change
without names changing, \texttt{dim=-1} silently becomes wrong. When
names change without positions changing, \texttt{dim=-1} silently stays
correct. A named coordinate gets the first case wrong and the second
right. A positional API gets the first case wrong and the second
accidentally right.} The named coordinate is predictably correct: it
fails when the name is wrong, and it fails loudly. The positional API is
unpredictably correct: it fails silently when the name drifts from the
position, and it succeeds by luck when they stay aligned.

Neither notation prevents all errors. But named coordinates make the
errors \emph{visible}. A compile error is visible. A silent semantic
drift is not.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{one-coordinate-three-jobs}{%
\subsection{One Coordinate, Three
Jobs}\label{one-coordinate-three-jobs}}

Inside \texttt{softmax{[}j{]}}, the coordinate \texttt{j} plays three
distinct roles:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = max}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{q}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, q, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);    }\CommentTok{// Role 1: stability scan}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ e}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, k, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = exp(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, k, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - m); }\CommentTok{// Role 2: exponentiate}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ z}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(e}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);     }\CommentTok{// Role 3: normalize}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

All three---\texttt{q}, \texttt{k}, \texttt{j}---range over the same
domain (the class axis). But each carries a different \textbf{gradient
contract}. The stability scan passes a sparse gradient: only the maximum
element receives a signal. The denominator scan passes a dense gradient:
every element contributes to the sum. The output has a
diagonal-plus-off-diagonal structure: each element's gradient depends on
itself and on every other element. The coordinate \texttt{j} is consumed
by \texttt{max}, consumed again by \texttt{sum}, and reconstructed by
the division---two different consumption events on the same coordinate,
with the coordinate surviving both.

In a \texttt{dim=-1} API, these three roles collapse into a single
integer. The reader cannot see which role \texttt{dim=-1} plays at each
step. In the named-coordinate version, the roles are given distinct
letters, and a reader can audit whether the gradient contracts are
satisfied.

Now ask yourself: why do we use three different letters (\texttt{q},
\texttt{k}, \texttt{j}) for the same coordinate? Why not just \texttt{j}
everywhere? Because the \emph{binding site} of each occurrence carries
different gradient implications. \texttt{max{[}q{]}} says: ``I consume
\texttt{q} and return a scalar per batch element. The backward pass
through me will broadcast the gradient signal to only the maximum
element.'' \texttt{sum{[}j{]}} says: ``I consume \texttt{j} and return a
scalar per batch element. The backward pass through me will broadcast
the gradient signal to \emph{all} elements.'' Same coordinate domain.
Different gradient contracts. Different letters make each contract's
scope visible: \texttt{q} is consumed by \texttt{max} and never seen
again. \texttt{k} is used in the exponent and survives. \texttt{j} is
consumed by \texttt{sum} and reconstructed by the division.

The letters are not decoration. They are the scope markers for the
coordinate's three lives. In \texttt{dim=-1}, all three are the same
integer. The scopes are invisible.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-gets-checked-at-the-call-site}{%
\subsection{What Gets Checked at the Call
Site}\label{what-gets-checked-at-the-call-site}}

Let's trace exactly what happens when you call
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)}.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  The compiler looks up \texttt{softmax}'s signature: coordinate
  parameter \texttt{j}, value parameter \texttt{x} with layout
  \texttt{{[}..left,\ j,\ ..right{]}}.
\item
  The coordinate argument \texttt{class} is bound to \texttt{j}.
\item
  The value argument \texttt{logits} is checked: does it have a
  coordinate named \texttt{class}? Yes---\texttt{logits} was declared as
  \texttt{logits{[}batch,\ class{]}}.
\item
  The packs are bound: \texttt{..left} = \texttt{{[}batch{]}},
  \texttt{..right} = \texttt{{[}{]}} (nothing on the right).
\item
  The return type is instantiated: \texttt{{[}f32;\ batch,\ class{]}}.
\item
  The call is valid.
\end{enumerate}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{follow-along-be-the-compiler}{%
\subsubsection{Follow Along: Be the
Compiler}\label{follow-along-be-the-compiler}}

Stop. You just read the six steps. Now do them yourself, for a different
call. Take two minutes. No scrolling ahead.

The function signature is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ softmax[j](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The call is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ probs}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Walk through the six steps. Write down: 1. Which coordinate parameter is
being bound? 2. Which coordinate argument is bound to it? 3. Does
\texttt{logits} carry that coordinate? 4. What are \texttt{..left} and
\texttt{..right} bound to? 5. What is the return type? 6. Is the call
valid?

Done? Compare.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
Step & Your answer\tabularnewline
\midrule
\endhead
1. Coordinate parameter & \texttt{j}\tabularnewline
2. Coordinate argument & \texttt{c}, bound to \texttt{j}\tabularnewline
3. Does \texttt{logits} carry \texttt{c}? &
Yes---\texttt{logits{[}b,\ c{]}}\tabularnewline
4. Pack bindings & \texttt{..left} = \texttt{{[}b{]}}, \texttt{..right}
= \texttt{{[}{]}}\tabularnewline
5. Return type & \texttt{{[}f32;\ b,\ c{]}}\tabularnewline
6. Valid? & Yes\tabularnewline
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

You just simulated the compiler's contract check. The six steps are
mechanical---no intuition, no shape arithmetic, no guessing. For every
function call, the compiler performs these six steps. For every function
call, the answer is yes or no. If no, the compiler emits an error naming
the missing coordinate.

Now do it again for a call that should fail:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ probs}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, f}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, f}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Walk through the steps. Where does it fail?

Answer: Step 3. \texttt{logits} carries \texttt{b} and \texttt{f}.
\texttt{c} is the coordinate argument. \texttt{c} is not in
\texttt{\{b,\ f\}}. The compiler reports: ``\texttt{logits} has no
coordinate named \texttt{c}. Available coordinates: \texttt{b},
\texttt{f}.''

The error message names the missing coordinate and the available ones.
The programmer looks at it and thinks: ``Oh, I meant
\texttt{softmax{[}f{]}(logits{[}b,\ f{]})}.'' One character fix. Caught
before execution.

Now consider five wrong calls:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
\begin{minipage}[b]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Call\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
What goes wrong\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Caught by\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\midrule
\endhead
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}batch,\ feature{]})}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{logits} has no \texttt{class}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Step 3: index existence\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{softmax{[}batch{]}(logits{[}batch,\ class{]})}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{batch} would be consumed, but \texttt{softmax} preserves
\texttt{j} in the return type---\texttt{batch} would be consumed and
returned, which is a contract violation\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Step 5: return type enforcement\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)} where \texttt{logits} is 1D\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{class} exists but \texttt{..left} and \texttt{..right} are both
empty---valid\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
No error (this is correct)\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}batch,\ class,\ extra{]})}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{..right} binds to \texttt{{[}extra{]}}, which is
fine---\texttt{softmax} is polymorphic over trailing dims\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
No error (this is correct)\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}batch,\ class{]},\ wrong\_arg)}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Wrong number of value arguments\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Step 1: arity check\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

Each check is a single, mechanical verification. No shape guessing. No
positional arithmetic. The coordinate names and the function signature
together form the contract. The call site satisfies it---or doesn't.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{function-composition}{%
\subsection{Function Composition}\label{function-composition}}

Coordinate-aware functions compose. The output of one becomes the input
of another, and the coordinate contracts chain.

Consider a pipeline: linear layer, then softmax:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ linear[in, out](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, W: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; out, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, b: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, out}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    sum}\OperatorTok{[}\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * W}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{out, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) + b}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{out}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ pipeline[in, class](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, W: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; class, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, b: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = linear}\OperatorTok{[}\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, W}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, b}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The coordinate \texttt{class} flows from the pipeline's signature
through \texttt{linear{[}in,\ class{]}} into the result \texttt{logits},
then into \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}}. At each step, the compiler
checks: does the argument carry the coordinate the function expects?
\texttt{linear} expects \texttt{in} and \texttt{out}---the caller binds
\texttt{class} to \texttt{out}. \texttt{logits} now carries
\texttt{class}. \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}} expects \texttt{class} on
its argument---\texttt{logits} has it. The chain is verified.

If a refactoring changes \texttt{linear}'s output coordinate from
\texttt{class} to \texttt{category}, the pipeline still
compiles---\texttt{linear{[}in,\ category{]}} produces a tensor with
\texttt{category}, and \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}} complains that
\texttt{category} is not \texttt{class}. The error is at the composition
boundary. The compiler names both coordinates. The mismatch is visible.

Positional composition has no such check.
\texttt{logits\ =\ linear(x);\ softmax(logits,\ dim=-1)}. If
\texttt{linear}'s output layout changes, \texttt{dim=-1} silently
normalizes over a different coordinate. The chain is unverified.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-contract-in-one-question}{%
\subsection{The Contract in One
Question}\label{the-contract-in-one-question}}

Every coordinate-aware function can be audited with a single question:
\textbf{does the caller's argument carry the coordinate that the
function claims to operate on?}

If yes: the call compiles, and the coordinate flow is guaranteed
consistent. If no: the call is rejected, and the error message names the
missing coordinate.

This is the difference between a type-level contract and a
documentation-level contract. \texttt{def\ softmax(logits,\ dim=-1)} has
a documentation-level contract: the docstring says \texttt{dim} is the
class dimension. But nothing checks it. \texttt{fn\ softmax{[}j{]}(x)}
has a type-level contract: \texttt{j} is a coordinate parameter, and the
compiler verifies that the argument carries it. The contract is not a
hope. It is a check.

The rest of this book builds on this distinction. Every chapter from
here forward---skeletons, recurrences, gradients, comparisons---assumes
that coordinate identities can be checked. The coordinate-aware function
is the mechanism that makes checking possible.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-square-matrix-test}{%
\subsection{The Square Matrix Test}\label{the-square-matrix-test}}

There is a simple, brutal test for whether a piece of tensor code is
robust to coordinate swaps. Set all dimension sizes equal. Swap two
axes. Ask: does the program still mean the same thing?

For a square input where
\texttt{batch\_size\ ==\ num\_classes\ ==\ 128}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ probs}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);   }\CommentTok{// correct}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ probs}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class, batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class, batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);   }\CommentTok{// bug}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Both lines produce a \texttt{(128,\ 128)} matrix where every row sums to
1. The cross-entropy loss descends identically. The training curves
overlay perfectly. But the first normalizes classes against each other.
The second normalizes examples against each other.

When \texttt{batch\_size\ ==\ num\_classes}, the probability matrix is
square. Softmax over rows and softmax over columns produce the same
numbers when the matrix has symmetric structure. The loss curves
overlay. The calibration reports pass. Six weeks later, a deployed model
silently normalizes examples against each other instead of classes
against each other.

\begin{figure}
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/softmax_roles.pdf}
\caption{The Square Matrix Test: when dimensions have equal extent, only
the coordinate name records which meaning was intended}
\end{figure}

No shape checker catches this. No gradient check catches this. Only a
notation that records \emph{which coordinate is the distribution}
catches this. \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)} records it.
\texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=-1)} does not.

The Square Matrix Test is named after this property: when all extents
are equal, a coordinate swap can hide inside shape compatibility. If
square matrices fool shape checkers---and they do, routinely---what can
prevent this class of error?

The Square Matrix Test is not specific to softmax. It applies to any
operation where two coordinates can have equal extents. Consider three
more cases:

\textbf{Matrix multiplication.}
\texttt{C{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ sum{[}k{]}(A{[}i,\ k{]}\ *\ B{[}k,\ j{]})} and
\texttt{C{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ sum{[}k{]}(A{[}k,\ i{]}\ *\ B{[}k,\ j{]})}
produce the same shape when \texttt{A} is square. The first uses
\texttt{A}'s rows. The second uses \texttt{A}'s columns (equivalent to
\texttt{A\^{}T\ @\ B}). When \texttt{A} is a square matrix of size
\texttt{128\ ×\ 128}, both expressions produce a \texttt{128\ ×\ 128}
output. Shape checkers see the same shape. Only the coordinate names
distinguish the two computations.

\textbf{Broadcast.}
\texttt{let\ out{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ A{[}i,\ j{]}\ +\ bias{[}j{]}} and
\texttt{let\ out{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ A{[}i,\ j{]}\ +\ bias{[}i{]}} both
produce shape \texttt{(128,\ 128)} when \texttt{i} and \texttt{j} have
the same extent. The first broadcasts \texttt{bias} over rows---bias
depends on columns. The second broadcasts \texttt{bias} over
columns---bias depends on rows. Semantically opposite. Shape-identical.
Distinguishable only by which coordinate appears in \texttt{bias}'s
bracket.

\textbf{Attention.} Self-attention and cross-attention use the same
\texttt{matmul(Q,\ K.transpose(-2,\ -1))} operation. When
\texttt{seq\_q\ ==\ seq\_k}, the code is textually identical (Chapter 9
will explore this in detail). The distinction between attending to
yourself and attending to a different sequence lives in the tensor
shapes at runtime---not in the source code. When the sequence lengths
differ, the shapes diverge and the bug surfaces. During development,
when they happen to be equal, the bug is invisible.

In every case, the Square Matrix Test reveals the same gap: shape
compatibility checks the arithmetic of dimensions. It does not check the
identity of dimensions. When two dimensions have the same size, shape
compatibility becomes identity-blind. Named coordinates restore sight.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-refactoring-a-detailed-demonstration}{%
\subsection{The Refactoring: A Detailed
Demonstration}\label{the-refactoring-a-detailed-demonstration}}

Let's walk through a refactoring in both notations, step by step, to see
where the errors surface---and where they don't.

\textbf{The setup.} A model with three files: \texttt{data.rs} (declares
\texttt{logits{[}batch,\ class{]}}), \texttt{model.rs} (calls
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)}), and \texttt{loss.rs} (calls
\texttt{cross\_entropy{[}class{]}(probs,\ labels)}).

\textbf{The refactoring.} A colleague decides that \texttt{class} is a
poor name---it suggests a classification head, but the dimension is more
general. It should be called \texttt{category}. The colleague renames
the coordinate in \texttt{data.rs}:
\texttt{logits{[}batch,\ category{]}}.

Now the colleague recompiles the project. Two errors appear:

\begin{verbatim}
error[E003]: model.rs:42: tensor `logits` has no coordinate named `class`
  --> model.rs:42:20
   |
42 |     softmax[class](logits);
   |                    ^^^^^^ `logits` has coordinates: batch, category
   |     help: did you mean `category`?

error[E003]: loss.rs:15: tensor `probs` has no coordinate named `class`
  --> loss.rs:15:25
   |
15 |     cross_entropy[class](probs, labels);
   |                         ^^^^^ `probs` has coordinates: batch, category
   |     help: did you mean `category`?
\end{verbatim}

The colleague fixes both errors: \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}} →
\texttt{softmax{[}category{]}}, \texttt{cross\_entropy{[}class{]}} →
\texttt{cross\_entropy{[}category{]}}. The project compiles. The
refactoring is complete. The compiler verified that every use of
\texttt{class} was updated.

Now replay the same scenario with a positional API.

\textbf{The setup.} \texttt{data.py}: \texttt{logits} has shape
\texttt{(batch,\ class)}. \texttt{model.py}:
\texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=1)}. \texttt{loss.py}:
\texttt{cross\_entropy(probs,\ labels,\ dim=1)}.

\textbf{The refactoring.} The colleague changes the comment in
\texttt{data.py}: the shape is now conceptually
\texttt{(batch,\ category)}. The tensor shape doesn't change.
\texttt{dim=1} is still \texttt{1}. Every \texttt{dim=1} in the codebase
is still correct---because the position didn't change.

The project compiles. The tests pass. The refactoring is ``complete.''

But what if the colleague had also changed the dimension order? What if
the new layout were \texttt{(category,\ batch)}? The colleague updates
the comment. The colleague does not update every \texttt{dim=1} in the
codebase---there are twenty-three of them across eight files. Some
should become \texttt{dim=0}. Some should stay \texttt{dim=1}. The
colleague updates the ones they remember. The ones they forget compile
silently.

The compiler does not complain. \texttt{dim=1} is always a valid
integer. The fact that some \texttt{dim=1}s should now be
\texttt{dim=0}s is not recorded anywhere the compiler can see.

The difference between the two refactorings is not the number of files
changed. It is the number of errors emitted. The Einlang refactoring
emits two errors---one for each call site that uses the old coordinate
name. The positional refactoring emits zero errors---even when the
dimension order changes and some \texttt{dim=1}s should become
\texttt{dim=0}s. Zero errors is not zero bugs. It is zero
\emph{detected} bugs.

The coordinate name is the audit trail. When you rename it, every use of
the old name becomes a compile error. You fix them one by one. When
they're all fixed, the project compiles. The compiler has verified that
the rename is complete.

A positional integer has no audit trail. When you change what position 1
means, no compile error points you to the places that depended on the
old meaning. You find them by reading code, by running tests, by
deploying to staging and hoping the metrics don't drift. The difference
between an audit trail and no audit trail is the difference between a
refactoring that takes ten minutes and a refactoring that takes three
weeks to debug.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-language-gets-a-name}{%
\subsection{The Language Gets a Name}\label{the-language-gets-a-name}}

We have been writing in a notation that puts coordinate names in
brackets, that requires reductions to state what they consume, that
makes broadcasting explicit in the indexing pattern. This notation needs
a name.

It is called \textbf{Einlang}---a contraction of ``Einstein'' and
``language,'' acknowledging the debt to Einstein summation notation
while distinguishing itself as a full programming language rather than a
string-based convention.

The name is not the point. The point is what the name represents: a
language where coordinates are first-class syntactic entities, not
comments embedded in variable names. Where coordinate contracts are
statically checked. Where the reader can audit coordinate flow without
reconstructing it from shape arithmetic.

\vspace{2em}

A coordinate-aware function does something that no positional API can
do: it makes the \textbf{identity} of the operated-on coordinate part of
the function's type-level contract. The caller must name the coordinate.
The name is checked against the argument's layout. The function body
uses the name without knowing its position.

This is the combination layer. The primitives---naming, permuting,
reducing, broadcasting---are composed into a function whose coordinate
behavior is specified in its signature. The function can be called,
passed around, and composed further, without losing the coordinate
information that the primitives established.

But there is a subtler consequence. When coordinates are part of the
type-level contract, refactoring becomes checkable. Rename a coordinate
from \texttt{class} to \texttt{category}, and every call site that
passes \texttt{class} becomes a compile error. The error message names
the call site and the missing coordinate. The refactoring is systematic:
change all \texttt{class} to \texttt{category}, and the errors disappear
one by one. No silent breakage. No ``hope I found all the places.'' The
compiler is the audit trail.

In a positional API, renaming a dimension's role (e.g., changing what
``position 1'' means) produces no errors. The code compiles. The integer
didn't change. Only the meaning changed. The compiler can't track
meaning. It tracks integers. The refactoring is silent---and its bugs
are silent with it.

This is the argument of the book, stated at the combination layer: when
the coordinate identity is part of the function's contract, the compiler
can audit every use of that identity. When the identity is in a comment,
the audit is yours to perform manually. The difference between the two
is the difference between a check and a hope.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Design a coordinate-aware function. Write its signature. Now
imagine a colleague calls it with the wrong coordinate name. What
happens? In \texttt{dim=-1}, nothing---the call succeeds silently. In
your function, the compiler stops and says: ``this tensor has no
coordinate named X.'' The difference is a bracket with a name in it.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-audit-your-own-call-sites}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: Audit Your Own Call
Sites}\label{stop-and-think-audit-your-own-call-sites}}

Open a file in your current project that calls a function with a
\texttt{dim} or \texttt{axis} argument. Any function---\texttt{softmax},
\texttt{mean}, \texttt{sum}, \texttt{concat}, \texttt{norm}. Find three
calls. For each call, answer:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{What coordinate does \texttt{dim} refer to?} Not what
  integer---what coordinate. Is it \texttt{batch}? \texttt{channel}?
  \texttt{sequence}? \texttt{feature}? Write it down.
\item
  \textbf{Could the dimension order change in a future refactoring?} Be
  honest. If you answer ``probably not,'' ask: did you answer the same
  way about every refactoring that has already happened in this
  codebase?
\item
  \textbf{If the dimension order changed, would this \texttt{dim} still
  be correct?} If the answer is no---if \texttt{dim=1} would silently
  point to a different coordinate---you have found a call site that
  depends on a position, not an identity.
\end{enumerate}

Now imagine each of those calls was written as
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)} instead. Would the answers change?

Question 1: the answer is in the bracket---\texttt{class}. Question 2:
it doesn't matter. \texttt{class} is \texttt{class} regardless of
position. Question 3: yes. \texttt{class} still means \texttt{class}
even after a layout change.

The named version answers all three questions with the same information:
the name in the bracket. The positional version answers question 1 with
context you must hold in your head, question 2 with a guess, and
question 3 with ``probably not.''

You don't need to rewrite your codebase in Einlang. You need to notice
that every \texttt{dim=1} in your codebase is a question that the
notation can't answer. The answer is in your head, or in a comment, or
in a variable naming convention. The answer is not in the code, where
the compiler could read it.

The coordinate habit is noticing that gap. You have now noticed it in
your own code. The rest of this book is about what happens when you fill
it.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{coordinate-parameters-vs.value-parameters}{%
\subsection{Coordinate Parameters vs.~Value
Parameters}\label{coordinate-parameters-vs.value-parameters}}

A coordinate-aware function has two kinds of parameters, and the
distinction matters.

\textbf{Coordinate parameters} (in brackets after the function name:
\texttt{fn\ softmax{[}j{]}}) name the coordinate the function operates
on. They control the structure of the computation---which axis is
reduced, which axis is broadcast, which axis is preserved. They are not
values; you cannot do arithmetic on them. They are compile-time
identities.

\textbf{Value parameters} (in the parenthesized argument list:
\texttt{x:\ {[}f32;\ ..left,\ j,\ ..right{]}}) hold the data. Their
shapes reference the coordinate parameters. They are the tensors the
function transforms.

The separation is syntactic but the implication is semantic. The
coordinate parameter says: ``this function's behavior depends on the
identity of this coordinate, not its position.'' The value parameter
says: ``this function consumes this tensor.'' A function that takes
\texttt{j} as a coordinate parameter and \texttt{x} as a value parameter
cannot confuse which is which. The brackets hold the coordinate. The
parentheses hold the value.

At the call site, the same separation applies:
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)}. \texttt{class} is in brackets---it
is a coordinate argument. \texttt{logits} is in parentheses---it is a
value argument. The syntax tells the reader which is which. In
\texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=-1)}, \texttt{dim=-1} is syntactically
identical to any other integer argument. The syntax does not distinguish
``this integer controls the axis of reduction'' from ``this integer is a
value being passed to the function.'' The distinction lives in the
documentation. The named version puts it in the syntax.

This separation between coordinate parameters and value parameters is
what enables the compiler to check the coordinate contract. Without it,
a coordinate name passed to a function would be just another string or
symbol---the compiler couldn't know it was supposed to be verified. The
brackets create a syntactic position that the compiler recognizes as
``coordinate argument.'' Everything in that position gets checked.
Everything outside it gets normal type-checking. The syntax carves out a
space for coordinate verification.

In the next chapter, we explore how coordinate-aware functions compose
into reusable skeletons---patterns that are identical across softmax,
LayerNorm, RMSNorm, and GroupNorm, differing only in which coordinates
play which roles.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-4-the-broadcast-self-audit}{%
\section{Chapter 4 · The Broadcast
Self-Audit}\label{chapter-4-the-broadcast-self-audit}}

\begin{quote}
``Silence is not absence. Silence is a claim. And claims can be
checked.''

--- The author, after a 4 AM broadcast bug
\end{quote}

\emph{Combinations · The inversion rule: what broadcasts forward
collects backward}

\vspace{2em}

You have now seen two coordinate operations: reduction (which consumes a
coordinate) and broadcasting (which copies along one). Chapter 2
introduced them as separate primitives. Chapter 3 showed how
coordinate-aware functions thread them through signatures.

But there is a deeper relationship between them---one that this chapter
is dedicated to making explicit. It is the relationship that governs
every gradient, every parameter update, every backward pass you will
ever write. And it can be stated in one sentence:

\textbf{What broadcasts in the forward pass is reduced in the backward
pass. What is reduced in the forward pass is broadcast in the backward
pass.}

This is the Inversion Rule. This chapter is about what it means, how to
check it, and why it catches the class of bugs that are shape-correct
but semantically wrong.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{two-lines-two-directions}{%
\subsection{Two Lines, Two Directions}\label{two-lines-two-directions}}

Read these two lines:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Forward}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = A}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + bias}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}

\CommentTok{// Backward: gradient of bias}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ d_bias}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(d_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Forward: \texttt{bias{[}j{]}} omits \texttt{i} in its index pattern. The
coordinate \texttt{i} is absent. So \texttt{bias} is copied along
\texttt{i}---every position along \texttt{i} receives the same
\texttt{bias} value. This is a broadcast.

Backward: \texttt{d\_bias{[}j{]}} is the gradient with respect to
\texttt{bias}. How do we get it? \texttt{d\_out{[}i,\ j{]}} carries
gradient signals from every \texttt{(i,\ j)} position. \texttt{bias}
contributed to all of them equally. To update \texttt{bias}, we must
collect all those signals. The collection is a sum---over \texttt{i}.
The coordinate that was broadcast forward is reduced backward.

Now the inverse:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Forward}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ row_sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(matrix}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}

\CommentTok{// Backward: gradient of matrix}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ d_matrix}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = d_row_sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;  }\CommentTok{// broadcast j back}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Forward: \texttt{sum{[}j{]}} consumes \texttt{j}. The output
\texttt{row\_sum{[}i{]}} has no \texttt{j}. Every \texttt{j} position
was collapsed into a single sum.

Backward: \texttt{d\_row\_sum{[}i{]}} is the gradient with respect to
\texttt{row\_sum}. To send it back to \texttt{matrix{[}i,\ j{]}}, we
must broadcast \texttt{d\_row\_sum} along \texttt{j}---every \texttt{j}
position receives the same gradient signal. The coordinate that was
reduced forward is broadcast backward.

Two lines. Two directions. One rule. The Inversion Rule is not a
separate piece of mathematics bolted onto the coordinate system. It is
the coordinate system, read in reverse.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-self-audit-three-questions}{%
\subsection{The Self-Audit: Three
Questions}\label{the-self-audit-three-questions}}

Now apply this to your own code. Every broadcast you write is a claim.
The claim is: \emph{this value does not depend on the coordinate I am
omitting.} If the claim is false, the forward pass is wrong. If the
claim is true but the backward pass doesn't reduce over the omitted
coordinate, the gradient is wrong.

Three questions for every broadcast:

\textbf{Question 1: What coordinate am I broadcasting over?} Is the name
visible in the code, or is it inferred from position?

In \texttt{out{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ A{[}i,\ j{]}\ +\ bias{[}j{]}}, the omitted
coordinate is \texttt{i}. The code says so: \texttt{A} has
\texttt{(i,\ j)}, \texttt{bias} has \texttt{(j)}. The difference is
\texttt{\{i\}}. The broadcast is visible in the index patterns.

In \texttt{out\ =\ A\ +\ bias}, the broadcast is invisible. The shapes
determine what happens. If \texttt{A} is \texttt{(32,\ 64)} and
\texttt{bias} is \texttt{(64,)}, NumPy broadcasts along axis 0. The code
doesn't say which coordinate is being broadcast over. You infer it from
shapes.

\textbf{Question 2: Is independence genuinely justified?} Does the
broadcast value genuinely not depend on that coordinate?

A bias term in a linear layer should not depend on the batch index. Each
sample gets the same bias. The broadcast over \texttt{batch} is
semantically justified.

A temperature scaling factor in a softmax should not depend on the class
index. The broadcast over \texttt{class} is semantically justified.

But what about a mask that you broadcast over the sequence length? If
the mask depends on the sequence position---if later positions are
masked differently than earlier ones---then broadcasting a single mask
value over all positions is semantically wrong. The shapes would work.
The code would run. But the mask would not encode the position-dependent
pattern you intended.

The broadcast self-audit asks: \emph{is this broadcast a computational
convenience, or a semantic claim?} If it is a semantic claim, is the
claim true?

\textbf{Question 3: What will the gradient do?} Does the backward
reduction produce the right shape for the parameter update?

In \texttt{d\_bias{[}j{]}\ =\ sum{[}i{]}(d\_out{[}i,\ j{]})}, the sum
over \texttt{i} produces a gradient of shape \texttt{(j)}---exactly
\texttt{bias}'s shape. The parameter update
\texttt{bias\ -=\ lr\ *\ d\_bias} is well-shaped.

But what if you wrote the broadcast differently? What if \texttt{bias}
had shape \texttt{(1,\ j)} and broadcasting expanded it to
\texttt{(i,\ j)}? The gradient would still be
\texttt{sum{[}i{]}(d\_out{[}i,\ j{]})}, producing \texttt{(j,)}. If your
optimizer expects \texttt{(1,\ j)}, you need a reshape. The reshape is a
positional hack to make the shapes align. The named version produces the
correct shape by construction---the gradient has the same coordinates as
the parameter.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-auditors-toolkit}{%
\subsection{The Auditor's Toolkit}\label{the-auditors-toolkit}}

Let's build a systematic procedure. Given any expression that contains a
broadcast, you can audit it with these steps:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{List the coordinate sets.} Write down the coordinates of every
  tensor in the expression.
\item
  \textbf{Compute the broadcast sets.} For each term, subtract its
  coordinate set from the output coordinate set. The difference is what
  that term broadcasts over.
\item
  \textbf{Check justification.} For each broadcast, ask: is it
  semantically correct for this term to be independent of these
  coordinates?
\item
  \textbf{Predict the gradient.} For each broadcast, write the backward
  reduction: sum over the broadcast set. Verify that the result has the
  same coordinates as the parameter.
\end{enumerate}

Let's apply this to a realistic example. Here is a layer normalization
with a learnable scale and shift:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ layer_norm[feature](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                        gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                        beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{2.0}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    (centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / (var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)) * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Step through the auditor's toolkit.

\textbf{Step 1: Coordinate sets.}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{x}: \texttt{\{..batch,\ feature\}}
\item
  \texttt{mean}: \texttt{\{..batch\}} --- \texttt{feature} was consumed
  by \texttt{mean{[}feature{]}}
\item
  \texttt{centered}: \texttt{\{..batch,\ feature\}}
\item
  \texttt{var}: \texttt{\{..batch\}} --- \texttt{feature} was consumed
  by \texttt{mean{[}feature{]}}
\item
  \texttt{gamma}: \texttt{\{feature\}}
\item
  \texttt{beta}: \texttt{\{feature\}}
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 2: Broadcast sets.}

The final expression is
\texttt{centered\ /\ (var\ **\ 0.5\ +\ eps)\ *\ gamma\ +\ beta}. The
output coordinates are \texttt{\{..batch,\ feature\}}.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{centered}: has \texttt{\{..batch,\ feature\}}. Broadcast set =
  \texttt{\{\}}. No broadcast.
\item
  \texttt{var}: has \texttt{\{..batch\}}. Broadcast set =
  \texttt{\{feature\}}. \texttt{var} broadcasts over \texttt{feature}.
\item
  \texttt{gamma}: has \texttt{\{feature\}}. Broadcast set =
  \texttt{\{..batch\}}. \texttt{gamma} broadcasts over \texttt{..batch}.
\item
  \texttt{beta}: has \texttt{\{feature\}}. Broadcast set =
  \texttt{\{..batch\}}. \texttt{beta} broadcasts over \texttt{..batch}.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 3: Justification.}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{var} broadcasts over \texttt{feature}: justified. The variance
  is computed per-batch-element, then applied to all features. This is
  the definition of layer normalization.
\item
  \texttt{gamma} broadcasts over \texttt{..batch}: justified.
  \texttt{gamma} is a per-feature parameter. Every batch element gets
  the same scale.
\item
  \texttt{beta} broadcasts over \texttt{..batch}: justified. Same
  reasoning as \texttt{gamma}.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 4: Gradient prediction.}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{d\_var{[}..batch{]}\ =\ sum{[}feature{]}(d\_out{[}..batch,\ feature{]}\ *\ ...)}.
  The gradient sums over \texttt{feature}---the broadcast set. Result:
  \texttt{\{..batch\}}, matching \texttt{var}.
\item
  \texttt{d\_gamma{[}feature{]}\ =\ sum{[}..batch{]}(d\_out{[}..batch,\ feature{]}\ *\ ...)}.
  The gradient sums over \texttt{..batch}---the broadcast set. Result:
  \texttt{\{feature\}}, matching \texttt{gamma}.
\item
  \texttt{d\_beta{[}feature{]}\ =\ sum{[}..batch{]}(d\_out{[}..batch,\ feature{]})}.
  Same. Result: \texttt{\{feature\}}, matching \texttt{beta}.
\end{itemize}

Every gradient has the same coordinates as its parameter. The broadcast
sets from Step 2 become the reduction sets in Step 4. The Inversion
Rule, applied mechanically.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{when-the-audit-fails}{%
\subsection{When the Audit Fails}\label{when-the-audit-fails}}

Now let's see what happens when a broadcast is shape-correct but
semantically wrong.

A programmer writes a temperature-scaled softmax:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = model(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scaled}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / temperature;}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ probs}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(scaled}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{temperature} is a scalar. It broadcasts over both \texttt{batch}
and \texttt{class}. The auditor asks Question 2: is independence
justified? Yes---temperature is a global scaling factor that applies
uniformly to all logits.

But the programmer intended \texttt{temperature} to be
per-class---different classes get different temperatures. They wrote:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ temperature}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = get_per_class_temperature();}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scaled}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / temperature}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Now \texttt{temperature} broadcasts over \texttt{batch} but \emph{not}
over \texttt{class}. The auditor asks: is \texttt{temperature}
independent of \texttt{batch}? Yes---all batch elements share the same
per-class temperatures. Is \texttt{temperature} independent of
\texttt{class}? No---and the index pattern
\texttt{temperature{[}class{]}} does \emph{not} broadcast over
\texttt{class}. The code is correct.

Now suppose the programmer accidentally wrote:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ temperature = get_per_class_temperature();  }\CommentTok{// returns scalar by mistake}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scaled}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / temperature;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{temperature} is a scalar broadcasting over everything. The
shapes work. The code runs. But the per-class variation is gone---every
class gets the same temperature. The loss descends but plateaus higher.
The bug is a broadcast that is wider than intended.

The auditor's Question 2 catches this: ``is independence genuinely
justified?'' The programmer intended \texttt{temperature} to depend on
\texttt{class}. But the scalar \texttt{temperature} is independent of
\texttt{class}---it broadcasts over it. The broadcast is a claim that
\texttt{temperature} doesn't depend on \texttt{class}. The claim is
false. The audit fails.

In a positional framework, \texttt{temperature} is just a number.
\texttt{logits\ /\ temperature} is valid regardless of whether
\texttt{temperature} should be per-class or global. The positional
notation doesn't distinguish between ``this is a scalar because it's
genuinely global'' and ``this is a scalar because I forgot to make it
per-class.'' The broadcast self-audit forces the distinction.

Now consider a more subtle failure---one that the audit catches but
positional debugging would miss for hours. A programmer is implementing
a weighted loss:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ losses}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = cross_entropy_per_class(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, labels}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ class_weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = get_class_weights();}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ weighted}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(losses}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * class_weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{class\_weights{[}class{]}} broadcasts over \texttt{batch}. The
audit asks: is \texttt{class\_weights} independent of \texttt{batch}?
Yes---class weights are per-class, shared across all batch elements. The
broadcast is justified.

Now the programmer refactors, making class weights adaptive based on
batch statistics:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ class_weights = compute_adaptive_weights(losses);  }\CommentTok{// BUG: returns scalar by accident}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ weighted}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(losses}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{batch, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * class_weights);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{compute\_adaptive\_weights} was supposed to return
\texttt{{[}f32;\ class{]}} but returns a scalar. The audit catches this:
\texttt{class\_weights} broadcasts over both \texttt{batch} and
\texttt{class}. But \texttt{class\_weights} should depend on
\texttt{class}---the broadcast over \texttt{class} is semantically
wrong. The loss will compile, run, and descend. But every class gets the
same weight. The adaptive weighting is silently disabled.

In a positional framework, this bug is invisible to shapes.
\texttt{(batch,\ class)\ *\ scalar} is valid.
\texttt{(batch,\ class)\ *\ (class,)} is also valid---it broadcasts
differently. The shape of \texttt{class\_weights} determines the
behavior. If \texttt{compute\_adaptive\_weights} accidentally returns a
scalar, the shapes still work, the broadcast is different, and the
behavior silently changes.

The Einlang compiler catches this if the return type of
\texttt{compute\_adaptive\_weights} is declared:
\texttt{fn\ compute\_adaptive\_weights(...)\ -\textgreater{}\ {[}f32;\ class{]}}.
If the function body returns a scalar, the return type check fails. The
coordinate contract at the function boundary catches the error before
the broadcast self-audit ever runs. The audit catches what the contract
doesn't; the contract catches what the audit doesn't. They are
complementary lines of defense.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-inversion-rule-in-one-diagram}{%
\subsection{The Inversion Rule in One
Diagram}\label{the-inversion-rule-in-one-diagram}}

\begin{verbatim}
Forward                         Backward
-------                         --------

Reduction consumes {j}           →    Broadcast {j} back
    sum[j](A[i, j])                      d_sum[i] → d_A[i, j]

Broadcast omits {i}              →    Reduction collects over {i}
    A[i, j] + bias[j]                   d_bias[j] = sum[i](d_out[i, j])

Permute rearranges {i, j}        →    Permute rearranges back
    y[j, i] = A[i, j]                   d_A[i, j] = d_y[j, i]

Elementwise preserves             →    Elementwise preserves
    y[i, j] = f(x[i, j])                d_x[i, j] = f'(x[i, j]) * d_y[i, j]
\end{verbatim}

Every forward operation has a backward dual. The dual is not a separate
rule. It is the forward rule, read backward, with the coordinate names
as the thread connecting the two directions.

Reduction → Broadcast. Broadcast → Reduction. Permute → Permute.
Elementwise → Elementwise.

The shopping cart model from Chapter 7 is this diagram, narrated as a
story. The forward pass is shopping: you walk through the aisles, items
enter your cart, some are consumed (reduction), some are copied
(broadcast). The backward pass is restocking: the manager reads the
record backward, replenishing what was consumed and collecting what was
copied.

The coordinate names are on both sides of the receipt. The Inversion
Rule is the guarantee that the two sides match.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-audit-as-a-habit}{%
\subsection{The Audit as a Habit}\label{the-audit-as-a-habit}}

The broadcast self-audit is not a tool. It is a habit. You don't run it.
You ask it.

Before you merge a pull request that contains a broadcast, ask the three
questions. Before you write a custom backward pass, trace the Inversion
Rule for every broadcast in the forward pass. Before you debug a
gradient that's the wrong shape, check whether the broadcast set and the
reduction set match.

The questions cost seconds. The bugs they catch cost hours. The
ratio---as the epilogue will remind you---is favorable.

But there is a deeper reason to practice the audit. Every time you ask
``what coordinate am I broadcasting over?'' you are doing something that
positional notation makes difficult and named notation makes easy: you
are connecting the operation to its intent. The broadcast is not just a
shape compatibility check. It is a semantic claim. The audit makes the
claim explicit.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-double-audit-when-broadcasts-compose}{%
\subsection{The Double Audit: When Broadcasts
Compose}\label{the-double-audit-when-broadcasts-compose}}

Most real code has more than one broadcast. A linear layer with bias has
one (bias over batch). A layer normalization has four (mean over
feature, variance over feature, gamma over batch, beta over batch). When
broadcasts compose, their backward reductions compose too. The auditor's
toolkit handles them mechanically---one broadcast at a time---but the
interactions are worth tracing.

Here is a multi-head attention projection followed by a residual
connection:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ attention_block[head, seq_q, seq_k, d](}
\NormalTok{    Q: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    K: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    V: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    W_o: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; head, d, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    b_o: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; d_out}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, seq_q, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scores}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(Q}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * K}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) / (d ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(scores}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ context}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * V}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ projected}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq_q, head, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(context}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * W_o}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, d, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    projected}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq_q, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + b_o}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d_out}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Walk through the auditor's toolkit for the final expression
\texttt{projected\ +\ b\_o}. Output coordinates:
\texttt{\{..batch,\ seq\_q,\ d\_out\}}. \texttt{projected} has
\texttt{\{..batch,\ seq\_q,\ d\_out\}}---no broadcast. \texttt{b\_o} has
\texttt{\{d\_out\}}---broadcasts over \texttt{\{..batch,\ seq\_q\}}.

Now the gradient:
\texttt{d\_b\_o{[}d\_out{]}\ =\ sum{[}..batch,\ seq\_q{]}(d\_out{[}..batch,\ seq\_q,\ d\_out{]})}.
The broadcast set from Step 2 becomes the reduction set in the backward
pass.

But there are hidden broadcasts too. Look at the \texttt{context}
computation: \texttt{sum{[}seq\_k{]}(weights\ *\ V)}. \texttt{V} has
\texttt{\{..batch,\ head,\ seq\_k,\ d\}}. The output \texttt{context}
has \texttt{\{..batch,\ head,\ seq\_q,\ d\}}. Inside the sum,
\texttt{weights{[}..batch,\ head,\ seq\_q,\ seq\_k{]}\ *\ V{[}..batch,\ head,\ seq\_k,\ d{]}}---\texttt{V}
omits \texttt{seq\_q}, so \texttt{V} broadcasts over \texttt{seq\_q}.
Every \texttt{(seq\_q,\ seq\_k)} position within a given
\texttt{(batch,\ head)} receives the same \texttt{V} value from
\texttt{seq\_k}. That broadcast is inside a reduction over
\texttt{seq\_k}. The backward pass:
\texttt{dV{[}..batch,\ head,\ seq\_k,\ d{]}\ =\ sum{[}seq\_q{]}(d\_context{[}..batch,\ head,\ seq\_q,\ d{]}\ *\ weights{[}..batch,\ head,\ seq\_q,\ seq\_k{]})}.
The forward broadcast over \texttt{seq\_q} becomes the backward sum over
\texttt{seq\_q}.

A double audit traces every broadcast in the expression and verifies
that each backward reduction matches. The procedure is the same for one
broadcast or ten. The coordinate sets tell you what to check.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{follow-along-audit-this-block}{%
\subsubsection{Follow Along: Audit This
Block}\label{follow-along-audit-this-block}}

Stop. Take out a pen or open a text file. Here is a smaller but complete
attention block---the projection, not the full attention:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ context}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{    sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * V}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ output}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, seq_q, head, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{    sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(context}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * W_o}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, d, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let} \KeywordTok{final}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, seq_q, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = output}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, seq_q, d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + b_o}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d_out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Apply the auditor's toolkit to this block. For each expression, write:
1. The output coordinate set 2. Each operand's coordinate set 3. The
broadcast set for each operand (output set minus operand set) 4. Whether
each broadcast is semantically justified 5. The backward reduction for
each broadcast

Take five minutes. Do the whole block. Then compare with the answers
below.

\vspace{2em}

Ready? Here is the audit, line by line.

\textbf{Line 1: \texttt{sum{[}seq\_k{]}(weights\ *\ V)}}

\begin{verbatim}
Output coordinates: {..b, head, seq_q, d}
weights: {..b, head, seq_q, seq_k}  → broadcast: {} (no omission, but seq_k is reduced)
V:       {..b, head, seq_k, d}      → broadcast: {seq_q} (V omits seq_q)
\end{verbatim}

\texttt{V} broadcasts over \texttt{seq\_q} inside the reduction. This is
correct: \texttt{V} provides values at each \texttt{seq\_k} position,
and those values are the same regardless of which \texttt{seq\_q} is
querying. The backward reduction:
\texttt{dV{[}..b,\ head,\ seq\_k,\ d{]}\ =\ sum{[}seq\_q{]}(d\_context{[}..b,\ head,\ seq\_q,\ d{]}\ *\ weights{[}..b,\ head,\ seq\_q,\ seq\_k{]})}.
The broadcast set \texttt{\{seq\_q\}} becomes the reduction set.

\textbf{Line 2: \texttt{sum{[}d{]}(context\ *\ W\_o)}}

\begin{verbatim}
Output coordinates: {..b, seq_q, head, d_out}
context: {..b, head, seq_q, d}  → broadcast: {} (no omission, d is reduced)
W_o:     {head, d, d_out}       → broadcast: {..b, seq_q} (W_o omits batch and seq_q)
\end{verbatim}

\texttt{W\_o} broadcasts over \texttt{..b} and \texttt{seq\_q}. This is
correct: the output projection weight is the same for all batch elements
and all query positions. The backward reduction:
\texttt{dW\_o{[}head,\ d,\ d\_out{]}\ =\ sum{[}..b,\ seq\_q{]}(d\_output{[}..b,\ seq\_q,\ head,\ d\_out{]}\ *\ context{[}..b,\ head,\ seq\_q,\ d{]})}.

\textbf{Line 3: \texttt{output\ +\ b\_o}}

\begin{verbatim}
Output coordinates: {..b, seq_q, d_out}
output: {..b, seq_q, d_out}  → broadcast: {}
b_o:    {d_out}              → broadcast: {..b, seq_q}
\end{verbatim}

\texttt{b\_o} broadcasts over \texttt{..b} and \texttt{seq\_q}. Correct:
output bias is the same for all batch elements and query positions.
Backward:
\texttt{db\_o{[}d\_out{]}\ =\ sum{[}..b,\ seq\_q{]}(d\_final{[}..b,\ seq\_q,\ d\_out{]})}.

\textbf{Putting it together.} Three expressions. Five broadcasts (one
hidden inside the first reduction, two explicit in the projections, one
in the bias, one more inside the backward). Every broadcast has a
semantic justification---``this value does not depend on that
coordinate.'' Every broadcast has a corresponding backward reduction
over the same coordinate set. The Inversion Rule holds for all of them.

Now ask yourself: in the PyTorch version of this block, how many of
these five broadcasts would you have noticed? The \texttt{b\_o}
broadcast is obvious---\texttt{projected\ +\ b\_o} with different
shapes. The \texttt{W\_o} broadcast is less
obvious---\texttt{torch.matmul} or \texttt{einsum} hides it. The
\texttt{V} broadcast inside the \texttt{sum{[}seq\_k{]}} reduction is
nearly invisible---it's inside a \texttt{matmul} or \texttt{einsum}
where the broadcasting is implicit. Two of the five broadcasts would
have escaped notice entirely. The audit reveals them.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-audit-without-einlang}{%
\subsection{The Audit Without Einlang}\label{the-audit-without-einlang}}

You do not need Einlang to perform a broadcast self-audit. You need to
know which coordinate is being broadcast over. The question is the same
in any framework. The difference is how hard the framework makes it to
answer.

In PyTorch, broadcasting is shape-driven. \texttt{(32,\ 64)\ +\ (64,)}
broadcasts along axis 0. Which coordinate is axis 0? The code doesn't
say. You infer it from context: axis 0 is probably \texttt{batch}, axis
1 is probably \texttt{feature}. But ``probably'' is not a check.

In Einlang, broadcasting is name-driven.
\texttt{out{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ A{[}i,\ j{]}\ +\ bias{[}j{]}} omits
\texttt{i}. The omitted coordinate is the broadcast coordinate. The code
says it.

The audit questions are the same. But in PyTorch, answering Question 1
(``what coordinate am I broadcasting over?'') requires shape
reconstruction. In Einlang, answering Question 1 requires reading a
bracket. The audit is the same. The effort is not.

Here is the PyTorch version of the attention block final line:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{output }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ projected }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b_o  }\CommentTok{# b_o: (d_out,), projected: (batch, seq_q, d_out)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

What coordinate is \texttt{b\_o} broadcasting over? You must know the
shapes. \texttt{projected} has shape \texttt{(batch,\ seq\_q,\ d\_out)}.
\texttt{b\_o} has shape \texttt{(d\_out,)}. The broadcast aligns to the
right: \texttt{b\_o} is expanded over \texttt{(batch,\ seq\_q)}. Those
are two coordinates. Are they both semantically justified? \texttt{b\_o}
is an output bias---it should not depend on \texttt{batch} or
\texttt{seq\_q}. Yes, justified.

Now change one thing upstream. \texttt{projected} is transposed to
\texttt{(d\_out,\ batch,\ seq\_q)} by a refactoring.
\texttt{output\ =\ projected\ +\ b\_o} still runs. \texttt{b\_o} now
broadcasts over \texttt{(batch,\ seq\_q)}---same set of coordinates, but
the positional alignment is different. The shapes
\texttt{(d\_out,\ batch,\ seq\_q)} and \texttt{(d\_out,)} align on axis
0, broadcasting over axes 1 and 2. The result is correct. But only
because the refactoring preserved \texttt{d\_out} as the first axis. If
it had moved \texttt{d\_out} to the middle, the broadcast would silently
change.

The Einlang version
\texttt{projected{[}..batch,\ seq\_q,\ d\_out{]}\ +\ b\_o{[}d\_out{]}}
does not care where \texttt{d\_out} sits in the positional layout. The
name \texttt{d\_out} identifies the shared axis regardless of position.
The broadcast is over \texttt{\{..batch,\ seq\_q\}}---the coordinates in
the output but not in \texttt{b\_o}. The set subtraction is
position-independent. The audit result is the same regardless of layout.

This is the broadcast self-audit's deepest value: it makes the audit
layout-independent. The questions are about coordinate identities, not
positions. The answers are stable under refactoring. The audit takes the
same effort for a 2D tensor as for a 6D tensor---because the number of
coordinate names, not the number of axes, determines the work.

In the next chapter, we see what happens when coordinate-aware functions
compose into reusable skeletons---patterns that are identical across
normalization, softmax, and attention, differing only in which
coordinates play which roles. And we will discover that the skeleton
itself is a broadcast self-audit, applied at the level of function
signatures.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Stop. Find the last broadcast you wrote---intentionally or not.
It's in your code right now, in some \texttt{A\ +\ b} or
\texttt{scale\ *\ x} or \texttt{mean{[}dim{]}} with
\texttt{keepdim=True}. Apply the three questions. Is the answer to
