Question 2 a confident yes? If not, you have found a claim that deserves
a name.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-audit-your-own-broadcasts}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: Audit Your Own
Broadcasts}\label{stop-and-think-audit-your-own-broadcasts}}

Open your most recent project. Search for patterns where broadcasting
happens implicitly: \texttt{+}, \texttt{*}, \texttt{/}, \texttt{-}
between tensors of different ranks. Find three examples. For each one,
perform the broadcast self-audit:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Write down the coordinate sets.} What are the output
  coordinates? What are each operand's coordinates? You may need to run
  the code or check \texttt{.shape} to determine ranks. Write them down.
\item
  \textbf{Compute the broadcast set.} For each operand, subtract its
  coordinate set from the output set. The difference is what that
  operand broadcasts over. Write it down.
\item
  \textbf{Name the broadcast coordinates.} For each broadcast set, can
  you name the coordinate? Not ``axis 0'' or ``the first
  dimension''---the coordinate name. Is it \texttt{batch}?
  \texttt{channel}? \texttt{height}? \texttt{width}? If you can't name
  it, you've found a broadcast whose identity is untethered from any
  declaration.
\item
  \textbf{Ask: is it justified?} Does the broadcasting operand genuinely
  not depend on those coordinates? If the answer is ``probably'' or ``I
  think so'' rather than ``yes, by construction''---you've found a
  broadcast that deserves a second look.
\end{enumerate}

This exercise takes five minutes. It will find at least one broadcast in
your code where the answer to question 4 is not a confident ``yes.''
That broadcast is a bug waiting for the right input shape.

The broadcast self-audit is not a tool. It is a reading habit. Every
broadcast is a claim. The claim is written in the brackets---or, in a
positional framework, inferred from the shapes. When the claim is
explicit, you can audit it. When it's implicit, you can only hope it's
correct. The difference between audit and hope is the difference between
a bug caught at review time and a bug caught at 3 AM.

The next chapter introduces the skeleton---the shared structure beneath
normalization, softmax, and every operation that follows a
reduce-broadcast-reduce pattern. The broadcast self-audit is the tool
for reading that structure. The skeleton is what it reveals.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-5-blocks-and-skeletons}{%
\section{Chapter 5 · Blocks and
Skeletons}\label{chapter-5-blocks-and-skeletons}}

\begin{quote}
``If you have a procedure with ten parameters, you probably missed
some.''

--- Alan Perlis
\end{quote}

\emph{Combinations · Pack polymorphism and normalization skeletons}

\vspace{2em}

Primitives let you say simple things. Combinations let you compose
simple things into complex ones. Abstraction lets you name the complex
things and use them as if they were primitive.

Chapter 3 showed how to wrap a coordinate pattern in a function
signature. This chapter shows what happens when you have several such
functions---and realize they are all the same skeleton, dressed in
different coordinate names.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-anatomy-of-a-coordinate-aware-function}{%
\subsection{The Anatomy of a Coordinate-Aware
Function}\label{the-anatomy-of-a-coordinate-aware-function}}

Here is the complete form:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ normalize[coord](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, coord, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, coord, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = max}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{coord}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, coord, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, coord, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, coord, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scale}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{coord}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, coord, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{2.0}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, coord, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / (scale}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..left, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Four things to notice.

\textbf{First, the coordinate parameter \texttt{coord}.} It appears in
brackets after the function name and in the type signatures. It is not a
value---you cannot do arithmetic on it. It is a coordinate identity.
When the function body uses \texttt{max{[}coord{]}} and
\texttt{sum{[}coord{]}}, it is verified that \texttt{coord} is the same
parameter declared in the signature.

\textbf{Second, the rest packs \texttt{..left} and \texttt{..right}.}
They stand for whatever coordinates surround \texttt{coord} in the
actual argument. If the caller passes \texttt{x{[}b,\ t,\ f{]}} and
writes \texttt{normalize{[}f{]}(x)}, then \texttt{..left} binds to
\texttt{{[}b,\ t{]}} and \texttt{..right} binds to nothing. If the
caller passes \texttt{x{[}b,\ h,\ f,\ d{]}} with
\texttt{normalize{[}f{]}(x)}, then \texttt{..left} binds to
\texttt{{[}b,\ h{]}} and \texttt{..right} binds to \texttt{{[}d{]}}. The
function body is polymorphic over the surrounding structure.

\textbf{Third, coordinate flow.} Inside the function body, the
coordinate \texttt{coord} is in scope. It can be used in reductions
(\texttt{max{[}coord{]}}, \texttt{sum{[}coord{]}}), in indexing, and
implicitly in the output shape. The packs \texttt{..left} and
\texttt{..right} flow from the input signature to the output signature.

\textbf{Fourth, the return type annotation.} The
\texttt{-\textgreater{}\ {[}f32;\ ..left,\ coord,\ ..right{]}} tells the
reader which coordinates survive. If the function body accidentally
consumed \texttt{coord} without reconstructing it, or dropped a pack, a
coordinate mismatch is reported.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{packs-and-polymorphism}{%
\subsection{Packs and Polymorphism}\label{packs-and-polymorphism}}

Packs are what make coordinate-aware functions reusable across different
tensor ranks. There are three patterns:

\textbf{Leading packs} (\texttt{..left} or \texttt{..batch}): absorb
dimensions that come before the coordinate of interest. Used when the
function treats all leading dimensions uniformly.

\textbf{Trailing packs} (\texttt{..right} or \texttt{..rest}): absorb
dimensions that come after. Used when the function operates on a
coordinate and doesn't care what follows.

\textbf{Named spatial packs} (\texttt{..spatial}): absorb spatial
dimensions as a group. A function that reshapes spatial coordinates can
treat them as a unit:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ move_channel[channel, ..spatial](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; channel, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..spatial, channel}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..spatial, channel}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When a pack is ambiguous at the call site, the caller disambiguates by
grouping:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{id_axes}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{(height, width)}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Pack parameters make coordinate-aware functions rank-polymorphic: the
same function works on 2D, 3D, 4D, or higher-dimensional tensors, as
long as the coordinate of interest exists somewhere in the shape.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{selection-reductions}{%
\subsection{Selection Reductions}\label{selection-reductions}}

A normal reduction returns a combined value. A selection reduction
returns an address:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ pred}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = argmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{max{[}class{]}(logits{[}b,\ class{]})} returns the maximum
\emph{value} for each \texttt{b}.
\texttt{argmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}b,\ class{]})} returns the index
within the \texttt{class} domain where that maximum occurs. The result
has the surviving coordinates---\texttt{{[}b{]}}---plus an implicit
address domain: the integer stored at each \texttt{b} is understood to
be an address in the \texttt{class} coordinate space.

This is more than a convenience. The return value of
\texttt{argmax{[}class{]}} is not just an integer tensor. It is an
integer tensor whose values are addresses in the \texttt{class} domain.
Later code can use this address to index back into the \texttt{class}
dimension of another tensor.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{one-skeleton-four-normalizations}{%
\subsection{One Skeleton, Four
Normalizations}\label{one-skeleton-four-normalizations}}

Before we see the skeleton table, let's discover it ourselves. Here are
four PyTorch normalization implementations. Read them. Find what they
share.

\textbf{LayerNorm:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ layer_norm(x, gamma, beta, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ((x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (var }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps).sqrt() }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ gamma }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{RMSNorm:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ rms_norm(x, gamma, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    rms }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{).sqrt()}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (rms }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ gamma}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{GroupNorm:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ group_norm(x, num_groups, gamma, beta, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    N, C, H, W }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, num_groups, C }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num_groups, H, W)}
\NormalTok{    mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.var(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (var }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps).sqrt()}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, C, H, W) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ gamma }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{InstanceNorm:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ instance_norm(x, gamma, beta, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    N, C, H, W }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{    mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.var(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (var }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps).sqrt() }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ gamma }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Stop. Take three minutes. Find the common structure. Don't just say
``they all normalize''---that's too broad. What specific sequence of
operations do they all perform? What varies between them? Write it down.

\vspace{2em}

Done? Here is what they share:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Compute a statistic} (mean, rms, or both) by reducing over one
  or more dimensions.
\item
  \textbf{Broadcast the statistic back} over the reduced dimensions (via
  \texttt{keepdim=True}).
\item
  \textbf{Apply the statistic elementwise} (subtract and divide).
\item
  \textbf{Scale and shift} with learned parameters that broadcast over
  the non-feature dimensions.
\end{enumerate}

Every one of these functions does: reduce, broadcast, elementwise,
scale. The difference is only \emph{which} dimensions are reduced and
\emph{which} parameters broadcast over \emph{which} remaining
dimensions.

But look at the code again. Can you \emph{see} the skeleton? In
LayerNorm, it's \texttt{x.mean(dim=-1,\ keepdim=True)}. In GroupNorm,
it's \texttt{x.mean(dim=(2,3,4),\ keepdim=True)}. In InstanceNorm, it's
\texttt{x.mean(dim=(2,3),\ keepdim=True)}. The \texttt{dim} arguments
are different integers. The \texttt{keepdim=True} flag is the same. The
\texttt{*\ gamma\ +\ beta} ending is the same.

The skeleton IS there---but it's encoded as shape arithmetic.
\texttt{dim=-1} means one thing in LayerNorm (``the last dimension'')
and a completely different set of integers in GroupNorm (``dimensions 2,
3, and 4''). The skeleton is visible to a human who understands the
dimension layout. It is invisible to a compiler. And it changes when the
layout changes.

Now here is the same skeleton in Einlang:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
\begin{minipage}[b]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
Function\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
Reduction coords\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
Broadcast params\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
Survivors\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\midrule
\endhead
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
Softmax\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{q} (max), \texttt{k} (sum)\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
none\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{..batch}, \texttt{j}\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
LayerNorm\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{f} (mean ×2)\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{gamma{[}f{]}}, \texttt{beta{[}f{]}}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{..batch}, \texttt{f}\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
RMSNorm\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{f} (mean)\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{gamma{[}f{]}}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{..batch}, \texttt{f}\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
GroupNorm\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{c\_in\_group}, \texttt{..spatial}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{gamma{[}g,\ c\_in\_group{]}},
\texttt{beta{[}g,\ c\_in\_group{]}}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{..batch}, \texttt{g}, \texttt{c\_in\_group},
\texttt{..spatial}\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
InstanceNorm\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{..spatial}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{gamma{[}c{]}}, \texttt{beta{[}c{]}}\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.22\columnwidth}\raggedright
\texttt{..batch}, \texttt{c}, \texttt{..spatial}\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

The skeleton is visible in the table because the coordinates are named.
Each column says \emph{what}, not \emph{where}. The reduction column
names the consumed coordinates. The broadcast column names the
parameters and their coordinate sets. The survivors column names what's
left.

In a positional API, all four collapse to a single \texttt{dim} argument
whose meaning shifts with the surrounding layout. \texttt{LayerNorm} and
\texttt{RMSNorm} both use \texttt{dim=-1}---but normalize different
statistics. \texttt{GroupNorm} uses three reduction dimensions buried in
a \texttt{reshape} chain. The skeleton is invisible.

In a named-coordinate API, the skeleton is a template you can check. The
reduction bracket names the consumed coordinates. The indexing pattern
names the survivors. The broadcast parameters name the omission. A
reviewer can verify that the broadcast coordinate in LayerNorm matches
the broadcast coordinate in the gradient without reconstructing both
from positional offsets.

This is abstraction: recognizing a pattern, naming it, and reusing it.
The pattern is ``normalize with named coordinates.'' Each instance fills
in the specific coordinates. The skeleton is constant.

The discovery exercise you just did---comparing four implementations,
finding their shared structure---is what you do every time you read
unfamiliar tensor code. In a positional API, the discovery requires
shape reconstruction. In a named API, the discovery requires reading
brackets. The cognitive difference between the two is the difference
between ``let me trace the shapes'' and ``let me read the names.'' Names
carry the structure. Positions hide it.

Now let's put this claim to the test. Here is a real GroupNorm
implementation in PyTorch:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ group_norm(x, num_groups, gamma, beta, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    N, C, H, W }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, num_groups, C }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num_groups, H, W)}
\NormalTok{    mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.var(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (var }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps).sqrt()}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, C, H, W)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ gamma }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Stop and read this carefully. Ask yourself: which dimensions are being
reduced by \texttt{dim=(2,\ 3,\ 4)}? What do positions 2, 3, and 4
correspond to? You need to trace backward through the
\texttt{reshape}---position 2 is \texttt{C\ //\ num\_groups} (channels
per group), position 3 is \texttt{H}, position 4 is \texttt{W}. But this
reasoning depends on the reshape chain. If the reshape changes, the
\texttt{dim} tuple must change with it. If someone adds a temporal
dimension before the spatial ones, the tuple shifts silently.

Now compare the Einlang version:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ group_norm[g, c_in_group, ..spatial](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; g, c_in_group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; g, c_in_group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ v}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        (x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) ** }\DecValTok{2.0}
\NormalTok{    );}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ y}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        (x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        / (v}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{) ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{;}
\NormalTok{    y}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{g, c_in_group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{g, c_in_group}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The reduced coordinates are named: \texttt{c\_in\_group} and
\texttt{..spatial}. No reshape needed. No positional arithmetic needed.
If a temporal dimension is added, \texttt{..spatial} absorbs it---the
reduction bracket stays the same. If \texttt{num\_groups} changes, the
coordinate \texttt{g} handles it---its domain just has a different size.

Here is the deeper point. In a positional API, ``feature'' is the last
axis in a 2D tensor---\texttt{dim=-1}. But in a 4D tensor, what was one
axis may span multiple actual dimensions: channels per group, height,
width. Positional code requires a reshape-permute-reshape dance to group
and ungroup those. Named coordinates handle this naturally: one semantic
coordinate (\texttt{c\_in\_group}) plus a spatial pack
(\texttt{..spatial}) together cover what \texttt{dim=(2,3,4)} covers in
the positional version. The names don't change when the layout changes.

This is what packs buy you. \texttt{..spatial} absorbs however many
spatial dimensions exist. The same \texttt{GroupNorm} skeleton works
whether spatial covers one axis or three.
\texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}} says exactly what is
consumed---no reshape chain to reverse-engineer.

Now one more question. Suppose you encounter a new normalization
variant---say, normalize only over the spatial dimensions, keeping the
channel-group dimension intact. What would you change?

Think about it. In the Einlang version, you change one thing: remove
\texttt{c\_in\_group} from the reduction bracket.
\texttt{mean{[}..spatial{]}(...)} instead of
\texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}(...)}. The skeleton is
unchanged. The coordinate names carry the design decision.

In the PyTorch version, you'd change \texttt{dim=(2,\ 3,\ 4)} to
\texttt{dim=(3,\ 4)}---but only if the reshape hasn't changed the
position of the spatial dimensions. If someone added a temporal axis
between \texttt{c\_in\_group} and \texttt{H}, the tuple would need to
shift to \texttt{dim=(4,\ 5)}. The fragility is not in the concept---it
is in the notation's inability to record \emph{which} dimensions are
spatial.

You have already been thinking in terms of the skeleton. The
four-normalizations table at the beginning of this section was not an
abstract taxonomy---it was a description of the mental model you were
already using. Named coordinates make that mental model executable.

\vspace{2em}

The normalization skeleton table reveals something deeper about named
coordinates. In a positional API, ``feature'' is the last axis in a 2D
tensor---but in a 4D tensor, what was one axis may now span multiple
actual dimensions. Named coordinates handle this naturally: one semantic
coordinate (\texttt{feature}) can correspond to one or more actual axes
in the tensor, depending on the layout. Positional code requires a
reshape-permute-reshape dance to group and ungroup those dimensions.
Named code just names them.

This is what packs buy you. \texttt{..spatial} absorbs however many
spatial dimensions there are. The same \texttt{GroupNorm} skeleton works
whether spatial covers one axis or three.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{skeletons-compose}{%
\subsection{Skeletons Compose}\label{skeletons-compose}}

The normalization skeleton and the attention skeleton compose. A
Transformer block is LayerNorm, then attention, then another LayerNorm,
then a feedforward. In a positional implementation, the norm dimensions
and attention dimensions share the \texttt{dim=-1} convention---until
one of them shouldn't.

Here is a complete Transformer block skeleton in Einlang:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ transformer_block[head, seq, d, d_ff](}
\NormalTok{    x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    W_q: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; head, d, d_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    W_k: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; head, d, d_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    W_v: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; head, d, d_v}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    W_o: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; head, d_v, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    W_1: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; d, d_ff}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    W_2: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; d_ff, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    gamma1: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta1: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    gamma2: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta2: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; d}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \CommentTok{// LayerNorm 1}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ norm1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = layer_norm}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, gamma1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \CommentTok{// Multi-head attention}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ attn_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = attention}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, seq, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        norm1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, norm1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, norm1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{        W_q}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, d, d_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, W_k}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, d, d_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, W_v}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, d, d_v}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, W_o}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, d_v, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{    );}
    
    \CommentTok{// Residual connection}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ res1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + attn_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    
    \CommentTok{// LayerNorm 2}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ norm2}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = layer_norm}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(res1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, gamma2}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta2}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \CommentTok{// Feedforward}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ ff}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d_ff}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = relu(sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(norm2}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * W_1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d, d_ff}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{));}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ ff_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d_ff}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(ff}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d_ff}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * W_2}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d_ff, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    
    \CommentTok{// Residual connection}
\NormalTok{    res1}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + ff_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Every coordinate that is consumed is named in a bracket. Every
coordinate that survives is named in the output pattern. The \texttt{d}
coordinate is consumed in two reductions (\texttt{layer\_norm{[}d{]}}
and \texttt{attention{[}...,\ d{]}}) and reconstructed each time. The
\texttt{head} coordinate appears on the attention weights but not on the
input \texttt{x}---it splits the feature dimension without changing the
data layout.

Now ask: if you wanted to change this to a cross-attention block where
queries come from one sequence and keys/values from another, what would
you change? In a positional implementation, the code wouldn't change at
all---the same \texttt{attention(Q,\ K,\ V)} call works for both. The
difference is only in which tensors you pass. In the Einlang version,
you'd change the signature: the first \texttt{norm1} gets coordinate
\texttt{seq\_q}, the second and third get coordinate \texttt{seq\_k}.
The code change is a coordinate name swap. The reader sees the
architectural decision in the type signature.

Skeletons compose because coordinate contracts compose. The output
coordinates of one function become the input coordinates of the next.
The compiler traces the flow. You trace the meaning.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{derive-instancenorm}{%
\subsection{Derive InstanceNorm}\label{derive-instancenorm}}

You've seen the table. Now derive one entry yourself. InstanceNorm
normalizes each sample's each channel independently over the spatial
dimensions. In 2D: for each \texttt{(N,\ C)}, compute mean and variance
over \texttt{(H,\ W)}.

Take two minutes. Write the Einlang signature for InstanceNorm. What
coordinates does it reduce over? What coordinates survive? What
parameters broadcast?

Hint: use \texttt{..spatial} for the spatial dimensions and \texttt{c}
for channel.

\vspace{2em}

Done? Compare:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ instance_norm[c, ..spatial](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, c, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                                gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                                beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, c, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ v}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{((x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) ** }\DecValTok{2.0}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ y}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        (x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - m}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) / (v}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{) ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{;}
\NormalTok{    y}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, c, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The reduced coordinates are \texttt{..spatial}. The surviving
coordinates are \texttt{..batch}, \texttt{c}, and
\texttt{..spatial}---the spatial coordinates are consumed for the
statistics but preserved in the output (the output still has spatial
dimensions). The broadcast parameters are \texttt{gamma{[}c{]}} and
\texttt{beta{[}c{]}}, which broadcast over \texttt{..batch} and
\texttt{..spatial}.

Now compare this to what you wrote. Did you: - Use \texttt{..spatial}
for the spatial dimensions? (It absorbs however many there are.) - Place
\texttt{..spatial} in the reduction bracket? (It's consumed for the
statistics.) - Keep \texttt{..spatial} in the return type? (The output
is not a scalar---it's a tensor with spatial dimensions.)

If you got all three, you understand the skeleton. The coordinate names
carried the design: \texttt{mean{[}..spatial{]}} says ``I am consuming
the spatial dimensions.'' The return type
\texttt{{[}f32;\ ..batch,\ c,\ ..spatial{]}} says ``the spatial
dimensions survive.'' The contradiction resolves: \texttt{..spatial} is
consumed in the reduction but reconstructed in the output---the
signature guarantees it.

Now consider: what if InstanceNorm should normalize over \texttt{c} as
well? You'd change the reduction bracket to
\texttt{{[}c,\ ..spatial{]}}. One change. The skeleton is the same. The
coordinate name carries the design decision.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{spot-the-skeleton}{%
\subsection{Spot the Skeleton}\label{spot-the-skeleton}}

Here are four Einlang function signatures. Three implement normalization
variants. One doesn't. Can you spot the odd one out?

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ A[j](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, j}\OperatorTok{]}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ B[coord](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, coord}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b}\OperatorTok{]}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ C[f](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, f}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, f}\OperatorTok{]}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ D[t](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, t}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, t}\OperatorTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Stop. Don't read the answer. Look at the return types.

Done? Function B is the odd one out. Its return type is
\texttt{{[}f32;\ ..b{]}}---the coordinate \texttt{coord} is missing. It
was consumed and not reconstructed. Functions A, C, and D all return
\texttt{{[}f32;\ ..b,\ \textless{}coordinate\textgreater{}{]}}---the
coordinate survives. B is a reduction function (like
\texttt{sum{[}coord{]}}). A, C, and D are normalization functions that
preserve the coordinate.

Now the deeper question: \textbf{why} is this distinction visible in the
type signature? Because the skeleton is more than ``reduce then
broadcast.'' The skeleton is ``reduce, then broadcast back to
\textbf{reconstruct the consumed coordinate in the output.}'' A pure
reduction consumes and doesn't reconstruct---the coordinate disappears
from the return type. A normalization consumes and reconstructs---the
coordinate reappears. The difference between ``gone forever'' and ``gone
and returned'' is the difference between a reduction and a
normalization. The return type records it.

The skeleton is visible in the type signature. The reduction bracket in
the body (\texttt{max{[}coord{]}}, \texttt{mean{[}f{]}},
\texttt{sum{[}j{]}}) tells you what is consumed. The return type tells
you whether it was reconstructed. A reader can distinguish a
normalization from a reduction without reading the body---the coordinate
flow is in the signature.

This is the abstraction layer. The function signature says: ``I operate
on coordinate \texttt{j}. I preserve \texttt{j} in the output.
Everything else passes through.'' The body fills in the specific
computation. The signature is the contract. The body is the
implementation. And the contract is checkable.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{coordinate-facts-flow}{%
\subsection{Coordinate Facts Flow}\label{coordinate-facts-flow}}

Coordinate facts flow through pointwise operations automatically. If
\texttt{x} carries the fact that it has coordinates
\texttt{{[}b,\ class{]}}, then \texttt{x\ **\ 2.0} also has coordinates
\texttt{{[}b,\ class{]}}. You don't need to re-declare them. Coordinate
facts are preserved through arithmetic, through function calls that
return tensors, through \texttt{if} expressions. The only operations
that change coordinate facts are those that explicitly manipulate
coordinates: reductions (which consume), rectangular declarations (which
introduce), and coordinate-aware function calls (which thread them
through signatures).

This flow is the foundation of abstraction. When you wrap a computation
in a coordinate-aware function, the coordinate facts flow from the
caller, through the function body, and out to the result. The function
doesn't need to re-derive what the caller already knew. The facts
propagate.

This is a stronger property than type inference. Type inference deduces
that \texttt{x\ **\ 2.0} has the same type as \texttt{x} (both
\texttt{f32}). Coordinate flow deduces that \texttt{x\ **\ 2.0} has the
same coordinate structure as \texttt{x} (both \texttt{{[}b,\ class{]}}).
The coordinate structure is not inferred from runtime shapes---it is
propagated from declarations. If \texttt{x} is declared as
\texttt{x{[}b,\ class{]}}, every expression built from \texttt{x}
carries \texttt{(b,\ class)} unless an operation explicitly removes a
coordinate.

The difference between type inference and coordinate flow is that type
inference is standard in every typed language, while coordinate flow is
absent from every major tensor framework. A PyTorch tensor carries shape
information at runtime---\texttt{(32,\ 64)}---but no coordinate
identities. The identities are lost the moment the tensor leaves the
data loader. In Einlang, the identities propagate through every
operation, every function call, every intermediate binding. They are
never inferred from shapes. They are propagated from declarations. The
source is always the declaration. The flow is always forward.

Now check your understanding. What happens to coordinate facts in these
three cases?

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{let\ y\ =\ x\ +\ 1.0;} --- coordinates flow through:
  \texttt{y} has the same coordinates as \texttt{x}.
\item
  \texttt{let\ y\ =\ sum{[}j{]}(x{[}i,\ j{]});} --- coordinate
  \texttt{j} is consumed: \texttt{y} has \texttt{{[}i{]}}.
\item
  \texttt{let\ y{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ x{[}j,\ i{]};} --- coordinates are
  rearranged: \texttt{y} has \texttt{{[}i,\ j{]}} but from
  \texttt{x{[}j,\ i{]}}.
\end{enumerate}

The third case is worth pausing on.
\texttt{y{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ x{[}j,\ i{]}} is a transpose. The declaration
bracket says \texttt{y} has coordinates \texttt{(i,\ j)}. The body
references \texttt{x{[}j,\ i{]}}---the same names, swapped positions.
\texttt{i} and \texttt{j} are preserved. Their order is changed. This is
the coordinate-aware way to write a transpose: not
\texttt{x.transpose(0,\ 1)}, but \texttt{y{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ x{[}j,\ i{]}}.
The names carry the permutation. No position counting needed.

\vspace{2em}

In the next chapter, we add a fourth role to our coordinates: time. A
coordinate that doesn't just sit there, but flows.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-skeleton-discovery-exercise}{%
\subsection{The Skeleton Discovery
Exercise}\label{the-skeleton-discovery-exercise}}

The four-normalizations table revealed that LayerNorm, RMSNorm,
GroupNorm, and InstanceNorm share a skeleton. But how do you discover
the skeleton in the first place? Not by reading a table. By writing the
functions and noticing what changes.

Take the following exercise. Write the softmax skeleton as a
coordinate-aware function using only the signature and the reduction
brackets---no body, no computation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ softmax[j](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ layer_norm[f](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, f}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; f}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; f}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, f}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ instance_norm[..s, c](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, c, ..s}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; c}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, c, ..s}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Now look at only the signatures. Can you tell which one reduces over
which coordinate? In \texttt{softmax{[}j{]}}, the coordinate parameter
\texttt{j} tells you: normalize over \texttt{j}. In
\texttt{layer\_norm{[}f{]}}, \texttt{f} tells you: normalize over
\texttt{f}. In \texttt{instance\_norm{[}..s,\ c{]}}, both \texttt{..s}
and \texttt{c} are in the signature---but which one is reduced? The
answer depends on which coordinate is placed in the reduction bracket in
the body. The signature alone can't tell you---it can only tell you
which coordinates exist. The body tells you which ones are consumed.

Now compare to the positional versions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ softmax(logits, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{): ...}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ layer_norm(x, normalized_shape, ...): ...}
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ instance_norm(x, ...): ...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The positional signatures tell you even less. \texttt{softmax} has
\texttt{dim}---a positional hint. \texttt{layer\_norm} has
\texttt{normalized\_shape}---a shape hint, not a coordinate hint.
\texttt{instance\_norm} has no hint at all---you must read the body to
know which dimensions are reduced.

The skeleton discovery exercise trains you to look at a function
signature and ask: which coordinates are needed for this computation?
The ones in the brackets are the answer. The ones not in the brackets
pass through. The signature is the skeleton's outline.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{The next time you write a function that takes a tensor and returns
a tensor, write its coordinate signature in a comment before the body.
Which coordinates survive? Which are consumed? Which broadcast? If you
can't answer from the code alone, the signature is the place to start.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-find-the-skeletons-in-your-code}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: Find the Skeletons in Your
Code}\label{stop-and-think-find-the-skeletons-in-your-code}}

You've seen the skeleton in LayerNorm, RMSNorm, GroupNorm, and
InstanceNorm. But skeletons aren't limited to normalization. Every
operation that follows a reduce-broadcast-elementwise pattern is a
skeleton. Go find them in your code.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Search for \texttt{mean(}, \texttt{sum(}, \texttt{max(}
  followed by \texttt{keepdim=True}.} Each of these is a
  reduction-statistic-broadcast pattern. For each one, ask: which
  coordinate is reduced? Which coordinate is the statistic broadcast
  over? If the \texttt{dim} argument is an integer, can you name the
  coordinate? If the answer is ``it's whatever dimension is at that
  position,'' you've found a skeleton whose identity depends on layout.
\item
  \textbf{Search for \texttt{*\ gamma\ +\ beta} or
  \texttt{*\ scale\ +\ shift}.} These are the broadcast-parameter
  suffixes of normalization skeletons. For each one, ask: which
  coordinates do \texttt{gamma} and \texttt{beta} broadcast over? Can
  you name them? If not, the broadcast is implicit.
\item
  \textbf{Find two functions in your code that you suspect share a
  skeleton.} They might both normalize something, or both pool
  something, or both compute some statistic. Write their Einlang
  signatures side by side---even if you're not using Einlang, just write
  \texttt{fn\ name{[}consumed{]}(x:\ {[}..batch,\ consumed{]})\ -\textgreater{}\ {[}..batch,\ consumed{]}}
  as a comment. Do they share the same skeleton? If yes, you've found a
  pattern. If no, they serve different purposes and should have
  different signatures.
\item
  \textbf{Design a new normalization variant.} What if you wanted to
  normalize over the batch dimension instead of the feature dimension?
  In a positional API, you'd change \texttt{dim=-1} to
  \texttt{dim=0}---and hope no other code depends on the output shape
  being \texttt{(...,\ feature)}. In Einlang, you'd change
  \texttt{layer\_norm{[}f{]}} to \texttt{layer\_norm{[}batch{]}}. The
  signature change documents the architectural decision. Design your
  variant's Einlang signature, then ask: would a colleague reading the
  signature understand what is normalized over?
\end{enumerate}

The skeleton is the bridge between Chapter 2's primitives and Chapter
7's gradients. Every skeleton's forward pass is a
reduce-broadcast-elementwise pattern. Every skeleton's backward pass is
the Inversion Rule applied to that pattern. When you see the skeleton
forward, you can predict its backward. The coordinate names are the
thread connecting the two directions.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-6-names-in-time}{%
\section{Chapter 6 · Names in Time}\label{chapter-6-names-in-time}}

\begin{quote}
``Time is what keeps everything from happening at once.''

--- Attributed to John Archibald Wheeler
\end{quote}

\emph{Combinations · Recurrence and causality}

\vspace{2em}

Every coordinate we have met so far is a spatial coordinate. You can sum
over it, broadcast along it, permute it. All positions along the
coordinate exist simultaneously. No position depends on any other.

Time is different. Time has a direction.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{not-all-axes-are-the-same}{%
\subsection{Not All Axes Are the Same}\label{not-all-axes-are-the-same}}

Look at this declaration:

\begin{verbatim}
u[t, i] = u[t-1, i] + f(u[t-1, i])
\end{verbatim}

\texttt{t} and \texttt{i} are both written in brackets. But \texttt{t}
does something no spatial coordinate does---it looks backward.
\texttt{t-1}.

In a spatial expression like \texttt{sum{[}i{]}(A{[}i,\ k{]})},
\texttt{i} is just an index. You never write \texttt{i-1}. Spatial
indices are concurrent---you sum over them, reduce along them, permute
them---but you don't \emph{recur} along them.

\texttt{t} is different. \texttt{t-1} means step one depends on step
zero, step two depends on step one. \texttt{t} is the direction of
recurrence. Not space. Time.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{recurrence-declarations}{%
\subsection{Recurrence Declarations}\label{recurrence-declarations}}

In Einlang, time is just another coordinate---but one that appears in
index arithmetic. You declare it with a range:

\begin{verbatim}
let u[t in 0..T, i] = init_temp(i);
let u[t in 1..T, i] = u[t-1, i] + alpha * (
    u[t-1, i+1] - 2.0 * u[t-1, i] + u[t-1, i-1]
);
\end{verbatim}

The first clause defines \texttt{u} at \texttt{t=0}---the initial
condition. The second clause defines \texttt{u} at every subsequent time
step in terms of the previous step. \texttt{t-1} is a backward
reference: the value at time \texttt{t} depends on the value at time
\texttt{t-1}.

This is a recurrence. The coordinate \texttt{t} carries time's
directional structure into the notation. You cannot write
\texttt{u{[}t+1,\ i{]}} to define \texttt{u{[}t,\ i{]}}---that would be
a forward reference, and it is rejected as a static error. Causality is
not a comment. It is a syntactic constraint. If the index expression
references an index greater than or equal to the declared index, it is a
static error. This is not philosophy. It is subtraction:
\texttt{t-1\ \textless{}\ t}, valid; \texttt{t+1\ \textgreater{}\ t},
rejected.

The declaration bracket names \emph{what} is being defined. The body
says \emph{how}. The separation keeps the declaration side simple and
declarative, while the body can use arbitrary index arithmetic:

\begin{verbatim}
let fib[0] = 0;
let fib[1] = 1;
let fib[n in 2..8] = fib[n-1] + fib[n-2];
\end{verbatim}

The recurrence index range \texttt{n\ in\ 2..8} goes in the declaration
bracket---it defines the domain. The expressions \texttt{n-1} and
\texttt{n-2} go in the body---they compute the value by referencing
earlier elements. Every reference must point strictly backward.

Now look at this line:

\begin{verbatim}
let h[t in 0..T] = step(h[t+1], x[t])
\end{verbatim}

If someone wrote this, what should happen? Stop and derive the rule
yourself. The declaration says \texttt{t\ in\ 0..T}---the statement
defines \texttt{h} at time \texttt{t}. The body references
\texttt{h{[}t+1{]}}---a value at time \texttt{t+1}. At the moment
\texttt{h{[}t{]}} is being computed, \texttt{h{[}t+1{]}} has not been
computed yet. \texttt{t+1} is strictly greater than \texttt{t}. The
rule: \textbf{every index reference to the declared variable must be
strictly less than the declared index.} \texttt{t+1\ \textless{}\ t} is
false. Error.

The check does not need to know that \texttt{t} is ``time.'' It does not
need to know what ``causality'' means. It does exactly one thing:
compare the reference index against the declared index, for every
reference to the declared variable in the body. Reference index
\texttt{\textless{}} declared index? Valid. Otherwise? Rejected. The
coordinate can be called \texttt{t}, \texttt{x}, or
\texttt{spatial\_index}---the check is the same. Causality is not a
name-declared property. It is subtraction.

This has a consequence that spatial coordinates don't require. Only the
time steps that are actually referenced backward need to be kept. If
every step references only \texttt{t-1}, the storage needed is a rolling
window of size 2, regardless of whether \texttt{T} is 100 or 100,000.
This follows mechanically from the backward references---no annotation
needed.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{judge-the-recurrence-an-exercise}{%
\subsubsection{Judge the Recurrence: An
Exercise}\label{judge-the-recurrence-an-exercise}}

Here are four recurrence fragments. For each one, decide: does it pass
the causality check? Take two minutes.

\textbf{Fragment A:}

\begin{verbatim}
let u[t in 0..T, i] = u[t-1, i] + u[t-2, i];
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Fragment B:}

\begin{verbatim}
let h[t in 0..T, i] = h[t-1, i] + h[t, i-1];
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Fragment C:}

\begin{verbatim}
let v[t in 0..T] = v[t+1] + v[t-1];
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Fragment D:}

\begin{verbatim}
let x[t in 0..T] = f(x[t-1]) + g(x[t]);
\end{verbatim}

Stop. Write your answers. Then read on.

\vspace{2em}

Done? Here are the rulings:

\textbf{Fragment A: passes.} \texttt{t-1\ \textless{}\ t} and
\texttt{t-2\ \textless{}\ t}. Both references are strictly backward.
Window size: 2 (references \texttt{t-1} and \texttt{t-2}).

\textbf{Fragment B: passes.} \texttt{t-1\ \textless{}\ t} (time
reference, backward). \texttt{i-1\ \textless{}\ i}? Wait---\texttt{i} is
the declared coordinate, but \texttt{i} is a spatial coordinate, not a
time coordinate. The body references \texttt{h{[}t,\ i-1{]}}---a value
at the same time step \texttt{t} but an earlier spatial position
\texttt{i-1}. Is this allowed?

Yes. The causality check applies to the recurrence coordinate
\texttt{t}, not to spatial coordinates. \texttt{i-1} is a spatial
offset, not a time offset. The rule is: \textbf{references to the
declared variable must have the recurrence coordinate strictly less than
the declared recurrence coordinate.} \texttt{t-1\ \textless{}\ t} is
true. \texttt{i-1} isn't checked because \texttt{i} isn't the recurrence
coordinate. Fragment B passes.

But notice: \texttt{h{[}t,\ i-1{]}} means the computation at position
\texttt{i} depends on position \texttt{i-1} at the same time \texttt{t}.
If the spatial iteration goes left to right, this is fine---\texttt{i-1}
is already computed. If the spatial iteration goes right to left,
\texttt{i-1} isn't computed yet. The compiler doesn't check spatial
causality by default because spatial coordinates don't have a declared
direction. If you want spatial causality, you declare the spatial
coordinate as directional too.

\textbf{Fragment C: REJECTED.} \texttt{t+1\ \textgreater{}\ t}. This is
a forward reference on the time coordinate. The body references a value
at \texttt{t+1}, which hasn't been computed yet (assuming forward
iteration). Error.

\textbf{Fragment D: REJECTED.} \texttt{x{[}t{]}} references the same
time step being defined. \texttt{t\ \textless{}\ t} is false. A value at
time \texttt{t} cannot depend on itself---that would be a circular
definition. The reference index must be strictly less than the declared
index. \texttt{t\ \textless{}\ t} is not strict.

Three of four fragments caught by one rule: reference index
\texttt{\textless{}} declared index. The spatial offset in Fragment B
doesn't trigger the rule because spatial coordinates aren't checked for
causality by default. The rule is simple. The check is mechanical.

Now let's put this mechanism to work.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-optimizer-as-a-recurrence}{%
\subsection{The Optimizer as a
Recurrence}\label{the-optimizer-as-a-recurrence}}

Training a model is a recurrence over time:

\begin{verbatim}
let w[t in 0..T, out, in] = init_random(out, in);
let w[t in 1..T, out, in] = w[t-1, out, in] - lr * grad[t-1, out, in];
\end{verbatim}

At \texttt{t=0}, \texttt{w} is the random initialization. At each
subsequent step, \texttt{w} is the previous \texttt{w} minus a gradient
step. The recurrence reads backward in time (\texttt{t-1}). The time
coordinate \texttt{t} makes the training trajectory explicit. You can
inspect \texttt{w{[}10,\ out,\ in{]}} to see the weights after 10 steps.
You can compute \texttt{w{[}T-1,\ out,\ in{]}\ -\ w{[}0,\ out,\ in{]}}
to see the total change. The time dimension is not hidden inside a
mutable variable---it is a coordinate like any other.

A full training step:

\begin{verbatim}
let logits[t, b, class] = model(x[t, b, feature], w[t, out, feature]);
let loss[t] = cross_entropy[class](logits[t, b, class], labels[t, b]);
let grad[t, out, feature] = @loss[t] / @w[t, out, feature];
let w[t+1, out, feature] = w[t, out, feature] - lr * grad[t, out, feature];
\end{verbatim}

The time coordinate \texttt{t} threads through forward, loss, gradient,
and update. Every tensor knows its temporal position. The gradient
\texttt{@loss{[}t{]}\ /\ @w{[}t,\ out,\ feature{]}} is explicitly
anchored to time step \texttt{t}. The optimizer step defines
\texttt{w{[}t+1{]}} in terms of \texttt{w{[}t{]}} and
\texttt{grad{[}t{]}}.

There is a quieter distinction at work here that deserves explicit
attention: \textbf{parameters versus hyperparameters.} Both are
\texttt{let} bindings. Both are immutable values in scope for subsequent
code. But they have different roles in the optimization story:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ weight: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; out, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = init_random(out, }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ learning_rate: }\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{ = }\DecValTok{0.001}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{weight} is a parameter---its value changes during training,
driven by gradients. Each coordinate on \texttt{weight} tells the
optimizer something: \texttt{out} names the output neurons, \texttt{in}
names the input connections. A weight decay regularizer that treats all
elements uniformly can apply without coordinate awareness, but a
per-neuron regularization policy needs to know which axis is
\texttt{out} and which is \texttt{in}.

\texttt{learning\_rate} is a hyperparameter---it controls the
optimizer's behavior but is not itself updated by gradients. It carries
no coordinate names because it has no coordinate structure.

This distinction is not a language feature. It is a naming discipline.
But the discipline is only possible because the language provides a
place to put the coordinate names. When your tensor framework only
records shapes, you can't name the axes. When you can't name the axes,
you can't write a regularizer that distinguishes them. The optimizer
sees \texttt{(128,\ 64)} and doesn't know which number is \texttt{out}
and which is \texttt{in}. The coordinate names on the parameter are the
bridge between the optimizer's generic arithmetic and the architecture's
specific structure.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{time-is-an-axis-with-a-direction}{%
\subsection{Time Is an Axis with a
Direction}\label{time-is-an-axis-with-a-direction}}

On a spatial axis, all positions exist concurrently. You can sum over
them in any order. On a time axis, position \texttt{t} depends on
position \texttt{t-1}. Not concurrency. Dependency.

This distinction has consequences. A recurrence carries two properties
that spatial coordinates don't require:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Causality}: every time-indexed reference in the body must be
  strictly less than the declared time index. \texttt{t-1} is valid.
  \texttt{t+1} is a compile error.
\item
  \textbf{Memory}: only the time steps that are actually referenced
  backward need to be kept in memory. If every step references only
  \texttt{t-1}, the storage needed is a rolling window of size 2,
  regardless of whether T is 100 or 100,000. This follows mechanically
  from the backward references---no annotation needed.
\end{enumerate}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{bidirectional-recurrence}{%
\subsection{Bidirectional Recurrence}\label{bidirectional-recurrence}}

Not all recurrences look only to the past. A bidirectional RNN reads the
sequence both ways:

\begin{verbatim}
let h_forward[t in 1..T, i] = step(h_forward[t-1, i], x[t, i]);
let h_backward[t in 0..T-1, i] = step_back(h_backward[t+1, i], x[t, i]);
\end{verbatim}

The forward recurrence reads \texttt{t-1}---standard. The backward
recurrence reads \texttt{t+1}---the future from the perspective of
\texttt{t}. This is still valid because the backward recurrence iterates
from right to left: \texttt{t} runs from \texttt{T-1} down to
\texttt{0}, so \texttt{t+1} is always already computed. The direction of
iteration determines which references are ``backward.''

The same coordinate domain, two different iteration directions, one
linguistic mechanism. The declaration bracket names the domain and
direction. The body states the dependency. Consistency is checked.

Now stop. Someone writes this:

\begin{verbatim}
let h[t in 0..T, i] = step(h[t+1, i], x[t, i]);
\end{verbatim}

No iteration direction declared. Just \texttt{t\ in\ 0..T}. The body
references \texttt{t+1}. Is this valid or not?

It depends on whether the compiler infers the iteration direction from
the reference pattern. If \texttt{t+1} references a time step that
hasn't been computed yet (because iteration goes left to right), this is
a forward reference and should be rejected. But if the compiler can
infer that \texttt{t} should iterate from \texttt{T} down to
\texttt{0}---making \texttt{t+1} already computed---it could be valid.

The Einlang rule is conservative: without an explicit reverse-direction
declaration, forward references are rejected. \texttt{t+1} with
\texttt{t\ in\ 0..T} is an error. The programmer must write
\texttt{t\ in\ T..0} (or equivalent syntax) to declare the reverse
iteration. The tool prevents the ambiguous case by default.

This is the same design choice as Chapter 3's Coordinate Contract and
Chapter 4's Pack Disambiguation: when a reference pattern is ambiguous,
the language requires the programmer to disambiguate. Default deny.
Explicit allow.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-rolling-window-what-causality-buys}{%
\subsection{The Rolling Window: What Causality
Buys}\label{the-rolling-window-what-causality-buys}}

Causality is not just a correctness check. It is a memory optimization.

When a recurrence body only references \texttt{t-1}, the compiler knows
that only one previous time step is needed. It can allocate a rolling
window of size 2 rather than storing the entire \texttt{(T,\ ...)}
tensor in memory. When \texttt{T} is 100,000, this is the difference
between allocating gigabytes and allocating megabytes.

This optimization follows mechanically from the backward references. The
compiler scans the body for time-indexed references. Every reference to
\texttt{t\ -\ k} (positive \texttt{k}) requires storing \texttt{k}
previous steps. The rolling window size is \texttt{max(k)}. No
annotation needed. The coordinate names and index arithmetic carry
enough information for the compiler to derive the memory plan.

Consider a second-order recurrence:

\begin{verbatim}
let u[t in 2..T, i] = u[t-1, i] + 0.5 * (u[t-1, i] - u[t-2, i]);
\end{verbatim}

References: \texttt{t-1} and \texttt{t-2}. Maximum offset: 2. Rolling
window size: 3 (current, t-1, t-2). The compiler derives this from the
index expressions. No \texttt{@roll\_window(3)} annotation. The
information is in the code, not in a compiler directive.

This is the pattern that recurs throughout this book: \textbf{make the
structural fact visible in the source code, and let the compiler derive
the engineering consequence.} The programmer writes \texttt{t-2}. The
compiler derives window size 3. The programmer writes
\texttt{sum{[}class{]}}. The compiler derives \texttt{axis=1}. The
programmer writes \texttt{bias{[}j{]}} omitting \texttt{i}. The compiler
derives the backward-pass sum over \texttt{i}. Source records intent.
Compiler derives execution.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{three-declarations-three-storage-schemes}{%
\subsubsection{Three Declarations, Three Storage
Schemes}\label{three-declarations-three-storage-schemes}}

The same recurrence declaration leads to different storage strategies
depending on what the programmer references. The compiler reads the
references and derives the strategy. Three scenarios:

\textbf{Scenario 1: Rolling window.}
\texttt{u{[}t{]}\ =\ f(u{[}t-1{]})}. References: \texttt{t-1} only.
Window size: 2 (one past step plus current). Storage: two arrays. The
compiler allocates \texttt{u\_prev} and \texttt{u\_curr}, swaps them
after each step. No allocation proportional to \texttt{T}.

\textbf{Scenario 2: Full materialization.}
\texttt{u{[}t{]}\ =\ f(u{[}t-1{]})} followed by
\texttt{mean{[}t{]}(u{[}t,\ i{]})}. The entire time trajectory is needed
for the final mean over \texttt{t}. The compiler sees that \texttt{t} is
consumed by a reduction after the recurrence, so all time steps must be
kept. Storage: full \texttt{(T,\ ...)} tensor.

\textbf{Scenario 3: Strided observation.}
\texttt{u{[}t{]}\ =\ f(u{[}t-1{]})} followed by
\texttt{u{[}0{]},\ u{[}10{]},\ u{[}20{]},\ ...} (every 10th step). The
compiler sees that only \texttt{u{[}k*10{]}} is used downstream.
Storage: rolling window of size 10, with the current and last 9 steps
buffered. At each multiple of 10, the current value is written to
persistent storage and the buffer recycles.

In all three scenarios, the declaration is the same:
\texttt{let\ u{[}t\ in\ 0..T,\ i{]}\ =\ f(u{[}t-1,\ i{]})}. The
difference is in what downstream code does with \texttt{u}. The compiler
reads the downstream uses and derives the storage strategy. No
annotations. No \texttt{@roll\_window(3)}. No \texttt{@materialize}. The
structural fact is in the code. The compiler derives the engineering
consequence.

Contrast with a positional framework, where you must manually choose
between \texttt{torch.empty(T,\ ...)} (full materialization) and a
rolling buffer (manual swapping). The choice is an optimization
decision, not a semantic one. The compiler should make it---and can make
it, if the code records the dependency structure. The recurrence body
records the dependency. The downstream uses record the observation
pattern. The compiler connects them.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{time-in-the-training-loop}{%
\subsection{Time in the Training Loop}\label{time-in-the-training-loop}}

The optimizer recurrence from earlier is worth tracing step by step:

\begin{verbatim}
// Step 0: random initialization
let w[0, out, in] = init_random(out, in);

// Step 1: forward pass
let logits[1, b, class] = model(x[1, b, feature], w[0, out, feature]);
let loss[1] = cross_entropy[class](logits[1, b, class], labels[1, b]);

// Step 1: backward pass
let grad[1, out, feature] = @loss[1] / @w[0, out, feature];

// Step 1: update
let w[1, out, feature] = w[0, out, feature] - lr * grad[1, out, feature];

// Step 2: forward pass
let logits[2, b, class] = model(x[2, b, feature], w[1, out, feature]);
// ... and so on
\end{verbatim}

At each time step, three things happen: forward (model produces output),
backward (gradient is computed), update (weights move against the
gradient). The time index \texttt{t} is explicit on every tensor. You
can read the value of \texttt{w} after any step. You can read the loss
at any step. The training trajectory is a tensor, not a sequence of
in-place mutations.

Now compare to the PyTorch equivalent:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{w }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ init_random(out, }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, T):}
\NormalTok{    logits }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ model(x[t], w)}
\NormalTok{    loss }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ cross_entropy(logits, labels[t])}
\NormalTok{    loss.backward()}
    \ControlFlowTok{with}\NormalTok{ torch.no_grad():}
\NormalTok{        w }\OperatorTok{-=}\NormalTok{ lr }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ w.grad}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{w} is a single mutable tensor. \texttt{loss} is a scalar. The
time dimension is the loop variable \texttt{t}---visible in the Python
control flow but absent from the tensor structure. You cannot inspect
\texttt{w{[}10{]}} without checkpointing the value at step 10 yourself.
The training trajectory exists in execution time, not in the type
system.

The Einlang version makes the training trajectory a data structure. The
PyTorch version makes it a side effect. The difference is whether you
can query the past.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{diffusion-models-time-as-a-coordinate-with-a-schedule}{%
\subsection{Diffusion Models: Time as a Coordinate with a
Schedule}\label{diffusion-models-time-as-a-coordinate-with-a-schedule}}

Diffusion models are the most time-intensive architecture in modern ML.
A forward process adds noise over \texttt{T} timesteps. A backward
process learns to reverse the noising. The time coordinate appears in
two roles: as a recurrence index for the sampling chain, and as a
conditioning signal for the denoising network.

\begin{verbatim}
let x[t in 0..T, b, c, h, w] = ...;
let eps[t in 1..T, b, c, h, w] = noise_schedule[t] * randn(...);
let x[t in 1..T, b, c, h, w] = sqrt(1 - beta[t]) * x[t-1, ...] + sqrt(beta[t]) * eps[t, ...];
\end{verbatim}

The time index \texttt{t} threads through the forward noising process.
At each step, noise is added. The schedule \texttt{beta{[}t{]}} controls
how much noise---and \texttt{beta} is indexed by \texttt{t}, making the
dependency visible.

In the backward pass (the learned denoising):

\begin{verbatim}
let x_hat[t in T..1, b, c, h, w] = denoise(x[t, ...], t, model(x[t, ...], t));
\end{verbatim}

The iteration runs backward: \texttt{T..1}. The model receives
\texttt{t} as conditioning---it needs to know which timestep it's
denoising. In a positional framework, \texttt{t} is a positional
encoding vector concatenated or added to the input, and the loop runs in
Python. In Einlang, \texttt{t} is a coordinate that flows through the
model call: the model's signature can declare
\texttt{fn\ denoise{[}t,\ ...{]}(x:\ {[}f32;\ t,\ ...{]})} and the
coordinate \texttt{t} is carried alongside the tensor data.

This is the same mechanism that carried \texttt{class} through
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}} in Chapter 3, applied to time. The
coordinate is the same kind of thing. The direction---forward or
backward---is the only difference.

Time is not ``special.'' It is a coordinate with a direction constraint.
The constraint is checked. The coordinate flows through functions. The
training loop is a recurrence. The diffusion process is a recurrence.
The optimizer is a recurrence. Three domains, one mechanism. The names
make them recognizable as the same thing. It carries direction. It
carries dependency. It carries a constraint: you can only look backward
along it. These properties are not metaphorical. They are enforced at
the level of index expressions. A colleague who writes
\texttt{let\ h{[}t\ in\ 0..T{]}\ =\ step(h{[}t+1{]},\ x{[}t{]})} will
get a compile error---not a runtime divergence, not a silent wrong
answer. The syntax makes the constraint checkable.

Now pause. Before you move to the next chapter, answer this: what other
tensor operations have an implied direction? Think about your own code.
Have you ever written a recurrence where the time axis was not the first
axis? Where the dependency went both forward and backward? Where the
``time'' was not time at all---but a layer index in a residual network,
an iteration counter in an optimizer, a step in a diffusion process?

Recurrence is not unique to RNNs. Every iterative computation is a
recurrence. Every optimizer step is a recurrence. Every diffusion
timestep is a recurrence. The coordinate \texttt{t} is not ``the time
axis.'' It is ``the axis along which things depend on earlier things.''
Causality is the constraint. The directional coordinate is the mechanism
that enforces it.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-find-the-time-axes-in-your-code}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: Find the Time Axes in Your
Code}\label{stop-and-think-find-the-time-axes-in-your-code}}

Not every axis is spatial. Some axes have direction---values depend on
earlier positions along the same axis. Go find them in your code.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Search for \texttt{for\ t\ in\ range(} in your training
  scripts.} Each one is a recurrence. The loop body mutates a variable.
  The mutation order is the time direction. Now ask: could you write
  this recurrence declaratively, with \texttt{t} as a coordinate? What
  would the declaration look like? What would \texttt{t-1} reference?
\item
  \textbf{Search for \texttt{reversed(range(} or backward loops.} Each
  one is a backward recurrence. The iteration direction is reversed. In
  a named-coordinate recurrence, the direction would be part of the
  declaration. In a positional loop, it's encoded in the
  \texttt{reversed} call. If the loop direction and the dependency
  direction disagree, the positional version runs silently. The named
  version rejects at compile time.
\item
  \textbf{Find a loop where you manually manage a rolling window.} You
  allocate a buffer of size \texttt{k}, shift values at each iteration,
  and overwrite the oldest. The window size \texttt{k} is determined by
  how far back the computation looks. In a named-coordinate recurrence,
  the compiler would derive \texttt{k} from the index expressions. In
  your manual version, \texttt{k} is a constant you chose. If the
  computation changes to look back 3 steps instead of 2, you must update
  \texttt{k}. Would you remember?
\item
  \textbf{Ask yourself: what other tensor operations have an implied
  direction?} Think about your own code. Have you ever written a
  recurrence where the time axis was not the first axis? Where the
  dependency went both forward and backward? Where the ``time'' was not
  time at all---but a layer index in a residual network, an iteration
  counter in an optimizer, a step in a diffusion process?
\end{enumerate}

The directional coordinate is not limited to time. Any coordinate where
position \texttt{n} depends on position \texttt{n-1} is directional. The
constraint is the same: you can only look backward along it. The
mechanism is the same: a directional declaration with index arithmetic.
The names \texttt{t}, \texttt{layer}, \texttt{step} are just names---the
compiler checks the direction, not the label.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-gradient-of-a-recurrence}{%
\subsection{The Gradient of a
Recurrence}\label{the-gradient-of-a-recurrence}}

Recurrences have gradients. And because recurrences are
self-referential---each step depends on the previous step---the gradient
must flow backward through time. This is Backpropagation Through Time
(BPTT), and its coordinate structure is the same recurrence, read in
reverse.

Forward: \texttt{h{[}t{]}\ =\ step(h{[}t-1{]},\ x{[}t{]})}. The output
\texttt{h{[}t{]}} depends on \texttt{h{[}t-1{]}}, which depends on
\texttt{h{[}t-2{]}}, and so on back to \texttt{h{[}0{]}}.

Backward: the gradient \texttt{d\_loss/d\_h{[}t{]}} must propagate to
\texttt{d\_loss/d\_h{[}t-1{]}}, then to \texttt{d\_loss/d\_h{[}t-2{]}},
and so on. At each step, the gradient flows through the \texttt{step}
function's Jacobian with respect to \texttt{h{[}t-1{]}}. The backward
recurrence:

\begin{verbatim}
let d_h[t in T..0] = @loss[t] / @h[t] + @step(h[t], h[t-1], x[t]) / @h[t] * d_h[t+1];
\end{verbatim}

The backward recurrence runs from \texttt{T} down to \texttt{0},
referencing \texttt{t+1} (the future in the backward direction, which
has already been computed). This is the same bidirectional mechanism
from Section 6, applied to the gradient. The coordinate \texttt{t} still
carries the causality constraint, but the iteration direction has
reversed.

In a positional framework, BPTT is implemented by writing a separate
backward loop that iterates in reverse. The relationship between the
forward loop \texttt{for\ t\ in\ range(T)} and the backward loop
\texttt{for\ t\ in\ reversed(range(T))} is in the programmer's
head---the two loops are separate code blocks. In Einlang, the backward
recurrence is generated from the forward recurrence by the same
Inversion Rule that governs reductions and broadcasts. The forward
recurrence declares \texttt{t\ in\ 1..T} with \texttt{t-1} references.
The backward recurrence is \texttt{t\ in\ T..0} with \texttt{t+1}
references, generated automatically. The time direction flips. The
coordinate names stay the same. The compiler generates the backward loop
from the forward declaration.

In the next chapter, we explore what happens when coordinates split,
merge, and carry arithmetic---the complex terrain of distance matrices,
convolutions, and fancy indexing.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-7-complex-terrain}{%
\section{Chapter 7 · Complex Terrain}\label{chapter-7-complex-terrain}}

\begin{quote}
``The map is not the territory---but a good map tells you where the
boundaries are.''

--- Adapted from Alfred Korzybski
\end{quote}

\emph{Combinations · Distance matrices, convolution, and fancy indexing}

\vspace{2em}

So far our coordinates have been simple: one name per axis, each axis
independent. Real tensor programs are messier. A coordinate can split in
two. It can carry arithmetic. It can appear in patterns where the same
name used twice means something different from two names used once.

This chapter explores the terrain where naming earns its keep---where
the coordinate story is too complex to hold in your head, and the
notation either carries it or loses it.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{distance-matrix-when-one-coordinate-becomes-two}{%
\subsection{Distance Matrix: When One Coordinate Becomes
Two}\label{distance-matrix-when-one-coordinate-becomes-two}}

Given a set of points, compute the pairwise distance between every pair.
The same coordinate---\texttt{point}---appears twice, playing two roles:
once as the source, once as the target.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ dist}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{point_i, point_j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{    sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{dim}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{( (points}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{point_i, dim}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - points}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{point_j, dim}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) ** }\DecValTok{2.0}\NormalTok{ ) ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{point} has been split into \texttt{point\_i} and
\texttt{point\_j}. Both index the same underlying domain---the set of
points. But \texttt{point\_i} walks the rows of the distance matrix and
\texttt{point\_j} walks the columns. The naming makes the split visible.
That both index into the same coordinate domain is checked. The reader
sees that \texttt{point} was duplicated, not two unrelated coordinates
that happen to share a prefix.

In a positional framework, this split is invisible:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{dist }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ((points[:, }\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{, :] }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ points[}\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{, :, :]) }\OperatorTok{**} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{**} \FloatTok{0.5}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{None} inserts a dimension. \texttt{:} slices everything. Which
dimension is \texttt{point\_i} and which is \texttt{point\_j}? You have
to count positions. If \texttt{points} changes from \texttt{(N,\ D)} to
