\texttt{(D,\ N)}, the positional code silently computes the wrong
matrix.

The coordinate-split pattern appears in many domains. Once you recognize
it, you see it everywhere:

\textbf{Graph neural networks.} \texttt{source\_node} and
\texttt{target\_node} split from the same node domain. Edges connect
\texttt{source\_node} to \texttt{target\_node}. The adjacency matrix has
\texttt{(source\_node,\ target\_node)} coordinates. A message-passing
step gathers from \texttt{target\_node} and scatters to
\texttt{source\_node}. In positional code, both are axis 0 and axis 1.
In named code, they carry different identities even when they index the
same domain.

\textbf{Contrastive learning.} \texttt{anchor} and \texttt{positive}
index the same set of samples. The similarity matrix has
\texttt{(anchor,\ positive)} coordinates. The loss pulls
\texttt{anchor{[}i{]}} close to \texttt{positive{[}i{]}} (pairwise) and
pushes \texttt{anchor{[}i{]}} away from \texttt{positive{[}j{]}} for
\texttt{i\ !=\ j}. The coordinate split
\texttt{anchor}/\texttt{positive} distinguishes ``same sample, different
view'' from ``different sample.'' In positional code, the distinction is
in the mask tensor, not in the coordinate structure.

\textbf{Collaborative filtering.} \texttt{user} and \texttt{item} share
a latent factor \texttt{k} through a matrix factorization:
\texttt{ratings{[}user,\ item{]}\ =\ sum{[}k{]}(U{[}user,\ k{]}\ *\ V{[}item,\ k{]})}.
The inner dimension \texttt{k} is shared and consumed. \texttt{user} and
\texttt{item} are different coordinates indexing different domains. In
positional code, \texttt{U\ @\ V.T}---all three identities
(\texttt{user}, \texttt{item}, \texttt{k}) collapsed into positions.

In every case, the positional approach uses
\texttt{unsqueeze}-and-\texttt{None} to create the cross-product
dimensions. The named approach splits one coordinate into two with
explicit names. The names record the split. The positional code records
the mechanic.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{convolution-coordinates-with-arithmetic}{%
\subsection{Convolution: Coordinates with
Arithmetic}\label{convolution-coordinates-with-arithmetic}}

A convolution is a sum of products with index arithmetic:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ conv}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, oc, oh, ow}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{ic, kh, kw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    input}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, ic, oh + kh, ow + kw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * weight}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{oc, ic, kh, kw}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The novelty is \texttt{oh\ +\ kh} and \texttt{ow\ +\ kw}. These are
index expressions---arithmetic on coordinate variables.
\texttt{oh\ +\ kh} says: to read from the input at spatial position
\texttt{oh\ +\ kh}, take the output position \texttt{oh} and add the
kernel offset \texttt{kh}. Bounds of this arithmetic are not verified
(that's a runtime check), but every coordinate in the index expression
is verified to be in scope and to have a known domain.

Notice where the expressions live: in the body index positions, not in
the declaration bracket. The declaration bracket names the output
coordinates \texttt{b,\ oc,\ oh,\ ow}---simple identifiers. The body
index expressions \texttt{oh\ +\ kh} describe how to compute the input
indices from the output indices and the kernel coordinates. Left side:
definition. Right side: computation.

The gradient with respect to \texttt{weight} follows mechanically from
the coordinate sets:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ dW}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{oc, ic, kh, kw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, oh, ow}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    dConv}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, oc, oh, ow}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * input}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, ic, oh + kh, ow + kw}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The coordinates \texttt{b}, \texttt{oh}, \texttt{ow} are summed away
because they appear in the output but not in \texttt{weight}. The
surviving coordinates \texttt{oc}, \texttt{ic}, \texttt{kh}, \texttt{kw}
are exactly \texttt{weight}'s coordinates. Set subtraction, applied to
coordinate names, derives the formula. No memorization of transpose
rules needed.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{depth-to-space-one-line-instead-of-three}{%
\subsection{Depth-to-Space: One Line Instead of
Three}\label{depth-to-space-one-line-instead-of-three}}

A depth-to-space operation reshapes a tensor by moving pixels from the
channel dimension into the spatial dimensions, increasing resolution. In
a positional framework:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{b, c, h, w }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(b, c }\OperatorTok{//} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, h, w)}
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.permute(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(b, c }\OperatorTok{//} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, h }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, w }\OperatorTok{*} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Three operations. Six position numbers to track. The semantic
claim---``channel pixels become spatial neighbors''---is invisible.

In Einlang:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ y}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, c_out, h * }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{ + dy, w * }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{ + dx}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{    x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, c_out * }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{ + dy * }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{ + dx, h, w}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

One line. The index arithmetic says exactly what moved where. The names
\texttt{dy} and \texttt{dx} declare the sub-pixel offsets. The
multiplication \texttt{h\ *\ 2\ +\ dy} expresses the output spatial
coordinate in terms of the input. The coordinate names carry the story.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{fancy-indexing-names-disambiguate}{%
\subsection{Fancy Indexing: Names
Disambiguate}\label{fancy-indexing-names-disambiguate}}

Fancy indexing---using arrays of indices rather than slices---is where
positional notation becomes most ambiguous. Consider gathering elements
from a matrix:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Positional: what does this gather?}
\NormalTok{result }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ matrix[indices_i, indices_j]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In NumPy, this does \emph{pairwise} indexing:
\texttt{result{[}k{]}\ =\ matrix{[}indices\_i{[}k{]},\ indices\_j{[}k{]}{]}}.
But if \texttt{indices\_i} is a row vector and \texttt{indices\_j} is a
column vector, broadcasting produces \emph{outer-product} indexing
instead. The behavior depends on the shapes of the index arrays, not on
anything written in the code.

In Einlang, the coordinate names disambiguate:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Pairwise gather: same coordinate name appears in both index positions}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ gathered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = matrix}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{indices_i}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, indices_j}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]]}\NormalTok{;}

\CommentTok{// Outer-product gather: different coordinate names → Cartesian product}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ outer}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = matrix}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{indices_i}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, indices_j}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When \texttt{k} appears in both index positions, pairwise indexing is
inferred: \texttt{indices\_i} and \texttt{indices\_j} are traversed
together. When \texttt{i} and \texttt{j} are different coordinates,
outer-product indexing is inferred: every combination is produced. The
distinction is visible in the coordinate names, not hidden in the shapes
of the index arrays.

In NumPy, you need \texttt{np.ix\_} or NEP 21's
\texttt{oindex}/\texttt{vindex} to explicitly declare which behavior you
want. In Einlang, the names do it.

Now stop and think about your own NumPy code. If you don't use
\texttt{np.ix\_}, how do you make NumPy produce outer-product indexing
instead of pairwise indexing? What mental conversion do you perform?

You probably create broadcast dimensions manually:
\texttt{indices\_i{[}:,\ None{]}} and \texttt{indices\_j{[}None,\ :{]}}.
You add a dummy axis. You count positions. You check that the shapes
will broadcast correctly. Then you index and hope.

What you are doing in your head is distinguishing two relationships:
\emph{these two index arrays walk together} (pairwise) versus
\emph{these two index arrays walk independently} (outer-product). You
encode the distinction through shape manipulation---adding dimensions,
aligning axes, relying on broadcast semantics. The distinction exists in
your mental model. It does not exist in the code.

In Einlang, the distinction is in the coordinate names. \texttt{k} in
both index positions means pairwise. \texttt{i} and \texttt{j} as
different coordinate names mean outer-product. The mental conversion you
were doing manually---``this is pairwise, that is outer-product''---is
now part of the notation. The code says what you meant, not the
mechanical steps to achieve it.

This is a specific instance of a general principle that has been quietly
accumulating throughout these chapters: \textbf{the coordinate name is
the unit of intent.} Every time you find yourself manually reshaping to
encode a semantic distinction, ask: could a coordinate name carry this
instead?

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{when-one-coordinate-becomes-two-gather-vs.scatter}{%
\subsection{When One Coordinate Becomes Two: Gather
vs.~Scatter}\label{when-one-coordinate-becomes-two-gather-vs.scatter}}

Fancy indexing isn't the only place where coordinates split. Consider
gathering rows from a matrix by index:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Positional: gather rows 0, 3, 2 from a matrix}
\NormalTok{rows }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ matrix[[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], :]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The index list \texttt{{[}0,\ 3,\ 2{]}} selects specific positions along
axis 0. But what if you also want to select specific columns? And what
if those column indices depend on the row?

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Positional: for each selected row, pick a different column}
\NormalTok{idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.array([}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{])   }\CommentTok{# row indices}
\NormalTok{col_idx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.array([}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{])  }\CommentTok{# column index for each row}
\NormalTok{result }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ matrix[idx, col_idx]  }\CommentTok{# pairwise: result[k] = matrix[idx[k], col_idx[k]]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This is pairwise indexing---\texttt{k} walks through both index arrays
together. But what if you wanted outer-product: every combination of
\texttt{idx} and \texttt{col\_idx}? You'd need:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{result }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ matrix[idx[:, }\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{], col_idx[}\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{, :]]  }\CommentTok{# outer-product via broadcast}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Two different behaviors, two different indexing patterns, one API. The
difference is in the shapes of the index arrays (1D vs 2D after
broadcasting), not in any semantic marker. If \texttt{idx} and
\texttt{col\_idx} happen to have the same length, the pairwise version
runs and produces a result---just not the one you wanted if you intended
outer-product.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Pairwise: same coordinate name in both index positions}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ gathered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = matrix}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{idx}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, col_idx}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]]}\NormalTok{;}

\CommentTok{// Outer-product: different coordinate names → Cartesian product}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ gathered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = matrix}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{idx}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, col_idx}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When \texttt{k} appears in both index positions, the compiler infers
pairwise indexing. When \texttt{i} and \texttt{j} are different,
outer-product is inferred. The coordinate name \emph{is} the
disambiguation. No shape-dependent behavior. No manual broadcasting.

Now ask yourself: which version of fancy indexing do you use more often?
Pairwise or outer-product? For most people, outer-product is rarer---but
when you need it, the positional approach requires adding dummy axes by
hand. The named approach requires changing one letter to two. The mental
model is the same in both cases (``these index together'' vs ``these
index independently''). The named notation records that model. The
positional notation buries it in shapes.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-coordinate-collision-test}{%
\subsection{The Coordinate Collision
Test}\label{the-coordinate-collision-test}}

There is a simple test for whether your notation disambiguates well.
Write down two operations that have the same shape but different
coordinate semantics. Show them to a colleague. Ask which is which.

For fancy indexing:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Operation A}
\NormalTok{result }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ matrix[idx, col_idx]}

\CommentTok{# Operation B}
\NormalTok{result }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ matrix[idx[:, }\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{], col_idx[}\VariableTok{None}\NormalTok{, :]]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If \texttt{idx} has length 5 and \texttt{col\_idx} has length 5, both
operations produce a \texttt{(5,\ 5)} result. But Operation A produces
the diagonal (pairwise), while Operation B produces the full Cartesian
product. From the code alone---without printing shapes or reading
comments---can your colleague tell which is which?

Now the Einlang versions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Operation A: pairwise}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ result}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = matrix}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{idx}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, col_idx}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]]}\NormalTok{;}

\CommentTok{// Operation B: outer-product}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ result}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = matrix}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{idx}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, col_idx}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The difference is in the coordinate names. \texttt{k} vs
\texttt{(i,\ j)}. One coordinate means pairwise. Two means
outer-product. Your colleague reads the code and sees the difference.
The code records the intent.

This is the Coordinate Collision Test: when two operations produce the
same shape but different semantics, does your notation distinguish them?
Positional notation often fails this test---by design. It records
shapes, not semantics. Named notation passes it---by design. It records
coordinates, and coordinates carry semantics.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{convolution-backward-gradient-as-index-arithmetic}{%
\subsection{Convolution Backward: Gradient as Index
Arithmetic}\label{convolution-backward-gradient-as-index-arithmetic}}

Chapter 7 introduced the convolution gradient for weights. Let's derive
the input gradient---the one that backpropagates through the
network---to see how index arithmetic survives differentiation.

Forward:
\texttt{conv{[}b,\ oc,\ oh,\ ow{]}\ =\ sum{[}ic,\ kh,\ kw{]}(input{[}b,\ ic,\ oh\ +\ kh,\ ow\ +\ kw{]}\ *\ weight{[}oc,\ ic,\ kh,\ kw{]})}.

We need \texttt{d\_input{[}b,\ ic,\ ih,\ iw{]}}. Apply the five-step
procedure from Chapter 7:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Hold one cell} of \texttt{input}:
  \texttt{input{[}b0,\ ic0,\ ih0,\ iw0{]}}.
\item
  \textbf{List every output cell that reads it.} The held cell is read
  by every \texttt{conv{[}b0,\ oc,\ oh,\ ow{]}} where
  \texttt{oh\ +\ kh\ =\ ih0} and \texttt{ow\ +\ kw\ =\ iw0}, for all
  \texttt{oc}, all \texttt{kh}, all \texttt{kw}. That means
  \texttt{oh\ =\ ih0\ -\ kh} and \texttt{ow\ =\ iw0\ -\ kw}. For each
  \texttt{(kh,\ kw)}, the output at position
  \texttt{(oh,\ ow)\ =\ (ih0\ -\ kh,\ iw0\ -\ kw)} receives a
  contribution.
\item
  \textbf{Attach the incoming gradient.} Each output cell
  \texttt{conv{[}b0,\ oc,\ oh,\ ow{]}} carries
  \texttt{d\_conv{[}b0,\ oc,\ oh,\ ow{]}}. The contribution through the
  held input cell is
  \texttt{d\_conv{[}b0,\ oc,\ ih0\ -\ kh,\ iw0\ -\ kw{]}\ *\ weight{[}oc,\ ic0,\ kh,\ kw{]}}.
\item
  \textbf{Multiply by the local derivative.} The derivative of
  \texttt{input\ *\ weight} with respect to \texttt{input} is
  \texttt{weight}.
\item
  \textbf{Sum over the path coordinates.} The output has
  \texttt{\{b,\ oc,\ oh,\ ow\}}. The input has
  \texttt{\{b,\ ic,\ ih,\ iw\}}. The path coordinates are
  \texttt{\{oc,\ kh,\ kw\}}---they appear in the output (via
  \texttt{oh,\ ow}) but are absorbed into \texttt{ih,\ iw} through the
  index arithmetic. But \texttt{oh,\ ow} are not independent---they are
  coupled to \texttt{ih,\ iw} through \texttt{kh,\ kw}. The sum is over
  \texttt{\{oc,\ kh,\ kw\}}, with the index relationship inverted:
  \texttt{oh\ →\ ih\ -\ kh}, \texttt{ow\ →\ iw\ -\ kw}.
\end{enumerate}

Result:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ d_input}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, ic, ih, iw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{oc, kh, kw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    d_conv}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, oc, ih - kh, iw - kw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * weight}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{oc, ic, kh, kw}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This is the convolution transpose---a transposed convolution with
flipped kernel indices. You derived it without memorizing ``the gradient
of convolution is a transposed convolution.'' You did coordinate
accounting: which coordinates is the output indexed by that the input is
not? The output uses \texttt{oh,\ ow} where the input uses
\texttt{ih,\ iw}. The kernel indices \texttt{kh,\ kw} are shared---they
appear in both the forward and the gradient. The output channel
\texttt{oc} is in the output but not the input---sum over it.

The index arithmetic \texttt{ih\ -\ kh} and \texttt{iw\ -\ kw} comes
from inverting the forward relationship \texttt{oh\ +\ kh\ →\ ih}. The
gradient ``reads'' from the output at the position where the input
contributed. The inversion is mechanical: solve
\texttt{oh\ +\ kh\ =\ ih} for \texttt{oh}, giving
\texttt{oh\ =\ ih\ -\ kh}.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-boundary-what-names-cant-check}{%
\subsection{The Boundary: What Names Can't
Check}\label{the-boundary-what-names-cant-check}}

Named coordinates are powerful, but they have a boundary. That a
coordinate \emph{exists} on a tensor is verified. Index arithmetic
bounds are not verified. \texttt{oh\ +\ kh} is syntactically
checked---\texttt{oh} and \texttt{kh} must be in scope---but whether
\texttt{oh\ +\ kh} exceeds the input's spatial extent is a runtime
question.

This boundary is not a flaw. It is a design choice. What can be proven
from names and domains alone is checked. Bounds checking is the
runtime's job. Semantic correctness---whether the formula means what you
think it means---is yours.

Arithmetic cannot be verified. But the guarantee is: \textbf{everything
that CAN be automatically checked IS automatically checked. Everything
that CANNOT be automatically checked is made explicitly visible for you
to review.} The coordinate names are the bridge between the two
categories. They make the checkable parts machine-verifiable and the
uncheckable parts human-visible.

The names are there so that when the runtime does report an
out-of-bounds error, the error message can say which coordinate
overflowed.
\texttt{IndexError:\ oh\ +\ kh\ =\ 67\ exceeds\ input\ width\ 64} is a
better error than \texttt{IndexError:\ dimension\ 3\ out\ of\ bounds}.

\vspace{2em}

Named coordinates handle the complex terrain---splits, arithmetic,
disambiguation---by giving each coordinate a persistent identity. The
same identity that survived a permutation in Chapter 1 survives index
arithmetic here. The notation scales because the names scale.

But there is a question worth asking before we leave this terrain:
\textbf{when do names fail?}

Names fail when the coordinate structure is truly unknown at compile
time. A fully dynamic computation graph where shapes depend on runtime
values---the number of detected objects in an image, the length of a
generated sequence---cannot be fully verified by coordinate names alone.
The names can check that \texttt{obj} is a declared coordinate and that
functions consuming it have consistent contracts. They cannot check that
\texttt{obj} has range 0..7 in one run and 0..12 in the next.

This is the boundary from Section 5, restated: names check consistency,
not correctness. The compiler verifies that the coordinate story is
internally coherent. It does not verify that the story matches reality.
For that, you need runtime assertions. For that, you need tests. The
names reduce the surface area of things that can go wrong silently. They
do not eliminate the need for vigilance.

But vigilance is easier when the code records what you were being
vigilant about. A name is a note to your future self: \emph{this
coordinate matters, I checked it, and the compiler checks that I checked
it.}

The boundary between what names can and cannot check is not a weakness
of the naming approach. It is a precise map of what is statically
knowable. Every fact the names cannot check---bounds of index
arithmetic, runtime-dependent shapes, semantic correctness of the
formula itself---is a fact that \emph{no} purely static system can
check. The names don't fail at these boundaries. They mark them. The
coordinate names are in the index expressions when the bounds are
checked at runtime, in the declaration when the shape is resolved, in
the function signature when the meaning is asserted. The names are there
when the check happens---even if the check is runtime, not compile time.

This is what ``everything checkable is checked'' means in practice. The
compiler checks what it can from declarations alone. What it cannot
check, it leaves visible---with names attached---so that runtime checks
and human reviewers know what to look for. A name is more useful when
it's checked at compile time, but it's still useful when it's only
checked at runtime.
\texttt{IndexError:\ oh\ +\ kh\ =\ 67\ exceeds\ input\ width\ 64} names
the coordinate that overflowed.
\texttt{IndexError:\ dimension\ 3\ out\ of\ bounds} names a position.
Which error would you rather debug at 3 AM?

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-the-splits-in-your-code}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: The Splits in Your
Code}\label{stop-and-think-the-splits-in-your-code}}

Every time a coordinate splits into two roles---source and target,
anchor and positive, user and item---you are doing coordinate splitting.
The split is the operation. The names record it.

Open your codebase. Find a place where you inserted a dimension with
\texttt{None}, \texttt{unsqueeze}, or \texttt{np.newaxis} to create a
pairwise matrix. For each one:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{What coordinate split?} What single coordinate were you
  splitting into two? Write the two new names.
\item
  \textbf{Why did you split it?} Was it for a distance matrix? A
  similarity matrix? An attention mask? An outer product?
\item
  \textbf{Would a reader know which is which?} If they see
  \texttt{matrix{[}i{]}} and \texttt{matrix{[}j{]}} with \texttt{i} and
  \texttt{j} as different names, they know it's a split. If they see
  \texttt{matrix{[}:,\ None{]}} and \texttt{matrix{[}None,\ :{]}}, they
  see shape manipulation. The same intent. Different visibility.
\end{enumerate}

The splits are there. The names make them readable.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Find the most complex tensor indexing line in your codebase---the
one with multiple \texttt{unsqueeze}/\texttt{expand}/\texttt{permute}
calls chained together. Rewrite it with coordinate names, even just in a
comment. Notice how many of those operations disappear when dimensions
have identities.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{when-index-arithmetic-meets-gradients}{%
\subsection{When Index Arithmetic Meets
Gradients}\label{when-index-arithmetic-meets-gradients}}

The convolution backward example earlier in this chapter derived the
input gradient:
\texttt{d\_input{[}b,\ ic,\ ih,\ iw{]}\ =\ sum{[}oc,\ kh,\ kw{]}(d\_conv{[}b,\ oc,\ ih\ -\ kh,\ iw\ -\ kw{]}\ *\ weight{[}oc,\ ic,\ kh,\ kw{]})}.
The index arithmetic \texttt{ih\ -\ kh} and \texttt{iw\ -\ kw} comes
from inverting the forward relationship \texttt{oh\ +\ kh\ =\ ih},
solved for \texttt{oh\ →\ ih\ -\ kh}.

This is a general pattern: index arithmetic in the forward pass produces
inverted index arithmetic in the backward pass. If the forward pass has
\texttt{input{[}...,\ oh\ +\ kh,\ ...{]}}, the backward pass has
\texttt{d\_conv{[}...,\ ih\ -\ kh,\ ...{]}}. The subtraction inverts the
addition. The coordinates involved in the arithmetic---\texttt{kh},
\texttt{kw}---become reduction axes in the backward sum. The coordinates
that were loop axes---\texttt{oh}, \texttt{ow}---become the path
coordinates that get summed.

In a positional autodiff engine like PyTorch's, this inversion is
computed by the engine's internal graph. The programmer never writes
\texttt{ih\ -\ kh}. The engine traces the forward operations, records
the index arithmetic as node dependencies, and derives the backward
computation automatically. The programmer writes
\texttt{conv2d(input,\ weight)} and the gradient is handled by autograd.

The difference is not correctness---both derive the same result. The
difference is auditability. When the backward pass is written explicitly
with coordinate names, a reader can trace \texttt{ih\ -\ kh} back to the
forward \texttt{oh\ +\ kh} and verify that the inversion is correct.
When the backward pass is generated by the autodiff engine, the
inversion is a black box. It is correct until it isn't---and when it
isn't (because a custom kernel was written with the wrong index
arithmetic, or because a \texttt{@tf.custom\_gradient} rule has a bug),
the reader has no source-level path from the forward expression to the
backward expression.

Named coordinates don't replace autodiff. They give autodiff's output a
form that a human can read, verify, and debug. The index arithmetic is
in the source. The coordinate names tell you what is being inverted. The
reader can trace the thread.

In the next chapter, we turn to the operation that makes all of this
learnable: differentiation. The forward pass builds a computation. The
backward pass reads it in reverse. And the names are the thread that
ties the two directions together.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-8-names-through-differentiation}{%
\section{Chapter 8 · Names Through
Differentiation}\label{chapter-8-names-through-differentiation}}

\begin{quote}
``In the forward pass, you eliminate information. In the backward pass,
you guess.''

--- The author, after a long debugging session
\end{quote}

\emph{Combinations · Automatic differentiation and the pullback}

\vspace{2em}

A derivative measures sensitivity. \texttt{@loss\ /\ @W} asks: if I
perturb \texttt{W} by a small amount, how much does \texttt{loss}
change? For scalar \texttt{loss} and scalar \texttt{W}, the answer is a
single number. For tensor \texttt{W}, the answer is a tensor of the same
shape as \texttt{W}---each element says how \texttt{loss} responds to
perturbing that specific element.

This chapter traces gradients through matrix multiplication,
convolution, and our own functions. At each step, coordinate names tell
the story of what the gradient must sum over---and what happens when the
names are wrong.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-shopping-cart-and-the-restocking-run}{%
\subsection{The Shopping Cart and the Restocking
Run}\label{the-shopping-cart-and-the-restocking-run}}

Before we dive into the math, an intuition model that will carry us
through the entire chapter. But instead of telling it to you, let's
discover it together.

You are writing the backward pass for a custom layer. The forward code
is in front of you:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ C}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(A}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * B}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You have \texttt{dC{[}i,\ j{]}}---the gradient of the loss with respect
to \texttt{C}. You need \texttt{dA{[}i,\ k{]}}. You don't want to look
up the formula. You don't want to memorize the transpose rule. You just
want to derive it from what the forward code says.

Start from first principles. \texttt{A{[}i,\ k{]}} appears exactly
where? Inside the \texttt{sum{[}k{]}}: it is multiplied by
\texttt{B{[}k,\ j{]}} and then summed over \texttt{k}. For a specific
element \texttt{A{[}i0,\ k0{]}}, which output cells does it contribute
to? Every output cell where \texttt{i\ =\ i0} AND the sum includes
\texttt{k\ =\ k0}. That means \texttt{C{[}i0,\ j{]}} for \emph{all}
\texttt{j}.

So \texttt{A{[}i0,\ k0{]}} sends a contribution
\texttt{A{[}i0,\ k0{]}\ *\ B{[}k0,\ j{]}} to \texttt{C{[}i0,\ j{]}}. The
gradient signal \texttt{dC{[}i0,\ j{]}} must flow back along that same
path. The local derivative of \texttt{A{[}i,\ k{]}\ *\ B{[}k,\ j{]}}
with respect to \texttt{A{[}i,\ k{]}} is \texttt{B{[}k,\ j{]}}. So the
contribution from output cell \texttt{C{[}i0,\ j{]}} back to
\texttt{A{[}i0,\ k0{]}} is \texttt{dC{[}i0,\ j{]}\ *\ B{[}k0,\ j{]}}.

Now sum over all the output cells that received a contribution. Those
are all \texttt{j} positions. The coordinate \texttt{j} is in \texttt{C}
but NOT in \texttt{A}. Sum over it:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ dA}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(dC}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * B}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{k, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You just derived the matmul pullback. You didn't memorize it. You didn't
look it up. You did coordinate accounting: which coordinates does the
output have that the operand doesn't? Sum over those.

Now stop and notice what you just did. In your head, you traced which
coordinates were ``paths'' from \texttt{A} to \texttt{C}. The coordinate
\texttt{j} was a path---every output position along \texttt{j} received
input from \texttt{A{[}i0,\ k0{]}}. To send the gradient back, you had
to sum over that path. The coordinate \texttt{k} was consumed by the
forward sum---it existed in \texttt{A} and was eliminated. The backward
pass doesn't need to sum over \texttt{k} because \texttt{A{[}i,\ k{]}}
only contributed to \texttt{C{[}i,\ j{]}} through the specific
\texttt{k} value in the sum. Each \texttt{k} position's gradient is
independent.

Here is the pattern. In the forward pass, some coordinates are
\emph{consumed}---a reduction (\texttt{sum{[}k{]}}) eliminates them.
Some coordinates are \emph{silent}---a broadcast copies a value along
them without the value depending on them. In the backward pass, every
consumption becomes a broadcast (the gradient must be spread back over
what was consumed) and every silence becomes a reduction (all the copies
must be collected).

This is the pullback rule, stated as a shopping trip:

\textbf{Forward pass = shopping.} You walk through the aisles with a
cart. From each shelf (input tensor), you take items (values). At the
register, some items are consumed: a reduction eliminated a coordinate
(\texttt{sum{[}k{]}}). Other items were never on your list: a broadcast
silently copied them along a coordinate you didn't care about
(\texttt{bias{[}j{]}} was silent on \texttt{b}).

The forward pass leaves a record:

\begin{verbatim}
// C[i, j] = sum[k](A[i, k] * B[k, j])
// Record: A appeared with coordinates [i, k]. Output has [i, j].
//         k was consumed by the sum. j is a path coordinate.
//         B appeared with coordinates [k, j]. k was consumed. i is a path coordinate.
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Backward pass = restocking.} The store manager takes the record
and works backward. For every coordinate that was consumed in the
forward pass, the manager must broadcast replenishment back. For every
coordinate that was a path (in the output but not in the operand), the
manager must sum the gradient signals:

\begin{verbatim}
// dA[i, k] = sum[j](dC[i, j] * B[k, j])
// Record reads: A has [i, k]. C has [i, j]. Path coordinate: j. Sum over j.
//               k was consumed forward → will be reconstructed (B provides it).
\end{verbatim}

The forward record and the backward record are the same document, read
in opposite directions. What was consumed becomes broadcast. What was a
path becomes a sum. The coordinate names tell you which is which.

Now let's see this pattern systematically.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-gradient-as-coordinate-subtraction}{%
\subsection{The Gradient as Coordinate
Subtraction}\label{the-gradient-as-coordinate-subtraction}}

We just derived the matmul pullback by hand. Let's state the general
rule.

\texttt{@loss\ /\ @W} has the same shape as \texttt{W}. The coordinates
on the gradient result are exactly the coordinates on the denominator.

\begin{figure}
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/gradient_pullback.pdf}
\caption{Forward and backward: the gradient sums over coordinates in C
but not in A}
\end{figure}

The path from \texttt{W} to \texttt{loss} eliminates every coordinate
that \texttt{W} carries. The gradient reconstructs those eliminations in
reverse.

The pullback rule in one sentence: \textbf{the gradient sums over the
set-difference of coordinates between the output and the operand.}
Output has \texttt{\{i,\ j\}}. Operand \texttt{A} has
\texttt{\{i,\ k\}}. Difference: \texttt{\{j\}}. Sum over \texttt{j}. The
missing coordinate \texttt{k} is provided by the other operand
\texttt{B{[}k,\ j{]}}.

Coordinate accounting. No transpose rules. No memorization.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-five-step-pullback-procedure}{%
\subsection{The Five-Step Pullback
Procedure}\label{the-five-step-pullback-procedure}}

Given a forward expression and a target operand, derive the gradient:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Hold one cell} of the target operand. Choose a specific
  element---say \texttt{A{[}i0,\ k0{]}}.
\item
  \textbf{List every output cell that reads it.} For
  \texttt{C{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ sum{[}k{]}(A{[}i,\ k{]}\ *\ B{[}k,\ j{]})},
  the held cell is read by every output where \texttt{i\ =\ i0} and the
  sum includes \texttt{k\ =\ k0}. That means \texttt{C{[}i0,\ j{]}} for
  \emph{all} \texttt{j}.
\item
  \textbf{Attach the incoming gradient.} Each output cell
  \texttt{C{[}i0,\ j{]}} carries a gradient signal
  \texttt{dC{[}i0,\ j{]}}. The contribution from the path through
  \texttt{A{[}i0,\ k0{]}} is \texttt{dC{[}i0,\ j{]}\ *\ B{[}k0,\ j{]}}.
\item
  \textbf{Multiply by the local derivative.} For elementwise
  multiplication inside the sum, the local derivative of
  \texttt{A{[}i,\ k{]}\ *\ B{[}k,\ j{]}} with respect to
  \texttt{A{[}i,\ k{]}} is \texttt{B{[}k,\ j{]}}.
\item
  \textbf{Sum the routes.} The path coordinate---the coordinate in
  \texttt{C} but not in \texttt{A}---is \texttt{j}. Sum over it.
\end{enumerate}

The result:
\texttt{dA{[}i,\ k{]}\ =\ sum{[}j{]}(dC{[}i,\ j{]}\ *\ B{[}k,\ j{]})}.
No calculus memorization. No transpose rules. Just coordinate
accounting.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{follow-along-two-more-pullbacks}{%
\subsubsection{Follow Along: Two More
Pullbacks}\label{follow-along-two-more-pullbacks}}

Now you do it. Take out a pen or open a text file. Two forward
expressions. Two gradient derivations. Use the five steps.

\textbf{Scenario 1: Broadcast then reduce.} Forward:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = A}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i, j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + bias}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You have \texttt{d\_out{[}i,\ j{]}}. You need \texttt{d\_bias{[}j{]}}.
Go through the five steps. What coordinates does the output have? What
coordinates does \texttt{bias} have? What's the path coordinate? Sum
over what?

Take two minutes. Write your answer. Then compare.

\vspace{2em}

Done? Here's the derivation:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Hold one cell: \texttt{bias{[}j0{]}}.
\item
  Every output cell reads it: \texttt{out{[}i,\ j0{]}} for \emph{all}
  \texttt{i}. The held \texttt{j0} value is copied to every \texttt{i}
  position.
\item
  Attach the incoming gradient: each output cell carries
  \texttt{d\_out{[}i,\ j0{]}}. The contribution from the path through
  \texttt{bias{[}j0{]}} is \texttt{d\_out{[}i,\ j0{]}\ *\ 1} (the local
  derivative of \texttt{x\ +\ bias} wrt \texttt{bias} is 1).
\item
  Local derivative: 1.
\item
  Sum over the path coordinates: output has \texttt{\{i,\ j\}}, bias has
  \texttt{\{j\}}. Difference: \texttt{\{i\}}. Sum over \texttt{i}.
\end{enumerate}

Result: \texttt{d\_bias{[}j{]}\ =\ sum{[}i{]}(d\_out{[}i,\ j{]})}. The
broadcast coordinate \texttt{i} becomes the reduction coordinate. The
Inversion Rule, mechanically applied.

\textbf{Scenario 2: Reduce to scalar.} Forward:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ total = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(data}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You have \texttt{d\_total} (a scalar). You need \texttt{d\_data{[}i{]}}.
Go through the five steps.

Hint: the output has no coordinates. \texttt{data} has \texttt{\{i\}}.
The path coordinates are\ldots{} the ones in \texttt{data} but not in
the output. Wait---that's backwards. The output is a scalar.
\texttt{data} has \texttt{\{i\}}. The output set minus data set
is\ldots{} negative? No.~The path is from \texttt{data} to
\texttt{total}: \texttt{data} has \texttt{\{i\}}, \texttt{total} has
\texttt{\{\}}. The coordinate \texttt{i} is consumed by the forward
\texttt{sum}. In the backward pass, the consumed coordinate is broadcast
back.

Result: \texttt{d\_data{[}i{]}\ =\ d\_total}. The scalar gradient is
broadcast to every \texttt{i} position. Each element of \texttt{data}
contributed equally to the sum (coefficient 1), so each receives the
full gradient signal.

Two scenarios. Two derivations. Same five steps. No calculus
memorization. The coordinate sets tell you what to sum over. The forward
expression tells you what to multiply by.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{convolution-gradients}{%
\subsection{Convolution Gradients}\label{convolution-gradients}}

A convolution is a sum of products with index arithmetic:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ conv}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, oc, oh, ow}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{ic, kh, kw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    input}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, ic, oh + kh, ow + kw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * weight}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{oc, ic, kh, kw}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The gradient with respect to \texttt{weight} sums over everything that
\texttt{weight} does not own:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ dW}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{oc, ic, kh, kw}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, oh, ow}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    dConv}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, oc, oh, ow}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * input}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, ic, oh + kh, ow + kw}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The coordinates \texttt{b}, \texttt{oh}, \texttt{ow} are summed away
because they appear in the output but not in \texttt{weight}. The
coordinates \texttt{oc}, \texttt{ic}, \texttt{kh}, \texttt{kw} survive
because they \emph{are} \texttt{weight}'s coordinates.

Again: set subtraction. The formula is mechanically derivable from the
coordinate sets. The same five steps produce the weight gradient with
the correct index arithmetic.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-broadcast-self-audit}{%
\subsection{The Broadcast Self-Audit}\label{the-broadcast-self-audit}}

A forward broadcast makes a promise. A backward gradient collects
dependence. If the two disagree, the gradient is silently wrong.

Consider a linear layer with a bias:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ z}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * W}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{out, }\KeywordTok{in}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) + bias}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The bias term omits \texttt{b}---it promises that the bias does not
depend on the batch element. Now compute the gradient:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ d_bias}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(d_loss}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, out}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The gradient sums over \texttt{b}. Why? Because in the forward pass,
\texttt{bias{[}out{]}} was replicated across every batch element. Every
batch element carries a piece of the gradient signal. To update
\texttt{bias}, we must collect all those pieces. The omitted coordinate
becomes the reduced coordinate in the backward pass.

Three questions for any broadcast:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{What coordinate am I broadcasting over?} Is the name visible
  in the code, or is it inferred from position?
\item
  \textbf{Is independence truly justified?} Does the broadcast value
  genuinely not depend on that coordinate?
\item
  \textbf{What will the gradient do?} Does the backward reduction
  produce the right shape for the parameter update?
\end{enumerate}

These three questions are the broadcast self-audit. They catch the class
of bugs where a broadcast is shape-correct but semantically wrong.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{custom-differentiation-with-fn}{%
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Custom Differentiation with
\texttt{@fn}}{Custom Differentiation with @fn}}\label{custom-differentiation-with-fn}}

Some functions have derivatives that are better expressed directly:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ relu(x) }\OperatorTok{\{} \KeywordTok{if}\NormalTok{ x > }\DecValTok{0.0} \OperatorTok{\{}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{\}} \KeywordTok{else} \OperatorTok{\{} \DecValTok{0.0} \OperatorTok{\}} \OperatorTok{\}}

\NormalTok{@fn relu(x) }\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{if}\NormalTok{ x > }\DecValTok{0.0} \OperatorTok{\{}\NormalTok{ @x }\OperatorTok{\}} \KeywordTok{else} \OperatorTok{\{} \DecValTok{0.0} \OperatorTok{\}}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{@fn} declaration shares the function's name and parameter
list. Inside the body, \texttt{@x} refers to the tangent flowing through
parameter \texttt{x}. Custom rules can be coordinate-aware:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{@fn softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    softmax_tangent}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x, @x)}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The coordinate parameter \texttt{j} appears in both the primal function
and its derivative rule. The tangent computation follows the same
coordinate contract as the primal.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{where-clauses-in-the-backward-pass}{%
\subsection{Where Clauses in the Backward
Pass}\label{where-clauses-in-the-backward-pass}}

A where clause acts as a gate in both directions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ pos_sum = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(data}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) }\KeywordTok{where}\NormalTok{ data}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{i}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ > }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In the forward pass, only positive elements are summed. In the backward
pass, the gradient signal is distributed only to the positive elements.
Elements that were filtered out receive zero gradient. You don't write a
separate backward filter. The where clause defines the domain of the
operation, and the domain applies in both directions.

This is the shopping cart model extended: the where clause is the gate
on the aisle door. During shopping (forward pass), only items matching
the condition enter the cart. During restocking (backward pass), only
shelves matching the condition receive replenishment. The gate is the
same in both directions. The condition is written once.

Now a harder one. What if the where clause references a coordinate that
is consumed by the operation?

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ top_sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) }\KeywordTok{where}\NormalTok{ logits}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b, class}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ > threshold}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{b}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The where clause references \texttt{class}---the coordinate being
consumed by \texttt{sum}. The condition
\texttt{logits{[}b,\ class{]}\ \textgreater{}\ threshold{[}b{]}} is a
tensor of shape \texttt{(batch,\ class)}. Each \texttt{(b,\ class)}
position has a boolean gate.

In the backward pass, the gradient signal \texttt{d\_top\_sum{[}b{]}} is
distributed back to \texttt{logits{[}b,\ class{]}}---but only to
positions where the gate was open. For each \texttt{b}, elements of
\texttt{class} that were below \texttt{threshold{[}b{]}} receive zero
gradient. The where clause is evaluated during the forward pass and its
boolean mask is stored. The backward pass reads the mask and gates the
gradient accordingly.

No separate backward code. No custom \texttt{@fn} rule. The where clause
is part of the reduction's domain specification, and the compiler
generates both the forward gating and the backward gating from it. The
coordinate names in the condition---\texttt{class} in this case---tell
the compiler which dimension the gate applies to.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{layernorm-a-complete-gradient-walkthrough}{%
\subsection{LayerNorm: A Complete Gradient
Walkthrough}\label{layernorm-a-complete-gradient-walkthrough}}

The pullback procedure is easiest to learn by example. Let's trace the
gradient of LayerNorm end to end, using nothing but coordinate sets.

Forward pass:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ layer_norm[feature](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                      gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                      beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ mean_val}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - mean_val}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{2.0}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    (centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / (var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)) * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

We have \texttt{d\_out{[}..batch,\ feature{]}}---the gradient of the
loss with respect to the LayerNorm output. We need
\texttt{dx{[}..batch,\ feature{]}}, \texttt{d\_gamma{[}feature{]}}, and
\texttt{d\_beta{[}feature{]}}.

\textbf{Step 1: Gradient of beta.} The output expression ends with
\texttt{+\ beta{[}feature{]}}. This is an elementwise addition.
\texttt{beta} omits \texttt{..batch} in its index pattern---it
broadcasts over all batch dimensions. By the Inversion Rule, the
gradient sums over the broadcast set:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ d_beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(d_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{d\_out} has \texttt{\{..batch,\ feature\}}. \texttt{beta} has
\texttt{\{feature\}}. Difference: \texttt{\{..batch\}}. Sum over it.
Result: \texttt{\{feature\}}, matching \texttt{beta}'s shape.

\textbf{Step 2: Gradient of gamma.} Same pattern.
\texttt{gamma{[}feature{]}} broadcasts over \texttt{..batch}. The
gradient sums over \texttt{..batch}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ d_gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    d_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / (var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The local derivative of \texttt{gamma\ *\ x\_norm} with respect to
\texttt{gamma} is \texttt{x\_norm}. Multiply by the incoming gradient.
Sum over the broadcast set. Done.

\textbf{Step 3: Gradient of x.} This is the complex one---\texttt{x}
contributes to the output through three paths: directly through
\texttt{centered} (which contains \texttt{x}), and indirectly through
\texttt{mean\_val} and \texttt{var} (which were computed from
\texttt{x}). But the coordinate accounting still works: \texttt{x} has
\texttt{\{..batch,\ feature\}}. The output has
\texttt{\{..batch,\ feature\}}. No coordinate difference. The gradient
must have the same shape. No sum---the contributions from all three
paths accumulate at each \texttt{(..batch,\ feature)} position.

Coordinate set subtraction tells you \emph{which} coordinates get
summed. The local computation tells you \emph{what} to sum. Together
they produce the gradient without shape memorization.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-pullback-as-coordinate-set-subtraction-a-summary}{%
\subsection{The Pullback as Coordinate Set Subtraction: A
Summary}\label{the-pullback-as-coordinate-set-subtraction-a-summary}}

Every pullback you have seen follows the same rule. Given a forward
expression and the operand whose gradient you want:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Write the operand's coordinate set. \texttt{A} has \texttt{\{i,\ k\}}.
\item
  Write the output's coordinate set. \texttt{C} has \texttt{\{i,\ j\}}.
\item
  The path coordinates = output set minus operand set. \texttt{\{j\}}.
\item
  Sum over the path coordinates.
  \texttt{dA{[}i,\ k{]}\ =\ sum{[}j{]}(...)}.
\end{enumerate}

The other operand (\texttt{B{[}k,\ j{]}}) provides the missing
coordinate \texttt{k}. The local derivative comes from differentiating
the forward operation with respect to the operand. The path-sum
structure comes from the coordinate sets.

This is the pullback rule as coordinate accounting. No transpose rules
memorized. No Jacobian dimensions counted. Just set subtraction, applied
to coordinate names. The names tell you which coordinates to sum over.
The forward expression tells you what to sum. The coordinate sets bridge
the two.

\vspace{2em}

The gradient is not a separate computation from the forward pass. It is
the forward pass, read backward. The coordinate names that organize the
forward pass---which survive, which are consumed, which are
omitted---organize the backward pass in exactly the same way. A
coordinate eliminated by a forward sum becomes a coordinate introduced
by a backward broadcast. A coordinate omitted by a forward broadcast
becomes a coordinate summed by a backward reduction.

The names are the same. The direction is reversed. The principle is
symmetric.

The shopping cart record, read forward, tells you what you bought. Read
backward, it tells the store what to restock. The names of the items are
on both sides of the receipt.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-gradient-of-a-coordinate-aware-function-call}{%
\subsection{The Gradient of a Coordinate-Aware Function
Call}\label{the-gradient-of-a-coordinate-aware-function-call}}

When a function call appears in the forward pass, its backward pass is
determined by the function's coordinate contract. The gradient does not
need to re-verify the contract---the forward call already did. The
gradient only needs to apply the Inversion Rule to each primitive
operation inside the function body.

Consider \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)}. Forward: the function body
contains \texttt{max{[}class{]}}, \texttt{sum{[}class{]}}, elementwise
operations, and a division. Backward: \texttt{max{[}class{]}} becomes a
broadcast (only the argmax position receives the gradient),
\texttt{sum{[}class{]}} becomes a broadcast (every summed element
receives the gradient), the division's gradient is elementwise.

The coordinate \texttt{class} is the reduction axis in both \texttt{max}
and \texttt{sum}. The backward pass broadcasts over \texttt{class} for
both. The gradient flows through the function's coordinate
parameter---\texttt{class} in, \texttt{class} out. The caller never sees
the internal backward operations. The coordinate contract at the call
site guarantees that \texttt{class} exists on the input, so the gradient
exists on the same coordinate.

A custom \texttt{@fn} rule for softmax overrides this automatic
derivation---but the coordinate parameter in the custom rule must match
the primal. The compiler checks this:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ softmax[j](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]} \OperatorTok{\{}\NormalTok{ ... }\OperatorTok{\}}

\NormalTok{@fn softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{j}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..left, j, ..right}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{\{}
    \CommentTok{// Custom backward: softmax_tangent[j](x, @x)}
    \CommentTok{// j must match the primal's coordinate parameter}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If the custom rule declares \texttt{@fn\ softmax{[}k{]}} (different
coordinate parameter name), the compiler reports a mismatch. If the
custom rule returns a gradient with different coordinates than the
primal's input, the compiler reports a mismatch. The coordinate contract
extends from the forward pass into the backward pass. The same names,
checked in both directions.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Revisit the last gradient you debugged. Write the forward
expression with coordinate names. Derive the backward expression using
coordinate set subtraction. Does the backward sum match the forward
broadcast? The answer to that question is the answer to whether your
gradient was correct.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-derive-a-gradient-by-hand}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: Derive a Gradient by
Hand}\label{stop-and-think-derive-a-gradient-by-hand}}

You now have the five-step pullback procedure. Test it on your own code.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Find a gradient you've written or debugged.} It could be a
  custom backward pass, a \texttt{torch.autograd.Function}, or a manual
  gradient check. Write down the forward expression.
\item
  \textbf{Write it with coordinate names.} Even if the original code is
  in PyTorch, write the Einlang equivalent. Name every coordinate. Write
  the reduction brackets. Write the index patterns.
\item
  \textbf{Derive the gradient using coordinate set subtraction.} Don't
  look at the original backward code. Just use the five steps. What
  coordinates does the output have? What coordinates does the operand
  have? What's the difference? Sum over the difference.
\item
  \textbf{Compare to the original.} Does your derived gradient match
  what you originally wrote? If yes---the coordinate accounting produced
  the same result as your manual derivation. If no---either your
  coordinate accounting was wrong, or your original gradient was. Check
  which.
\item
  \textbf{Repeat for a more complex operation.} Try it on a convolution,
  a normalization, or an attention operation. The five steps are the
  same. The coordinate sets are larger. The procedure doesn't change.
\end{enumerate}

The pullback is not a separate computation from the forward pass. It is
the forward pass, read backward, through the lens of the Inversion Rule.
Every forward reduction becomes a backward broadcast. Every forward
broadcast becomes a backward reduction. The coordinate names are the
bridge between the two directions. The five steps are the procedure for
crossing it.

In the next chapter, we leave the single-language perspective and do
something new. We put Einlang side by side with PyTorch---same
computation, two notations---and ask what the names let you see that
positions hide.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-9-comparison-normalization}{%
\section{Chapter 9 · Comparison:
Normalization}\label{chapter-9-comparison-normalization}}

\begin{quote}
``You don't understand a notation until you've seen what it hides.''

--- The author
\end{quote}

\emph{Comparisons · LayerNorm, RMSNorm, GroupNorm in two notations}

\vspace{2em}

You are two weeks into a Transformer project. LayerNorm is working. You
swap in RMSNorm for the memory-efficient run---same \texttt{dim=-1},
same shape, loss looks fine. Then you try GroupNorm on the convolutional
front-end. \texttt{dim=-1} again. The shapes align. The loss descends.
Three days later you notice the GroupNorm is normalizing over
\texttt{channels\_per\_group} instead of \texttt{spatial}. The
\texttt{dim=-1} that was \texttt{feature} in LayerNorm became
\texttt{channel-group-index} in GroupNorm, silently.

Each normalization normalizes over different coordinates. Each uses a
position number to say which one. Switch from one to another, and every
\texttt{dim} must be audited---because \texttt{dim=-1} means
\texttt{feature} in LayerNorm, \texttt{channel} in GroupNorm, and
nothing at all in RMSNorm.

Every chapter so far has been in Einlang. We built the primitives,
composed them into functions, traced their gradients, and gave the
language a name.

This chapter changes the lens. We take three real normalization
functions---LayerNorm, RMSNorm, GroupNorm---and write each in both
PyTorch and Einlang, side by side. The question is not ``which is
better.'' The question is: \textbf{what does each notation make visible,
and what does each notation hide?}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-normalization-skeleton}{%
\subsection{The Normalization
Skeleton}\label{the-normalization-skeleton}}

Every normalization follows the same three-step skeleton:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Reduce} to get statistics (mean, variance, max).
\item
  \textbf{Broadcast} the statistics back to the original shape.
\item
  \textbf{Apply} elementwise (subtract, divide, scale, shift).
\end{enumerate}

The difference between LayerNorm, RMSNorm, and GroupNorm is only
\emph{which coordinates} are reduced over and \emph{which coordinates}
the broadcast parameters align with. In a positional notation, these
differences are buried in \texttt{dim} arguments and reshape chains. In
a named notation, they are visible in the coordinate names.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{layernorm}{%
\subsection{LayerNorm}\label{layernorm}}

Given an input of shape \texttt{(batch,\ seq,\ feature)}, LayerNorm
normalizes across the \texttt{feature} dimension for each
\texttt{(batch,\ seq)} position independently.

\textbf{PyTorch:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ LayerNorm(nn.Module):}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, normalized_shape, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
        \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{()}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.ones(normalized_shape))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(normalized_shape))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x):}
\NormalTok{        mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.var(dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, unbiased}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ torch.sqrt(var }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps) }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{dim=-1} is correct as long as \texttt{feature} is the last
dimension. It always is---until a refactoring makes it not.
\texttt{keepdim=True} is needed so the broadcast aligns; forgetting it
produces a silent shape mismatch in the gradient.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ layer_norm[feature](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{2.0}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    (centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / (var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)) * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

What the Einlang version makes visible: - \texttt{mean{[}feature{]}}
says ``I am reducing over \texttt{feature}.'' The name is in the
bracket. - \texttt{mean{[}..batch{]}} says ``\texttt{mean} only has
batch dimensions.'' The broadcast over \texttt{feature} is explicit in
the subtraction
\texttt{x{[}..batch,\ feature{]}\ -\ mean{[}..batch{]}}---\texttt{mean}
omits \texttt{feature}, so it broadcasts along it. -
\texttt{gamma{[}feature{]}} says ``gamma aligns with the feature
dimension.'' The pack \texttt{..batch} absorbs whatever batch structure
exists.

If the input changes from \texttt{(batch,\ seq,\ feature)} to
\texttt{(batch,\ feature,\ seq)}, the Einlang code still
works---\texttt{..batch} now absorbs \texttt{(batch,)} and
\texttt{feature} is at position 1 instead of 2. The PyTorch code
silently normalizes over \texttt{seq} instead of \texttt{feature}.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{rmsnorm}{%
\subsection{RMSNorm}\label{rmsnorm}}

RMSNorm is simpler than LayerNorm: no mean subtraction, just scaling by
the root-mean-square.

\textbf{PyTorch:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ RMSNorm(nn.Module):}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, normalized_shape, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
        \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{()}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.ones(normalized_shape))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x):}
\NormalTok{        rms }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.sqrt(x.}\BuiltInTok{pow}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ rms }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ rms_norm[feature](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ sq}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{2.0}\NormalTok{;}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ ms}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(sq}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / (ms}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{) * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The skeleton is identical to LayerNorm---reduce over \texttt{feature},
broadcast back along it, apply elementwise. The difference is only
\emph{which statistics} are computed. In PyTorch, LayerNorm and RMSNorm
are different classes with different internal logic but identical
\texttt{dim=-1} interfaces. The fact that they share a skeleton is
invisible in the code. In Einlang, you can overlay the two functions and
see that only the body differs---the coordinate contract is the same.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{groupnorm}{%
\subsection{GroupNorm}\label{groupnorm}}

GroupNorm divides channels into groups and normalizes within each group.
This requires splitting the \texttt{channel} dimension into
\texttt{(group,\ channel\_per\_group)} and reducing over both
\texttt{channel\_per\_group} and the spatial dimensions.

\textbf{PyTorch:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ GroupNorm(nn.Module):}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, num_groups, num_channels, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
        \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{()}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num_groups }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ num_groups}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.ones(num_channels))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(num_channels))}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x):}
\NormalTok{        N, C, H, W }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{        G }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.num_groups}
\NormalTok{        x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, G, C }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ G, H, W)}
\NormalTok{        mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.var(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, unbiased}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ torch.sqrt(var }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps)}
\NormalTok{        x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, C, H, W)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma.view(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta.view(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The reshape-permute-reshape dance is the positional price of grouping.
\texttt{dim=(2,\ 3,\ 4)} means ``reduce over channel\_per\_group,
height, and width''---but those positions are only correct after the
reshape. The reader must mentally compile the grouping semantics from
the reshape chain: \texttt{reshape} splits channels into groups,
\texttt{mean(dim=(2,3,4))} reduces within each group, \texttt{reshape}
merges them back. The grouping is a manual compilation step, performed
by the programmer, invisible in the source.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ group_norm[group, c_in_group, ..spatial](}
\NormalTok{    x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, group, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; group, c_in_group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; group, c_in_group}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, group, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{    );}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{2.0}
\NormalTok{    );}
\NormalTok{    (centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{        / (var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{        * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{group, c_in_group}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{group, c_in_group}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

What the Einlang version makes visible: -
\texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}} names exactly which
coordinates are being reduced. No \texttt{dim=(2,\ 3,\ 4)} whose meaning
depends on a reshape. - \texttt{gamma{[}group,\ c\_in\_group{]}} aligns
with two coordinates. No \texttt{.view(1,\ -1,\ 1,\ 1)} to manually
position the broadcast. - No reshape is needed because the coordinates
\texttt{group} and \texttt{c\_in\_group} are separate from the start.
The function signature declares the grouped layout directly.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{instancenorm-the-fourth-variant}{%
\subsection{InstanceNorm: The Fourth
Variant}\label{instancenorm-the-fourth-variant}}

LayerNorm normalizes over \texttt{feature}. GroupNorm normalizes over
\texttt{c\_in\_group\ +\ spatial}. InstanceNorm normalizes over
\texttt{spatial} alone---one statistic per channel per sample. It is
used in style transfer, where the ``style'' of an image is captured by
per-channel statistics.

\textbf{PyTorch:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ InstanceNorm(nn.Module):}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, num_features, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{):}
        \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{()}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.ones(num_features))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(num_features))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x):}
        \CommentTok{# x: (N, C, H, W)}
\NormalTok{        mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.var(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, unbiased}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ torch.sqrt(var }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps) }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma.view(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta.view(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{dim=(2,\ 3)} means ``reduce over H and W.'' But how do you know
H and W are at positions 2 and 3? Because the comment says
\texttt{\#\ x:\ (N,\ C,\ H,\ W)}. If the input has shape
\texttt{(N,\ C,\ L)} for 1D, \texttt{dim=(2,)}. If it has shape
\texttt{(N,\ T,\ C,\ H,\ W)} for video, \texttt{dim=(3,\ 4)}. The
\texttt{dim} tuple is a function of the input layout. Change the layout,
and every normalization call must be audited.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ instance_norm[..spatial, channel](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, channel, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; channel}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; channel}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, channel, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{2.0}
\NormalTok{    );}
\NormalTok{    (centered}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ / (var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, channel}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{        * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{channel}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{channel}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{..spatial} absorbs however many spatial dimensions there
