are---1, 2, 3. The reduction bracket \texttt{mean{[}..spatial{]}}
doesn't change. The skeleton is the same as LayerNorm, RMSNorm, and
GroupNorm. Only the coordinate in the bracket differs.

Now overlay all four normalization functions. The differences are
exactly which coordinates appear in the reduction bracket:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Function & Reduction bracket & Broadcast params\tabularnewline
\midrule
\endhead
LayerNorm & \texttt{mean{[}feature{]}} &
\texttt{gamma{[}feature{]}}\tabularnewline
RMSNorm & \texttt{mean{[}feature{]}} &
\texttt{gamma{[}feature{]}}\tabularnewline
InstanceNorm & \texttt{mean{[}..spatial{]}} &
\texttt{gamma{[}channel{]}}\tabularnewline
GroupNorm & \texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}} &
\texttt{gamma{[}group,\ c\_in\_group{]}}\tabularnewline
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

The body of every function is: reduce to get statistics,
subtract-and-divide, scale-and-shift. The reduction bracket is the only
structural difference. In the PyTorch versions, this unity is
invisible---each function has its own \texttt{dim} argument, its own
view-reshape chain, its own parameter shape conventions. The skeleton is
scattered across four classes.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{tracing-a-reshape-bug}{%
\subsection{Tracing a Reshape Bug}\label{tracing-a-reshape-bug}}

Let's trace what happens when a refactoring breaks the positional
GroupNorm. This is not hypothetical. Here is the original code again:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Original: input (N, C, H, W)}
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, num_groups, C }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num_groups, H, W)}
\NormalTok{mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

A colleague adds a temporal dimension for video processing. The input
becomes \texttt{(N,\ T,\ C,\ H,\ W)}. They update the reshape:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Updated: input (N, T, C, H, W)}
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, T, num_groups, C }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num_groups, H, W)}
\NormalTok{mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{# bug? or correct?}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Wait---which dimension is \texttt{T}? The original
\texttt{dim=(2,\ 3,\ 4)} mapped to \texttt{(C//num\_groups,\ H,\ W)}.
After adding \texttt{T} at position 1, the reshape puts \texttt{T} at
position 1, shifting everything: position 2 is now \texttt{num\_groups}
(not \texttt{C//num\_groups}), position 3 is \texttt{C//num\_groups},
position 4 is \texttt{H}, position 5 is \texttt{W}. The tuple should be
\texttt{(3,\ 4,\ 5)}.

But the colleague wrote \texttt{(2,\ 3,\ 4,\ 5)}. Position 2 includes
\texttt{num\_groups}. The normalization now normalizes across
\emph{different groups}, collapsing the very distinction GroupNorm
exists to preserve. The code runs. The shapes match. The loss
descends---slightly worse, but within training noise. The model ships.
Six months later, someone notices the segmentation masks are blurrier
than expected.

Now replay the same refactoring in Einlang:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Original: input [N, T, C, H, W]}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ group_norm[group, c_in_group, ..spatial](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, group, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, ...)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{..spatial} absorbs \texttt{(H,\ W)}. Adding \texttt{T} changes
nothing---\texttt{..batch} absorbs it, or if \texttt{T} should be
preserved independently, it's added as a separate coordinate. The
reduction bracket \texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}} stays the
same. No positions to shift. No tuples to renumber.

The difference is not that Einlang programmers are better at arithmetic.
The difference is that the Einlang notation doesn't \emph{require}
positional arithmetic. The coordinate names abstract over positions, so
layout changes don't cascade through every reduction bracket in the
codebase.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-the-comparison-reveals}{%
\subsection{What the Comparison
Reveals}\label{what-the-comparison-reveals}}

The three PyTorch implementations are correct. They run. They're fast.
The problem is not that they don't work---it's that the coordinate story
is stored in the programmer's head, not in the code.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{dim=-1} in LayerNorm means \texttt{feature}. Until it doesn't.
\item
  \texttt{dim=(2,\ 3,\ 4)} in GroupNorm means
  \texttt{c\_in\_group,\ H,\ W}. But only after the reshape.
\item
  \texttt{.view(1,\ -1,\ 1,\ 1)} means ``gamma is silent on batch,
  height, and width.'' The code doesn't say that. You deduce it from the
  shape.
\item
  \texttt{keepdim=True} means ``I need this for broadcasting later.''
  The forward intent and backward consequence are connected only by
  convention.
\end{itemize}

In the Einlang versions, these facts are in the brackets. The coordinate
that is reduced is named. The coordinates that are broadcast are visible
by their absence. The skeleton---reduce, broadcast, apply---is the same
across all three functions, and the similarity is visible in the code.

Now pause and run a thought experiment. If you change the number of
groups in the PyTorch GroupNorm from 4 to 8, which lines change? Walk
through the code in your head. The \texttt{reshape} line
changes---\texttt{C\ //\ num\_groups} is now different. The
\texttt{dim=(2,\ 3,\ 4)} tuple stays the same---but only because the
reshape preserved the relative positions. If you had added a temporal
dimension before the spatial ones, the tuple would need to shift. Which
line is most likely to be forgotten in that refactoring?

In the Einlang version, changing the group count changes nothing in the
code---the coordinate \texttt{group} just has a larger domain. Adding a
temporal dimension is absorbed by \texttt{..spatial}. The reduction
bracket doesn't change. The broadcast parameters don't change. The
skeleton is stable under layout changes because the coordinate names
abstract over positions.

This is not a hypothetical. Every piece of production normalization code
has been refactored at least once. The question is whether the notation
made the refactoring safe or silent.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{batchnorm-where-the-skeleton-breaks}{%
\subsection{BatchNorm: Where the Skeleton
Breaks}\label{batchnorm-where-the-skeleton-breaks}}

BatchNorm normalizes over the batch dimension, not the feature
dimension. In training, it computes per-feature statistics across the
batch. In inference, it uses running averages. This introduces a
complication that none of the previous normalizations had: the
normalization depends on the data distribution, and the data
distribution changes between training and inference.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# PyTorch BatchNorm}
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ BatchNorm(nn.Module):}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, num_features, eps}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1e-5}\NormalTok{, momentum}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{):}
        \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{__init__}\NormalTok{()}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.ones(num_features))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(num_features))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.register_buffer(}\StringTok{'running_mean'}\NormalTok{, torch.zeros(num_features))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.register_buffer(}\StringTok{'running_var'}\NormalTok{, torch.ones(num_features))}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eps}
        \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.momentum }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ momentum}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ forward(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, x):}
        \ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.training:}
\NormalTok{            mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{# over batch}
\NormalTok{            var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.var(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, unbiased}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}
            \CommentTok{# update running stats...}
        \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            mean }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.running_mean}
\NormalTok{            var }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.running_var}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ (x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ torch.sqrt(var }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.eps) }\OperatorTok{*} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.gamma }\OperatorTok{+} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.beta}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The coordinate story: \texttt{dim=0} reduces over \texttt{batch}. The
running statistics are shape \texttt{(feature,)}. In inference, they
broadcast over the batch. The code works. But the distinction between
``statistics computed from the current batch'' (training) and
``statistics accumulated from many batches'' (inference) is captured in
an \texttt{if\ self.training} branch and a \texttt{register\_buffer}
call. The coordinate structure is identical in both modes. The semantic
difference---fresh vs.~accumulated statistics---is a runtime flag, not a
structural distinction.

In Einlang, the training/inference distinction could be captured in the
type system by whether the batch statistic carries a time coordinate:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ batch_norm[feature, ..batch](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    gamma: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, beta: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    running_mean: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, running_var: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \CommentTok{// Training: compute fresh statistics from current batch}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ batch_mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ batch_var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{        (x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - batch_mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) ** }\DecValTok{2.0}
\NormalTok{    );}
    \CommentTok{// Training path}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ train_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        (x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - batch_mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        / (batch_var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    \CommentTok{// Inference path}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ infer_out}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        (x}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ - running_mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        / (running_var}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ + }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{e-}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        * gamma}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ + beta}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{feature}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{;}
    \CommentTok{// ...}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Both paths reduce over \texttt{..batch} to produce
\texttt{\{feature\}}-shaped statistics. The difference is whether those
statistics came from the current tensor or from a running accumulator.
The reduction bracket is the same. The source of the statistics is
different. The naming makes explicit what \texttt{self.training} hides:
in training, \texttt{batch\_mean} is derived from \texttt{x} (creating a
data dependency for the backward pass); in inference,
\texttt{running\_mean} is a constant (no gradient flows through it).

This is the boundary where the coordinate notation meets the runtime.
The reduction bracket names what is consumed. It does not name whether
the statistics are fresh or accumulated. That distinction lives in the
data dependency graph, not in the coordinate structure. The coordinate
skeleton can only carry so much. The rest is in the code's semantics.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Open your LayerNorm or RMSNorm implementation. Find
\texttt{dim=-1}. Ask: is \texttt{-1} still the feature dimension? How do
you know? The answer should be in the code, not in your memory of what
the tensor shape was when you wrote it.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-audit-your-normalization}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: Audit Your
Normalization}\label{stop-and-think-audit-your-normalization}}

Find the normalization code in your current project. It might be a
\texttt{LayerNorm} call, an \texttt{RMSNorm}, a \texttt{GroupNorm}, or a
custom \texttt{(x\ -\ mean)\ /\ std\ *\ gamma\ +\ beta}. For each one:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{What coordinate does the reduction consume?} Not ``which
  position'' --- which coordinate. If the code says
  \texttt{x.mean(dim=-1)}, the answer depends on what's at position -1.
  Trace it back to the data loader or the declaration. Write the
  coordinate name down.
\item
  \textbf{What would break if the dimension order changed?} If someone
  added a \texttt{head} dimension or a \texttt{time} dimension, would
  the reduction still consume the right coordinate? For LayerNorm with
  \texttt{dim=-1}, the answer might be ``yes, feature is conventionally
  last.'' For GroupNorm with \texttt{dim=(2,3,4)}, the answer is almost
  certainly ``no --- those positions correspond to specific dimensions
  in the reshape chain.''
\item
  \textbf{Is the reduction semantically correct?} LayerNorm normalizes
  over \texttt{feature}. GroupNorm normalizes over \texttt{c\_in\_group}
  and \texttt{..spatial}. Are you normalizing over the coordinates you
  intend to normalize over? If the answer requires running the code to
  check shapes, the notation isn't carrying the intent.
\end{enumerate}

Now imagine each normalization written with coordinate names.
\texttt{mean{[}feature{]}(x)} instead of \texttt{x.mean(dim=-1)}.
\texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}(x)} instead of
\texttt{x.reshape(...).mean(dim=(2,3,4))}. For each one, the coordinate
name answers question 1. The name's independence from position answers
question 2. The semantic meaning of the name answers question 3.

The normalization audit is a specialization of the broadcast self-audit
from Chapter 4. Every normalization is a reduce-broadcast-elementwise
pattern. The reduction bracket names the consumed coordinates. The
broadcast parameters name the alignment. The skeleton is the same. The
names change. The audit is the same. The questions change.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{tracing-a-reshape-bug-1}{%
\subsection{Tracing a Reshape Bug}\label{tracing-a-reshape-bug-1}}

Let's trace a reshape bug through its entire life, in both notations.
This is the bug that the coordinate audit is designed to catch before it
reaches production.

\textbf{Day 0.} A programmer writes GroupNorm for 4D input
\texttt{(N,\ C,\ H,\ W)}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ group_norm(x, num_groups, gamma, beta):}
\NormalTok{    N, C, H, W }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{    x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, num_groups, C }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num_groups, H, W)}
\NormalTok{    mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    var }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.var(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ ((x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ mean) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (var }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps).sqrt()).reshape(N, C, H, W) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ gamma }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{dim=(2,3,4)} tuple means: normalizing over
\texttt{C//num\_groups}, \texttt{H}, and \texttt{W}. The programmer
knows this because they can count: dimension 2 is
\texttt{C//num\_groups}, dimension 3 is \texttt{H}, dimension 4 is
\texttt{W}. The code is correct.

\textbf{Day 60.} A colleague adds temporal dimension for video input.
The tensor is now \texttt{(N,\ T,\ C,\ H,\ W)}. The colleague updates
the reshape:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{N, T, C, H, W }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.shape}
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, T, num_groups, C }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num_groups, H, W)}
\NormalTok{mean }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), keepdim}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{# }\AlertTok{BUG}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{dim=(2,3,4)} now means: normalizing over \texttt{num\_groups},
\texttt{C//num\_groups}, and \texttt{H}. Wait --- \texttt{num\_groups}
at position 2, \texttt{C//num\_groups} at position 3, \texttt{H} at
position 4. But \texttt{W} is at position 5. And \texttt{T} is at
position 1. The tuple \texttt{(2,3,4)} needs to be \texttt{(3,4,5)}. The
programmer forgets. The code runs. The shapes match because
\texttt{keepdim=True} preserves the reduced dimensions. The bug is:
GroupNorm is now normalizing over
\texttt{(num\_groups,\ c\_in\_group,\ H)} instead of
\texttt{(c\_in\_group,\ H,\ W)}. Width is not normalized. Group is
normalized --- collapsing the grouped structure.

The loss still descends. The model still produces video outputs. But the
normalization is wrong. The bug will surface as ``the model performs
worse on wide videos.''

Now replay in Einlang:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Original}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ group_norm[g, c_in_group, ..spatial](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, ...)}

\CommentTok{// With temporal dimension added — same signature}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ group_norm[g, c_in_group, ..spatial](x: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, t, g, c_in_group, ..spatial}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, ...)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The signature absorbs \texttt{t} into \texttt{..batch} (if it's a
leading dimension) or \texttt{..spatial} (if temporal is treated as
spatial). The reduction bracket
\texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}} doesn't change. The
coordinates being reduced are still named \texttt{c\_in\_group} and
\texttt{..spatial}. The position of those coordinates in the tensor
layout doesn't matter --- the names find them.

The bug doesn't happen. Not because the programmer is smarter. Because
the notation doesn't require positional arithmetic. The coordinate names
abstract over positions. Adding a dimension changes which positions the
coordinates map to, but the reduction bracket still names the same
coordinates. No \texttt{dim} tuple to update. No reshape chain to
re-align.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-coordinate-audit-for-normalization}{%
\subsection{The Coordinate Audit for
Normalization}\label{the-coordinate-audit-for-normalization}}

Every normalization function can be audited with three questions. They
are the same questions as the broadcast self-audit from Chapter 4,
specialized for normalization's three-step skeleton:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinates does the reduction consume?} In
  \texttt{mean{[}feature{]}}, the consumed coordinate is
  \texttt{feature}. In \texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}}, the
  consumed coordinates are \texttt{c\_in\_group} and all spatial
  dimensions. The reduction bracket names them directly. In a positional
  \texttt{dim=-1} or \texttt{dim=(2,3,4)}, the consumed coordinates must
  be inferred from the layout and the reshape chain.
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinates do the broadcast parameters align with?} In
  \texttt{gamma{[}feature{]}}, gamma aligns with \texttt{feature}---the
  same coordinate that was consumed by the reduction. This is the
  Inversion Rule from Chapter 4: the reduction consumes
  \texttt{feature}, then \texttt{gamma} broadcasts back along
  \texttt{feature}. In a positional \texttt{.view(1,\ -1,\ 1,\ 1)}, the
  alignment is encoded in the view shape, which must be reconstructed by
  the reader.
\item
  \textbf{Does the normalization axis change meaning if the layout
  changes?} If the input changes from \texttt{(batch,\ feature)} to
  \texttt{(batch,\ time,\ feature)}, does \texttt{dim=-1} still mean
  \texttt{feature}? In LayerNorm, yes---\texttt{feature} is
  conventionally the last axis. In GroupNorm after a reshape, no---the
  positions shift and \texttt{dim} must be updated. The Einlang versions
  are stable under layout changes because the coordinate names don't
  change, only the positions they map to.
\end{enumerate}

Three questions. One answer format: the coordinate names in the
brackets. If the brackets have names, the answers are in the code. If
the brackets have numbers, the answers are in your head.

The next chapter compares attention mechanisms---where the gap between
what the code says and what the code means grows wider still.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-10-comparison-attention}{%
\section{Chapter 10 · Comparison:
Attention}\label{chapter-10-comparison-attention}}

\begin{quote}
``The attention mechanism is the only thing standing between your model
and the void. You should probably know which coordinates it's attending
over.''

--- A reviewer
\end{quote}

\emph{Comparisons · Self-attention, cross-attention, and multi-query
attention in two notations}

\vspace{2em}

You are writing an encoder-decoder model. During development, source and
target happen to have the same sequence length---64 tokens.
Self-attention works. Cross-attention works. The code for both is a
single positional function: \texttt{attention(Q,\ K,\ V,\ mask)}. The
shapes match. You ship.

Six weeks later, a configuration change sets source length to 128,
target to 64. The code still runs---same shapes at the
\texttt{attention} call, broadcasting absorbs the mismatch. But your
model now attends from every target position to every source position
\emph{twice}, silently. The BLEU score drops two points. You spend three
days tracing the drop to a transposed mask that was broadcasting along
the wrong axis. The Square Matrix Test, first encountered with softmax
in Chapter 3, returns with a vengeance.

Three numbers govern attention: the number of query heads, the number of
key-value heads, and the sequence length. When any two coincide,
positional code for one variant becomes textually identical to
positional code for another. Only the coordinate names distinguish them.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{scaled-dot-product-attention-the-skeleton}{%
\subsection{Scaled Dot-Product Attention: The
Skeleton}\label{scaled-dot-product-attention-the-skeleton}}

The core operation:

\[\text{Attention}(Q, K, V) = \text{softmax}\left(\frac{QK^T}{\sqrt{d}}\right)V\]

In Einlang, the coordinate names tell the story:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ attention[seq_q, seq_k, head, d](}
\NormalTok{    Q: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    K: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    V: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..batch, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scores}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(Q}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * K}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) / (d ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(scores}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * V}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Three coordinates do all the work: \texttt{seq\_q} (which queries we're
attending \emph{from}), \texttt{seq\_k} (which keys we're attending
\emph{to}), and \texttt{d} (the inner dimension that gets contracted).
\texttt{softmax{[}seq\_k{]}} normalizes over the key sequence---each
query position produces a distribution over key positions.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{self-attention-vs.cross-attention}{%
\subsection{Self-Attention
vs.~Cross-Attention}\label{self-attention-vs.cross-attention}}

Self-attention uses the same sequence for queries and keys.
Cross-attention uses different sequences---queries from the decoder,
keys from the encoder.

\textbf{PyTorch (self-attention):}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ self_attention(Q, K, V):}
\NormalTok{    d }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Q.shape[}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    scores }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(Q, K.transpose(}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (d }\OperatorTok{**} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    weights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.softmax(scores, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(weights, V)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{PyTorch (cross-attention):}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ cross_attention(Q, K, V):}
\NormalTok{    d }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Q.shape[}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    scores }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(Q, K.transpose(}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (d }\OperatorTok{**} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    weights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.softmax(scores, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(weights, V)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

They are identical. Stop and look at that. Two pieces of
code---self-attention and cross-attention---with different semantics,
different gradient behavior, different architectural implications.
Textually identical.

Where is the difference? In the code? No---the code for both is
\texttt{torch.matmul(Q,\ K.transpose(-2,\ -1))}. In the tensor shapes at
runtime? Yes---\texttt{seq\_q} equals \texttt{seq\_k} in one case and
differs in the other. But shapes are runtime values, not source-level
facts. The notation tells you nothing about which case you're in. You
must trace the shapes backward through the forward pass to know---and
after a refactoring, you must do it again.

The code does not distinguish between self-attention and cross-attention
because the distinction---whether \texttt{seq\_q\ ==\ seq\_k}---is not
recorded anywhere in the source. It is recorded in the shapes of the
tensors passed at runtime.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{// Self-attention: same coordinate for queries and keys}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ self_attention[seq, head, d](Q: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, K: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, V: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head, seq, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    attention}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_q=seq, seq_k=seq, head, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(Q, K, V)}
\OperatorTok{\}}

\CommentTok{// Cross-attention: different coordinates for queries and keys}
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ cross_attention[seq_q, seq_k, head, d](Q: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, K: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{, V: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    attention}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_q, seq_k, head, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(Q, K, V)}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The distinction is in the type signatures. Self-attention uses
\texttt{seq} for both queries and keys. Cross-attention uses
\texttt{seq\_q} and \texttt{seq\_k}---two different coordinate names,
potentially with different domain sizes. A reader can tell which is
which without checking whether the tensors happen to have the same
shape.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-square-matrix-test-for-attention}{%
\subsection{The Square Matrix Test for
Attention}\label{the-square-matrix-test-for-attention}}

When \texttt{seq\_q\ ==\ seq\_k} and
\texttt{head\ ==\ some\_other\_dimension}, the attention matrix is
square. The positional code for self-attention, cross-attention, and a
transposed variant are numerically identical. Consider this bug:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Intended: cross-attention from decoder (seq_len=32) to encoder (seq_len=100)}
\CommentTok{# Bug: accidentally used the same tensor for Q and K}
\NormalTok{Q }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ decoder_hidden   }\CommentTok{# shape (batch, head, 32, d)}
\NormalTok{K }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ decoder_hidden   }\CommentTok{# bug: should be encoder_hidden, shape (batch, head, 100, d)}
\NormalTok{V }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ decoder_hidden}
\NormalTok{output }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ cross_attention(Q, K, V)  }\CommentTok{# silently becomes self-attention}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If \texttt{decoder\_hidden} and \texttt{encoder\_hidden} happen to have
the same sequence length during development (both 32, or both padded to
the same length), this bug is invisible. The shapes match. The loss
descends. The model learns---just not what you intended.

In Einlang,
\texttt{cross\_attention{[}seq\_q,\ seq\_k,\ ...{]}(Q,\ K,\ V)} with
\texttt{Q} and \texttt{K} both bound to \texttt{decoder\_hidden} would
trigger a coordinate mismatch if \texttt{decoder\_hidden} has
\texttt{seq\_q} but not \texttt{seq\_k} as its declared coordinate. If
both tensors carry both coordinates (because they were declared with
different names), the mismatch is caught at the call site.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{multi-query-attention-mqa}{%
\subsection{Multi-Query Attention
(MQA)}\label{multi-query-attention-mqa}}

MQA uses multiple query heads but only one key-value head, broadcasting
the KV head across query heads. This is a performance optimization that
changes the coordinate structure:

\textbf{PyTorch:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ mqa_attention(Q, K, V):}
    \CommentTok{# Q: (batch, head_q, seq_q, d)}
    \CommentTok{# K: (batch, 1, seq_k, d)      -- single KV head}
    \CommentTok{# V: (batch, 1, seq_k, d)}
\NormalTok{    d }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Q.shape[}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    scores }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(Q, K.transpose(}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (d }\OperatorTok{**} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    weights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.softmax(scores, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(weights, V)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The code is identical to standard attention. The only difference is that
\texttt{K} has shape \texttt{(batch,\ 1,\ seq\_k,\ d)} instead of
\texttt{(batch,\ head,\ seq\_k,\ d)}. The \texttt{1} broadcasts silently
over all query heads. If someone changes the KV projection to output
\texttt{head\_kv} heads instead of \texttt{1}, the code still runs---it
just produces a different attention pattern. The \texttt{1} is a
positional convention, not a checked fact.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ mqa_attention[head_q, head_kv, seq_q, seq_k, d](}
\NormalTok{    Q: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head_q, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    K: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head_kv, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    V: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head_kv, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head_q, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scores}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_q, head_kv, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(Q}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_q, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * K}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_kv, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) / (d ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scores_merged}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_q, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = mean}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head_kv}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(scores}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_q, head_kv, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_q, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(scores_merged}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_q, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_q, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ * V}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_kv, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{head\_q} and \texttt{head\_kv} are different coordinates. The
function signature declares that queries have \texttt{head\_q} heads and
keys have \texttt{head\_kv} heads. When called as MQA, \texttt{head\_kv}
has size 1---but it's a named coordinate, not a silent \texttt{1} buried
in the shape. If a refactoring changes the KV head count, the coordinate
name \texttt{head\_kv} remains, and it is verified.

The key insight: \texttt{head\_kv} is a coordinate whose domain happens
to be size 1 in the MQA case. It is not a broadcasting hack. It is a
structural fact, visible in the type.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{grouped-query-attention-gqa-the-middle-ground}{%
\subsection{Grouped-Query Attention (GQA): The Middle
Ground}\label{grouped-query-attention-gqa-the-middle-ground}}

Between MHA (\texttt{head\_q\ ==\ head\_kv}) and MQA
(\texttt{head\_kv\ ==\ 1}) lies GQA: \texttt{head\_kv} is a small
number, say 4, that divides \texttt{head\_q}. Each KV head is shared by
a group of query heads. This is a coordinate \emph{grouping}
problem---structurally identical to GroupNorm from Chapter 8.

\textbf{PyTorch (GQA):}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ gqa_attention(Q, K, V, num_kv_heads):}
    \CommentTok{# Q: (batch, head_q, seq_q, d)}
    \CommentTok{# K: (batch, num_kv_heads, seq_k, d)}
    \CommentTok{# V: (batch, num_kv_heads, seq_k, d)}
\NormalTok{    head_q }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Q.shape[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
    \CommentTok{# Repeat KV heads: (batch, num_kv_heads, seq_k, d) → (batch, head_q, seq_k, d)}
\NormalTok{    repeat_factor }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ head_q }\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{ num_kv_heads}
\NormalTok{    K }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ K.unsqueeze(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).expand(}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, repeat_factor, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{).reshape(Q.shape)}
\NormalTok{    V }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ V.unsqueeze(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{).expand(}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, repeat_factor, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{).reshape(Q.shape)}
    \CommentTok{# ... identical to standard attention from here}
\NormalTok{    d }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Q.shape[}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    scores }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(Q, K.transpose(}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (d }\OperatorTok{**} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    weights }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.softmax(scores, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ torch.matmul(weights, V)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The grouping logic---unsqueeze, expand, reshape---is spread across two
lines. The fact that \texttt{head\_q} is grouped into
\texttt{(num\_kv\_heads,\ repeat\_factor)} is encoded in a reshape chain
that the reader must reverse-engineer. If the grouping factor changes,
the reshape must be updated. If the layout changes (e.g.,
\texttt{head\_q} moves from position 1 to position 2), the unsqueeze and
expand must be re-aligned.

\textbf{Einlang (GQA):}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{fn}\NormalTok{ gqa_attention[head_group, head_kv, seq_q, seq_k, d](}
\NormalTok{    Q: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head_group, head_kv, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    K: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head_kv, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    V: }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head_kv, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{) -> }\OperatorTok{[}\DataTypeTok{f32}\NormalTok{; ..b, head_group, head_kv, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\OperatorTok{\{}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ scores}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_group, head_kv, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(Q}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_group, head_kv, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{             * K}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_kv, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{) / (d ** }\DecValTok{0.5}\NormalTok{);}
    \KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_group, head_kv, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ =}
\NormalTok{        softmax}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(scores}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_group, head_kv, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{);}
\NormalTok{    sum}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(weights}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_group, head_kv, seq_q, seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{             * V}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head_kv, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{head\_group} and \texttt{head\_kv} are separate coordinates from
the start. No reshape. No expand. No unsqueeze. \texttt{K} and
\texttt{V} are indexed by \texttt{head\_kv} alone---they broadcast over
\texttt{head\_group} because they omit it. The broadcast is visible in
the indexing pattern: \texttt{K{[}..b,\ head\_kv,\ seq\_k,\ d{]}} has no
\texttt{head\_group}, while \texttt{Q} has both.

Now compare the three variants side by side:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
Variant & Query heads & KV heads & Coordinate structure\tabularnewline
\midrule
\endhead
MHA & \texttt{head} & \texttt{head} & \texttt{head} shared by Q, K,
V\tabularnewline
GQA & \texttt{head\_group\ ×\ head\_kv} & \texttt{head\_kv} &
\texttt{head\_kv} shared; \texttt{head\_group} only on Q\tabularnewline
MQA & \texttt{head\_q} & \texttt{head\_kv} (size 1) & \texttt{head\_kv}
on K, V; \texttt{head\_q} only on Q\tabularnewline
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

In the Einlang signatures, the difference between the three variants is
visible in which coordinates appear on which parameters. In the PyTorch
implementations, the difference is buried in reshape chains and the
value of \texttt{num\_kv\_heads}. The coordinate names make the
architecture visible. The positional code makes it deducible---after
counting dimensions and tracing reshapes.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-attention-coordinate-audit}{%
\subsection{The Attention Coordinate
Audit}\label{the-attention-coordinate-audit}}

Every attention variant can be audited with four questions. Ask them of
any positional attention code you encounter:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinate does \texttt{softmax} normalize over?} In
  \texttt{softmax(scores,\ dim=-1)}, the answer is ``whatever is last.''
  In \texttt{softmax{[}seq\_k{]}(scores)}, the answer is
  \texttt{seq\_k}.
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinate distinguishes queries from keys?} In MHA,
  it's the same (\texttt{seq}). In cross-attention, it's different
  (\texttt{seq\_q} vs \texttt{seq\_k}). In positional code, this
  distinction is in the tensor shapes at runtime. In named code, it's in
  the function signature.
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinate groups query heads with KV heads?} In GQA,
  \texttt{head\_group} groups query heads over a shared KV head. In MQA,
  \texttt{head\_kv} has size 1. In MHA, there's no
  grouping---\texttt{head} is the same coordinate on Q and K. The
  grouping structure is invisible in the positional \texttt{matmul};
  visible in the named index patterns.
\item
  \textbf{Does the backward pass know what to sum over?} The gradient of
  attention sums over \texttt{seq\_q} for \texttt{dK} and \texttt{dV},
  over \texttt{seq\_k} for \texttt{dQ}, and over the head grouping for
  the KV projection. In positional autodiff, these sums happen silently.
  In named coordinates, they follow from the coordinate sets---same
  set-subtraction rule from Chapter 7 applied to attention.
\end{enumerate}

You don't need Einlang to ask these questions. You need to know that
they are the right questions. And the right questions are only visible
when the notation has a place for the answers.

Stop now and look at the last attention implementation you wrote. Can
you answer all four questions from the code alone?

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-kv-cache-audit}{%
\subsection{The KV-Cache Audit}\label{the-kv-cache-audit}}

Autoregressive generation uses a KV-cache: keys and values from previous
time steps are stored and reused. The cache introduces a new coordinate
relationship: \texttt{seq\_past} (cached) and \texttt{seq\_new}
(current) must be concatenated into a single \texttt{seq\_k} for the
attention computation.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Positional KV-cache}
\NormalTok{K_full }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.cat([K_cache, K_new], dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{seq_dim)  }\CommentTok{# which axis is seq_dim?}
\NormalTok{V_full }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.cat([V_cache, V_new], dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{seq_dim)}
\NormalTok{output }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ attention(Q_new, K_full, V_full)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The \texttt{dim} argument to \texttt{torch.cat} is a position number. If
\texttt{K\_cache} has shape \texttt{(batch,\ head,\ past\_len,\ d)} and
\texttt{K\_new} has shape \texttt{(batch,\ head,\ 1,\ d)}, then
\texttt{seq\_dim} is \texttt{2}. But if the layout is
\texttt{(batch,\ past\_len,\ head,\ d)}, \texttt{seq\_dim} is
\texttt{1}. The integer shifts with the layout. Change the layout, audit
every \texttt{cat} call.

In Einlang, the concatenation axis is named:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ K_full}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = concat}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{seq_k}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(}
\NormalTok{    K_cache}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_past, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    K_new}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_new, d}\OperatorTok{]}
\NormalTok{);}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ output}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..batch, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = attention}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, seq_q, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(Q_new, K_full, V_full);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{concat{[}seq\_k{]}} names the concatenation axis. The coordinate
\texttt{seq\_k} absorbs both \texttt{seq\_past} and \texttt{seq\_new}
into a single coordinate. If the layout changes, the coordinate name
doesn't. The \texttt{cat} happens over \texttt{seq\_k} regardless of
position.

The audit questions for a KV-cache: 1. Which coordinate does
\texttt{concat} operate over? (\texttt{seq\_k}) 2. Which coordinate does
the attention reduce over? (\texttt{seq\_k}---the same coordinate) 3.
Does the cached \texttt{seq\_k} range differ from the new
\texttt{seq\_k} range? (They are different domains, now merged)

The coordinate names make the cache structure visible. The positional
\texttt{dim=seq\_dim} records a position. The named
\texttt{concat{[}seq\_k{]}} records an identity.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-the-comparison-reveals-1}{%
\subsection{What the Comparison
Reveals}\label{what-the-comparison-reveals-1}}

Attention is the Square Matrix Test at scale. Almost every dimension can
be square with some other dimension in some configuration.
\texttt{seq\_q} can equal \texttt{seq\_k}. \texttt{head\_q} can equal
\texttt{head\_kv}. \texttt{d} can equal any of them. When dimensions
coincide, positional code for different architectures becomes textually
identical.

In Einlang, the coordinate names persist through the coincidence.
\texttt{self\_attention{[}seq,\ ...{]}} and
\texttt{cross\_attention{[}seq\_q,\ seq\_k,\ ...{]}} are different
functions with different signatures, even when
\texttt{seq\_q\ ==\ seq\_k} at runtime. The difference is in the source
code, where a reader can see it and static analysis can check it.

The attention chapter, more than the normalization chapter, reveals a
tension that runs through the whole book. Positional APIs for attention
are identical across variants---self, cross, MHA, MQA, GQA---because the
positional \texttt{matmul} and \texttt{softmax} don't need to know which
coordinates are which. The code is maximally reusable. And maximally
ambiguous. The same line \texttt{torch.matmul(Q,\ K.transpose(-2,\ -1))}
describes fundamentally different operations depending on the runtime
shapes of Q and K. The notation compresses different meanings into the
same symbol. The compression is efficient. It is also silent.

Named coordinates decompress. Self-attention and cross-attention get
different signatures. MHA and GQA get different coordinate structures.
The decompression costs keystrokes---more brackets, more names, more
declarations. But it recovers the information that the positional
compression discarded. The information is the identity of each
dimension. When you discard it, you gain genericity. When you keep it,
you gain auditability. The choice between the two notations is not a
choice between right and wrong. It is a choice between compression and
legibility, evaluated at the specific point where the cost of ambiguity
exceeds the cost of keystrokes.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{flash-attention-the-coordinate-structure-survives-optimization}{%
\subsection{Flash Attention: The Coordinate Structure Survives
Optimization}\label{flash-attention-the-coordinate-structure-survives-optimization}}

Flash Attention is a memory-efficient exact attention algorithm that
fuses the QK\^{}T matmul, softmax, and PV matmul into a single tiled
kernel. It dramatically reduces memory usage by recomputing the softmax
statistics in the backward pass rather than storing the full attention
matrix. From the user's perspective, the function signature is identical
to standard attention. The coordinate structure is unchanged.

This is a demonstration of the principle from Chapter 14: lowering is
strategy-independent. The same coordinate structure maps to different
execution strategies. Flash Attention is a lowering strategy---a choice
of how to execute the computation, not what computation to execute. The
coordinate names \texttt{seq\_q}, \texttt{seq\_k}, \texttt{head},
\texttt{d} are identical whether the lowering chooses the standard
attention kernel or the Flash Attention kernel.

In a positional API, Flash Attention is a drop-in replacement: replace
\texttt{attention(Q,\ K,\ V)} with \texttt{flash\_attention(Q,\ K,\ V)}.
The shapes are the same. The coordinate structure is the same---but only
implicitly, in the shapes. In an Einlang API, the coordinate contract is
the same---\texttt{fn\ attention{[}seq\_q,\ seq\_k,\ head,\ d{]}(...)}
for both. The lowering strategy (\texttt{standard} vs \texttt{flash}) is
an annotation, not a signature change:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\AttributeTok{#[}\NormalTok{strategy}\AttributeTok{(}\NormalTok{flash}\AttributeTok{)]}
\KeywordTok{let}\NormalTok{ output}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{..b, head, seq_q, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{ = attention}\OperatorTok{[}\NormalTok{head, seq_q, seq_k, d}\OperatorTok{]}\NormalTok{(Q, K, V);}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The coordinate names don't change. The contract doesn't change. Only the
execution strategy changes. This is the separation that the compiler's
lowering pass enables: coordinate contracts define what is computed.
Lowering strategies define how it is computed. The names belong to the
first. The optimizations belong to the second. They are orthogonal.

When a new attention variant appears---Flash Attention 2, Flash
Attention 3, a sparse attention kernel, a sliding-window attention---the
coordinate contract remains the same. The lowering strategy changes. The
names survive the optimization. The reader of the code still sees
\texttt{attention{[}head,\ seq\_q,\ seq\_k,\ d{]}(Q,\ K,\ V)} and knows
what coordinates are involved, regardless of which kernel executes
underneath.

This is the parting lesson of the comparison chapters. The coordinate
structure is the invariant. The execution strategy is the variable.
Named coordinates record the invariant. Positional notation records
neither---it defers both to runtime. The difference is whether the
invariant survives the next optimization.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Read the last attention implementation you wrote. Can you
distinguish self-attention from cross-attention from the code
alone---without checking tensor shapes at runtime? If the answer is no,
the distinction lives in your head. That is the gap.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-the-attention-audit}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: The Attention
Audit}\label{stop-and-think-the-attention-audit}}

Open the last attention implementation you wrote. It might be
self-attention, cross-attention, multi-head, multi-query, or
grouped-query. For each attention variant in your code, answer these
four questions from the Coordinate Audit in Section 6:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinate does \texttt{softmax} normalize over?} In
  positional code, this is \texttt{dim=-1} or \texttt{dim=seq\_dim}. Can
  you name the coordinate without checking the tensor shape at runtime?
  If the answer is ``the last dimension'' --- that's a position, not an
  identity.
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinate distinguishes queries from keys?} In
  self-attention, it's the same coordinate. In cross-attention, they're
  different. Can you tell which variant you have from the code alone? If
  the code is \texttt{attention(Q,\ K,\ V)} for both --- the distinction
  is in the tensor shapes, not in the source.
\item
  \textbf{Which coordinate groups query heads with KV heads?} In GQA,
  query heads are grouped with KV heads. In MQA, KV heads = 1. In MHA,
  they're the same. Can you tell which architecture you have from the
  code? If the grouping is
  \texttt{repeat\_factor\ =\ head\_q\ //\ num\_kv\_heads} --- the
  grouping is a runtime calculation, not a source-level fact.
\item
  \textbf{Does the backward pass know what to sum over?} The gradient of
  attention needs to sum over \texttt{seq\_q} for
  \texttt{dK}/\texttt{dV}, over \texttt{seq\_k} for \texttt{dQ}, and
  over the head grouping for the KV projection. Are these sums explicit
  in the backward code? Or are they generated by autograd from
  positional matmuls?
\end{enumerate}

You don't need Einlang to ask these questions. You need to know they're
the right questions. And they're only the right questions when the
notation has a place for the answers.

Now do one more thing. Write the Einlang signature for your attention
function --- just the signature, no body.
\texttt{fn\ my\_attention{[}seq\_q,\ seq\_k,\ head,\ d{]}(Q:\ ...,\ K:\ ...,\ V:\ ...)\ -\textgreater{}\ ...}.
Does the signature tell a reader: - Whether it's self-attention
(\texttt{seq\_q} = \texttt{seq\_k}) or cross-attention (different)? -
Whether it's MHA (same \texttt{head} on Q, K, V), GQA
(\texttt{head\_group,\ head\_kv}), or MQA (\texttt{head\_q,\ head\_kv})?
- What \texttt{softmax} normalizes over?

If the signature answers all three, you've discovered why the coordinate
names matter for attention. If it doesn't, the distinction lives in your
head --- and in the tensor shapes at runtime.

In the next chapter, we complete the comparison trilogy with physical
simulation---where the coordinates represent temperature, pressure, and
velocity fields, and confusing them means solving the wrong physics.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-11-comparison-physics}{%
\section{Chapter 11 · Comparison:
Physics}\label{chapter-11-comparison-physics}}

\begin{quote}
``The map is not the territory---but when the map says `temperature' and
the territory says `pressure,' you have a problem that no amount of
correct math can fix.''

--- The author
\end{quote}

\emph{Comparisons · Heat equations and field components in two
notations}

\vspace{2em}

You are simulating a two-dimensional thermodynamic system: temperature,
pressure, velocity-x, velocity-y, evolving on a spatial grid over time.
You store them in a tensor of shape \texttt{(T,\ N,\ 4)}. Integer
\texttt{2} is velocity-x. Integer \texttt{3} is velocity-y. You're
writing field-specific equations, slicing \texttt{{[}:,:,2{]}} and
\texttt{{[}:,:,3{]}} throughout the code. It works.

A colleague adds a magnetic field. Instead of appending it at index 4,
they insert it at index 0---``put the most important field first.''
Every \texttt{{[}:,:,2{]}} now silently reads pressure instead of
velocity-x. Every \texttt{{[}:,:,3{]}} reads velocity-x instead of
velocity-y. The shapes are correct. The code runs. The physics is wrong.
The simulation produces results that look plausible---pressure gradients
where velocity should be, velocity where magnetic flux should be---and
you discover the error three weeks later, staring at a contour plot that
shouldn't be symmetric.

The first two comparison chapters looked at machine learning
primitives---normalization and attention. This chapter looks at
something older: physical simulation. The heat equation, fluid dynamics,
multi-field coupling. Computations where coordinates carry physical
meaning---temperature, pressure, velocity---and confusing them means
solving the wrong physics.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-heat-equation}{%
\subsection{The Heat Equation}\label{the-heat-equation}}

The one-dimensional heat equation describes how temperature diffuses
through a rod over time:

\[u_t = \alpha \cdot u_{xx}\]

In explicit Euler stepping, each point's new temperature is a weighted
average of its neighbors:

\[u[t, i] = u[t-1, i] + \alpha \cdot (u[t-1, i+1] - 2 \cdot u[t-1, i] + u[t-1, i-1])\]

\textbf{NumPy:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ heat_diffusion(initial, alpha, T):}
\NormalTok{    N }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(initial)}
\NormalTok{    u }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros((T, N))}
\NormalTok{    u[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ initial}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, T):}
\NormalTok{        u[t, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ u[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ alpha }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}
\NormalTok{            u[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{:] }\OperatorTok{-} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ u[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ u[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, :}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{])}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ u}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The code works. But the Laplacian---the discrete second derivative---is
spread across three slice expressions: \texttt{u{[}t-1,\ 2:{]}},
\texttt{u{[}t-1,\ 1:-1{]}}, and \texttt{u{[}t-1,\ :-2{]}}. The
relationship between them (``these three terms form a stencil over the
spatial coordinate'') is invisible. If you swap \texttt{alpha} from 0.1
to 0.5 (violating the CFL condition), the code still runs---it just
produces physically impossible results.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{verbatim}
let u[t in 0..T, i] = initial[i];
let u[t in 1..T, i] = u[t-1, i] + alpha * (
    u[t-1, i+1] - 2.0 * u[t-1, i] + u[t-1, i-1]
);
\end{verbatim}

The Laplacian is a single expression:
\texttt{u{[}t-1,\ i+1{]}\ -\ 2*u{[}t-1,\ i{]}\ +\ u{[}t-1,\ i-1{]}}. The
index arithmetic \texttt{i+1} and \texttt{i-1} makes the stencil
visible. \texttt{i} is the spatial coordinate, and the offsets
\texttt{+1} and \texttt{-1} are relative to it. The declaration bracket
says \texttt{t\ in\ 1..T,\ i}---time runs from 1 to T-1, space runs over
the whole domain. The recurrence is a fact about the coordinate
\texttt{t}, stated in the bracket.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{multi-field-coupling}{%
\subsection{Multi-Field Coupling}\label{multi-field-coupling}}

Real simulations track multiple physical fields---temperature, pressure,
velocity components---coupled through partial differential equations. In
a positional array, these fields are stored along an integer axis:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# state shape: (T, N, 4)}
\CommentTok{# state[..., 0] = temperature}
\CommentTok{# state[..., 1] = pressure}
\CommentTok{# state[..., 2] = velocity_x}
\CommentTok{# state[..., 3] = velocity_y}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ coupled_step(state, t, alpha, beta):}
\NormalTok{    temp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[t, :, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    press }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[t, :, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    vx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[t, :, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    vy }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[t, :, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{]}
    \CommentTok{# ... coupled equations ...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{state{[}t,\ :,\ 0{]}} extracts temperature.
\texttt{state{[}t,\ :,\ 1{]}} extracts pressure. The mapping from
integer to physical quantity is in the comments. If a new field is
added---say, humidity---it becomes \texttt{state{[}...,\ 4{]}}. If the
order changes---temperature moves from index 0 to index 2---every
\texttt{{[}:,\ 0{]}} silently becomes wrong. The code runs. The numbers
change. No error is raised.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{verbatim}
let state[t in 0..T, i, field] = init_field(field, i);

let temp[t, i] = state[t, i, field=0];
let press[t, i] = state[t, i, field=1];
let vx[t, i] = state[t, i, field=2];
let vy[t, i] = state[t, i, field=3];
\end{verbatim}

\texttt{field} is a coordinate. Its values are named: \texttt{field=0}
is temperature, \texttt{field=1} is pressure. If humidity is added, it
becomes \texttt{field=4}---a new coordinate value, not a new integer to
remember. If the field order changes, the name \texttt{field=0} still
means temperature, regardless of where it sits in the array.

But the Einlang version does more: it names the \emph{physical
coordinate} \texttt{i} and the \emph{field coordinate} \texttt{field}
separately. The coupling equations can reference them by name. A term
that depends on temperature reads \texttt{state{[}t,\ i,\ field=0{]}}. A
term that depends on the spatial gradient reads
\texttt{state{[}t,\ i+1,\ field=0{]}\ -\ state{[}t,\ i-1,\ field=0{]}}.
The code says which field and which spatial offset.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{adding-a-new-field}{%
\subsection{Adding a New Field}\label{adding-a-new-field}}

Suppose the simulation is extended to include humidity. In the
positional version:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Before: state shape (T, N, 4)}
\CommentTok{# After:  state shape (T, N, 5)}
\CommentTok{# Every [..., 0:4] slice must be audited.}
\CommentTok{# Every equation that referenced field indices must be checked.}
\NormalTok{state }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros((T, N, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{temp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[:, :, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]      }\CommentTok{# unchanged — luckily}
\NormalTok{press }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[:, :, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]     }\CommentTok{# unchanged — luckily}
\NormalTok{vx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[:, :, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]        }\CommentTok{# unchanged — luckily}
\NormalTok{vy }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[:, :, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{]        }\CommentTok{# unchanged — luckily}
\NormalTok{humidity }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[:, :, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{]  }\CommentTok{# new}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Every integer index must be verified. The compiler provides no help. If
humidity was inserted at index 0 instead of appended at index 4, every
subsequent index shifts by one.

In the Einlang version:

\begin{verbatim}
let state[t in 0..T, i, field] = init_field(field, i);

let temp[t, i] = state[t, i, field=0];
let press[t, i] = state[t, i, field=1];
let vx[t, i] = state[t, i, field=2];
let vy[t, i] = state[t, i, field=3];
let humidity[t, i] = state[t, i, field=4];  // new line
\end{verbatim}

The existing field assignments are unchanged. \texttt{field=0} is still
temperature, regardless of whether humidity is \texttt{field=4} or
\texttt{field=0} with everything else shifted. The coordinate names are
stable under insertions because they are names, not positions.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-coupled-burgers-equation}{%
\subsection{The Coupled Burgers
Equation}\label{the-coupled-burgers-equation}}

The 1D coupled Burgers equation for velocity \texttt{v} and temperature
\texttt{T}:

\[v_t + v \cdot v_x = \nu \cdot v_{xx} + \beta \cdot T_x\]

Each term has a specific coordinate interpretation: \texttt{v\_t} is the
time derivative (difference along \texttt{t}), \texttt{v\_x} is the
spatial derivative (difference along \texttt{i}), \texttt{v\_\{xx\}} is
the second spatial derivative, and \texttt{T\_x} is the temperature
gradient driving the velocity.

\textbf{NumPy:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, T):}
\NormalTok{    v_xx }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (v[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{:] }\OperatorTok{-} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{v[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ v[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, :}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ dx}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{    v_x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (v[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{:] }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ v[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, :}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{dx)}
\NormalTok{    T_x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (T[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{:] }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ T[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, :}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{dx)}
\NormalTok{    v[t, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (v[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
                  \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ dt }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (nu }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ v_xx}
                          \OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ v[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ v_x}
                          \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ beta }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ T_x))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The field identity (\texttt{v} vs \texttt{T}) is in variable names. The
coordinate identity (\texttt{t} vs \texttt{i}) is in bracket positions.
The stencil structure is in the slicing patterns.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{verbatim}
let v[t in 1..T, i] = v[t-1, i]
    + dt * (nu * (v[t-1, i+1] - 2.0*v[t-1, i] + v[t-1, i-1]) / (dx**2)
            - v[t-1, i] * (v[t-1, i+1] - v[t-1, i-1]) / (2.0*dx)
            + beta * (T[t-1, i+1] - T[t-1, i-1]) / (2.0*dx));
\end{verbatim}

The terms are identifiable by their coordinate arithmetic: \texttt{i+1}
and \texttt{i-1} are spatial derivatives. \texttt{t-1} is the time
recurrence. \texttt{v{[}...{]}} and \texttt{T{[}...{]}} are different
fields, named as different tensors. The equation reads like the PDE it
discretizes.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-the-comparison-reveals-2}{%
\subsection{What the Comparison
Reveals}\label{what-the-comparison-reveals-2}}

Physical simulation exposes a different failure mode than machine
learning. In ML, wrong coordinates produce wrong gradients and degraded
performance. In physics, wrong coordinates produce physically impossible
results---negative temperatures, violated conservation laws---that may
look plausible at a glance.

The integer field index (\texttt{state{[}...,\ 2{]}}) is the weakest
link in the positional chain. Stop and test yourself. If you saw
\texttt{state{[}...,\ 2{]}} in a Fortran simulation code---no comments,
no documentation, just the integer index---could you be 100\% certain it
is velocity-x? If you answered yes, ask yourself: is that certainty
coming from the code, or from a convention you memorized?

A convention is a fact that lives outside the notation. It is correct
until someone reorganizes the field order, or inserts a new field at
index 0, or reuses the same integer for a different field in a different
function. The convention drifts. The integer stays the same. The bug is
not in the arithmetic---it is in the gap between what the integer means
and what the code records.

In Einlang, \texttt{field=0} and \texttt{field=1} are names. They
survive reorganization because the name \texttt{field=0} is tied to the
coordinate value, not to its position. If a new field is inserted at
\texttt{field=0}, the compiler flags the conflict---two fields cannot
both be \texttt{field=0}. The integer \texttt{2} would silently become a
different field. The name \texttt{field=0} would refuse.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{adding-a-magnetic-field}{%
\subsection{Adding a Magnetic Field}\label{adding-a-magnetic-field}}

Now extend the simulation with a magnetic field \texttt{B} as a fifth
physical quantity. In the positional version, this means changing the
state shape from \texttt{(T,\ N,\ 4)} to \texttt{(T,\ N,\ 5)} and
auditing every occurrence of the integer \texttt{4} and every slice that
assumed four fields:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{# Before: state shape (T, N, 4)}
\CommentTok{# After:  state shape (T, N, 5)}
\CommentTok{# Must audit: every [..., 0:4], every [..., 3], every hardcoded "4"}
\NormalTok{B }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[:, :, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{]  }\CommentTok{# new — but is it really at index 4?}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If \texttt{B} was inserted at index 0, every subsequent index shifts:
\texttt{temp} moves from 0 to 1, \texttt{press} from 1 to 2, and so on.
The compiler provides no help. The integer \texttt{state{[}...,\ 0{]}}
does not know it was supposed to be temperature.

In the Einlang version:

\begin{verbatim}
let state[t in 0..T, i, field] = init_field(field, i);

let temp[t, i] = state[t, i, field=0];
let press[t, i] = state[t, i, field=1];
let vx[t, i] = state[t, i, field=2];
let vy[t, i] = state[t, i, field=3];
let B[t, i] = state[t, i, field=4];     // new line — one change
\end{verbatim}

The existing field assignments are unchanged. \texttt{field=0} is still
temperature. If \texttt{B} were inserted at \texttt{field=0}, the
compiler would flag every subsequent \texttt{field=N} reference as
referring to a shifted domain---because each field value now maps to a
different physical quantity. The coordinate name \texttt{field} is
stable under insertions. The integer index \texttt{2} is not.

The cost difference is not in writing the initial code. It is in every
modification made after the original author has forgotten which integer
meant what.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-wave-equation-a-stencil-in-two-notations}{%
\subsection{The Wave Equation: A Stencil in Two
Notations}\label{the-wave-equation-a-stencil-in-two-notations}}

The 1D wave equation describes how a displacement propagates through a
medium:

\[u_{tt} = c^2 \cdot u_{xx}\]

In explicit finite differences, it becomes a three-point stencil in
space and a two-point stencil in time:

\[u[t, i] = 2 \cdot u[t-1, i] - u[t-2, i] + c^2 \cdot (u[t-1, i+1] - 2 \cdot u[t-1, i] + u[t-1, i-1])\]

\textbf{NumPy:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ wave_step(u, t, c):}
\NormalTok{    u[t, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ u[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ u[t}\DecValTok{-2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
                   \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ c}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (u[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{:] }\OperatorTok{-} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ u[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{:}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ u[t}\DecValTok{-1}\NormalTok{, :}\OperatorTok{-}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The time index \texttt{t} is in variable position
\texttt{u{[}t,\ ...{]}}. The spatial stencil \texttt{i-1,\ i,\ i+1} is
distributed across three slices: \texttt{u{[}t-1,\ 2:{]}},
\texttt{u{[}t-1,\ 1:-1{]}}, \texttt{u{[}t-1,\ :-2{]}}. The second
derivative structure \texttt{(f{[}i+1{]}\ -\ 2*f{[}i{]}\ +\ f{[}i-1{]})}
is visible only if you mentally align the three slices.

\textbf{Einlang:}

\begin{verbatim}
let u[t in 0..1, i] = initial[i] + dt * v_initial[i];
let u[t in 2..T, i] =
    2.0 * u[t-1, i] - u[t-2, i]
    + c**2 * (u[t-1, i+1] - 2.0 * u[t-1, i] + u[t-1, i-1]);
\end{verbatim}

The Laplacian stencil is a single expression:
\texttt{u{[}t-1,\ i+1{]}\ -\ 2*u{[}t-1,\ i{]}\ +\ u{[}t-1,\ i-1{]}}. The
index arithmetic names the stencil offsets: \texttt{i+1} is the right
neighbor, \texttt{i-1} is the left neighbor. The time recurrence names
\texttt{t-1} (one step back) and \texttt{t-2} (two steps back). If
someone accidentally writes \texttt{i+2} instead of \texttt{i+1}, the
stencil would be wrong---but the error is a single character in a named
expression, not a misaligned slice that the reader must reconstruct.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-inventory-what-the-physics-chapter-found}{%
\subsection{The Inventory: What the Physics Chapter
Found}\label{the-inventory-what-the-physics-chapter-found}}

Physical simulation exposes a failure mode distinct from machine
learning. In ML, wrong coordinates degrade performance---the loss is
worse, the BLEU score drops. In physics, wrong coordinates produce
physically impossible results: negative absolute temperatures, violated
conservation laws, waves that amplify instead of propagating. The
symptoms may look plausible at a glance---the contour plot has the right
general shape, the time series has the right range. Only a physicist's
eye catches them.

The integer field index (\texttt{state{[}...,\ 2{]}}) and the positional
stencil slices (\texttt{u{[}t-1,\ 2:{]}}) share the same root cause:
\textbf{the mapping from integer to meaning lives outside the notation.}
The integer \texttt{2} is velocity-x because the comment says so. The
slice \texttt{2:} is the right neighbor because the reshape put it
there. When the mapping drifts---a new field inserted, a dimension
reordered---the integer stays the same and the meaning changes.

In Einlang, \texttt{field=2} is velocity-x because the coordinate value
\texttt{2} is bound to the name \texttt{field}. If the field order
changes, \texttt{field=2} still refers to the same physical
quantity---or the compiler catches the inconsistency. The name is tied
to the coordinate, not to its position. The stencil \texttt{i+1} is the
right neighbor because \texttt{i} is the spatial coordinate and
\texttt{+1} is the rightward offset. If the spatial dimension moves,
\texttt{i} still means spatial---the name doesn't change.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-navier-stokes-skeleton}{%
\subsection{The Navier-Stokes
Skeleton}\label{the-navier-stokes-skeleton}}

Fluid dynamics is the grand challenge of computational physics. The
Navier-Stokes equations couple velocity, pressure, and vorticity across
three spatial dimensions and time. The codebase is typically hundreds of
thousands of lines of Fortran or C++, with integer dimension indices
scattered throughout. The most common bugs are coordinate
swaps---confusing \texttt{x} for \texttt{y} velocity, or the \texttt{x}
momentum equation for the \texttt{y} momentum equation.

Here is a simplified 2D Navier-Stokes time step in Einlang, using the
same coordinate conventions from the heat equation and Burgers equation:

\begin{verbatim}
let u[t in 1..T, i, j] = u[t-1, i, j]
    + dt * (nu * (u[t-1, i+1, j] - 2.0*u[t-1, i, j] + u[t-1, i-1, j]) / dx**2
           + nu * (u[t-1, i, j+1] - 2.0*u[t-1, i, j] + u[t-1, i, j-1]) / dy**2
           - u[t-1, i, j] * (u[t-1, i+1, j] - u[t-1, i-1, j]) / (2.0*dx)
           - v[t-1, i, j] * (u[t-1, i, j+1] - u[t-1, i, j-1]) / (2.0*dy)
           - (p[t-1, i+1, j] - p[t-1, i-1, j]) / (2.0*dx));
\end{verbatim}

The terms are recognizable: the first two lines are the viscous
diffusion (Laplacian in \texttt{i} and \texttt{j}), the third line is
the advection (velocity convecting itself), the fourth line is the
pressure gradient. Each term names its coordinates and offsets.
\texttt{i+1} and \texttt{i-1} are always the x-differences. \texttt{j+1}
and \texttt{j-1} are always the y-differences. The fields \texttt{u},
\texttt{v}, \texttt{p} are separate tensors with separate names.

In the positional Fortran/C++ version, the same code uses array indices
like \texttt{U(I+1,\ J)}, \texttt{U(I,\ J+1)}, \texttt{P(I+1,\ J)}---the
coordinate names \texttt{i} and \texttt{j} are loop variables, not part
of the tensor structure. The field identity is in the variable name
(\texttt{U}, \texttt{V}, \texttt{P}). The stencil is distributed across
multiple array access expressions. If an index is typed
wrong---\texttt{U(I,\ J+1)} where \texttt{U(I+1,\ J)} was intended---the
compiler cannot catch it because both are valid array accesses. The bug
survives compilation and produces physically plausible but incorrect
results.

The Einlang version separates three concerns that the Fortran version
merges: 1. \textbf{Field identity}: \texttt{u}, \texttt{v}, \texttt{p}
are different tensors, not different array names pointing into the same
multi-field state tensor. 2. \textbf{Coordinate identity}: \texttt{i} is
the x-coordinate, \texttt{j} is the y-coordinate. The offsets
\texttt{+1} and \texttt{-1} say which direction. 3. \textbf{Stencil
structure}: the finite difference terms are grouped by physical meaning
(diffusion, advection, pressure).

In Fortran, all three concerns are compressed into \texttt{U(I+1,\ J)}.
The compression works. But it makes every stencil access look like every
other stencil access. When they differ, only the reader's eye catches
the difference.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{comparison-with-jax-and-functional-physics}{%
\subsection{Comparison with JAX and Functional
Physics}\label{comparison-with-jax-and-functional-physics}}

JAX's functional approach to physics simulation---pure functions, no
mutation, explicit state passing---shares philosophical ground with
Einlang. In JAX, a simulation step is:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ step(state, dt):}
\NormalTok{    u, v, p }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ state[}\StringTok{'u'}\NormalTok{], state[}\StringTok{'v'}\NormalTok{], state[}\StringTok{'p'}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    u_new }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ u }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ dt }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (nu }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ laplacian(u) }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ advection(u, v) }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ grad_x(p))}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ \{}\StringTok{'u'}\NormalTok{: u_new, }\StringTok{'v'}\NormalTok{: v_new, }\StringTok{'p'}\NormalTok{: p_new\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The fields are named dictionary keys. The stencils are in the function
names (\texttt{laplacian}, \texttt{advection}, \texttt{grad\_x}). The
coordinate structure---which stencil operates on which axis---is inside
each function, not at the call site. This is a design choice:
