encapsulate the stencil, expose the physics.

Einlang's contribution is a third axis of naming: not just the field
(dictionary key) and the operation (function name), but the
\emph{coordinate}. \texttt{u{[}t-1,\ i+1,\ j{]}} names the field
(\texttt{u}), the time recurrence (\texttt{t-1}), and the spatial offset
(\texttt{i+1,\ j}). JAX names the field and the operation. Einlang adds
the coordinate. When the coordinate is wrong---when \texttt{i+1} should
be \texttt{j+1}---the name is visible at the point of error. In JAX, the
error is inside \texttt{grad\_x}, and the call site only knows it called
\texttt{grad\_x}, not which coordinate \texttt{grad\_x} operates on.

The three comparison chapters---normalization, attention,
physics---converge on the same finding. Each notation makes different
facts visible. Positional notation makes shapes visible. JAX's
functional notation makes data dependencies visible. Einlang's
coordinate notation makes coordinate identities visible. None is
strictly better. But when the bug is a coordinate identity error---and a
surprising fraction of tensor bugs are---the notation that records
identities is the notation that catches the error.

\vspace{2em}

The three comparison chapters---normalization, attention, physics---have
shown a consistent pattern. Positional notation is not incorrect. It is
\emph{underspecified}. It records shapes. It does not record identities.
When identities matter---and they always matter---the difference between
a correct program and a wrong one is a name that was never written down.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{If you saw \texttt{state{[}...,\ 2{]}} in a simulation code with
no comments, could you be 100\% certain it is velocity-x? If your answer
is ``yes, because of the convention''---ask yourself whether the
convention survived the last refactoring. The integer stays the same.
The meaning drifts. Only a name can anchor it.}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-integer-index-audit}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: Integer Index
Audit}\label{stop-and-think-integer-index-audit}}

Physical simulation code is dense with integer indices. Every
\texttt{state{[}...,\ k{]}} is a claim about which field lives at
position \texttt{k}. Every \texttt{u{[}i+1{]}} is a claim about what
\texttt{+1} means. Audit your own code --- or any simulation code you've
read:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Find every integer field index.} \texttt{state{[}...,\ 0{]}},
  \texttt{state{[}...,\ 1{]}}, \texttt{state{[}...,\ 2{]}}. For each
  one, can you name the physical quantity? If the answer is ``yes,
  because 0 is density, 1 is velocity-x, 2 is velocity-y'' --- where is
  that mapping recorded? In a comment? In an enum? In variable names?
  How far away from the index is the documentation?
\item
  \textbf{Find every stencil offset.} \texttt{u{[}i+1,\ j{]}},
  \texttt{u{[}i-1,\ j{]}}, \texttt{u{[}i,\ j+1{]}},
  \texttt{u{[}i,\ j-1{]}}. For each one, which spatial direction does it
  represent? If the answer is ``\texttt{i+1} is the right neighbor in
  x'' --- is that always true, or does it depend on the mesh convention?
  Could someone accidentally write \texttt{i-1} intending right?
\item
  \textbf{Trace one physical quantity through the code.} Pick
  \texttt{temperature}. Find every place it's read and written. If the
  code uses integer indexing --- \texttt{state{[}...,\ 3{]}} --- you
  must find every \texttt{3} and check that it corresponds to
  temperature. If the field order changes (a new diagnostic field is
  inserted at position 2), every \texttt{3} becomes wrong. Some should
  become \texttt{4}. Some should stay \texttt{3} (if they referred to a
  different field that was originally at position 4). The refactoring
  requires manual verification of every integer index. There is no
  compiler to verify it for you.
\end{enumerate}

In Einlang, there is. \texttt{state{[}...,\ temp{]}} stays \texttt{temp}
regardless of field order. The coordinate name is the anchor. The
integer is the implementation detail. The difference is whether the
anchor is in the code or in your head.

Now we enter the book's most critical section. We stop comparing
notations and start building: the engine that gives names their power.
How does a compiler represent, analyze, and lower a program written in
named coordinates? You are about to see what Einlang looks like from the
inside---and build the machinery that makes the first ten chapters
checkable.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-12-the-shape-of-thought}{%
\section{Chapter 12 · The Shape of
Thought}\label{chapter-12-the-shape-of-thought}}

\begin{quote}
``A compiler is a program that reads a program written in one
language---and says it again in another.''

--- Adapted from SICP
\end{quote}

\emph{Construction · Intermediate representation}

\vspace{2em}

A compiler cannot operate on source code strings directly. It needs an
internal language. This language must satisfy two conditions. First, it
must be able to express every structure in the source language with
perfect fidelity---no information lost in translation. Second, it must
be simple enough that checker passes can traverse it mechanically,
without parsing or disambiguation.

We use S-expressions---parenthesized lists---as this internal language.
In this language, coordinate names are preserved exactly as written.
They are not pre-resolved to integers. Model names remain model names.
The structure mirrors the source, stripped of syntactic sugar, with all
semantic relationships intact.

The compiler's first job is translation: read Einlang, write
parentheses. Here is what that translation looks like.

\vspace{2em}

You just wrote a short Einlang program.

\begin{verbatim}
let x = softmax[class](logits[batch, class]);
let y = sum[class](x[batch, class] * labels[batch, class]);
\end{verbatim}

Someone asks: what is the shape of \texttt{y}? You cannot run this
code---there is no data. But you can still answer. How?

Stop and actually do it. Trace the coordinates in your head.
\texttt{logits} has \texttt{{[}batch,\ class{]}}.
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}} preserves the shape---output is still
\texttt{{[}batch,\ class{]}}, bound to \texttt{x}. Then
\texttt{sum{[}class{]}} consumes \texttt{class}. The surviving
coordinate is \texttt{batch}. \texttt{y} has shape \texttt{{[}batch{]}}.

You just performed coordinate propagation in your head. You tracked each
name---where it was introduced, how it flowed through the function call,
whether it survived the reduction. You did not need data. You did not
need to run the program. You needed only the names.

Now ask: what exactly did your brain do? It maintained a mental table:

\begin{verbatim}
logits:  coordinates {batch, class}
x:       coordinates {batch, class}   ← softmax preserves
labels:  coordinates {batch, class}
y:       coordinates {batch}           ← sum consumes class
\end{verbatim}

At each step, you looked at the operation and asked: which coordinates
go in, which come out? The answer was always in the brackets.
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}} says \texttt{class} is the normalized
coordinate---preserved. \texttt{sum{[}class{]}} says \texttt{class} is
the reduced coordinate---consumed.

You did not think about positions. You did not count axes. You did not
check shapes. You checked names. And you got the answer instantly.

This---exactly this---is what a compiler must do. It must answer the
same questions you just answered: what is the shape of every tensor?
Which coordinates survive each operation? Does the coordinate contract
at each call site match the function's declaration? The compiler needs
to do this for every line of every program, without data, without
execution. It needs an internal form in which these questions have
mechanical answers.

That form is the \textbf{intermediate representation}. And the first
decision in designing it is the most important one: \textbf{names must
survive translation.} If the IR replaced \texttt{class} with
\texttt{axis=1}, the compiler would have to reconstruct the identity you
just effortlessly traced. Reconstruction is brittle---it depends on
conventions, on consistent naming, on the very human discipline that
positional notation abandons. Preservation is mechanical---if the name
is in the IR, the compiler can check it. If the name is gone, it cannot.

A compiler cannot operate on source code strings directly. It needs an
internal language. This language must satisfy two conditions. First, it
must express every structure in the source language with perfect
fidelity---no information lost in translation. Second, it must be simple
enough that checker passes can traverse it mechanically, without parsing
or disambiguation.

We use S-expressions---parenthesized lists---as this internal language.
In this language, coordinate names are preserved exactly as written.
They are not pre-resolved to integers. Model names remain model names.
The structure mirrors the source, stripped of syntactic sugar, with all
semantic relationships intact.

The compiler's first job is translation: read Einlang, write
parentheses. Here is what that translation looks like.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-you-write-and-what-the-compiler-sees}{%
\subsection{What You Write, and What the Compiler
Sees}\label{what-you-write-and-what-the-compiler-sees}}

\begin{verbatim}
A[i, j] = B[i, k] * C[k, j]
\end{verbatim}

After parsing, the compiler sees a tree:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output A (i j))}
\NormalTok{  (}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (index B (i k)) (index C (k j))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{A{[}i,\ j{]}} becomes
\texttt{(let-decl\ (output\ A\ (i\ j))\ ...)}. \texttt{B{[}i,\ k{]}}
becomes \texttt{(index\ B\ (i\ k))}. \texttt{*} becomes \texttt{(*)}.

One-to-one. On the left is source code; on the right, parentheses.
Commas, equals signs, brackets---all gone. But every relationship
remains. \texttt{i} and \texttt{j} are names. \texttt{k} is a name.
Nothing has been turned into a number.

The compiler, at this stage, does not speak in numbers. It speaks in
names. Because names carry information---numbers come later.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{reduction-1}{%
\subsection{Reduction}\label{reduction-1}}

\begin{verbatim}
sum[i](A[i] * B[i])
\end{verbatim}

Becomes:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(reduction sum (i)}
\NormalTok{  (}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (index A (i)) (index B (i))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{sum{[}i{]}(...)} becomes \texttt{(reduction\ sum\ (i)\ ...)}.
The bracket holds the name of the reduction axis; the body sits one
level below. The structure mirrors what you write in mathematical
notation: Σ, then the index variable, then the expression.

Reduction with a where clause:

\begin{verbatim}
sum[i](A[i] * B[i]) where i > 5
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(reduction sum (i)}
\NormalTok{  (}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (index A (i)) (index B (i)))}
\NormalTok{  (where (}\OperatorTok{>}\NormalTok{ i (literal }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{where} becomes the third child of \texttt{reduction}. It filters
the reduction variable \texttt{i}---each iteration accumulates only
elements that satisfy the condition. Not a post-hoc mask. A gate on the
reduction itself.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{broadcasting}{%
\subsection{Broadcasting}\label{broadcasting}}

\begin{verbatim}
A[i, j] + bias[j]
\end{verbatim}

Becomes:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (index A (i j)) (index bias (j)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{bias} is indexed only by \texttt{j}. \texttt{A} is indexed by
\texttt{i} and \texttt{j}. The coordinate \texttt{i} is absent from
\texttt{bias}'s index list. The compiler records this absence---not as a
shape fact, but as a coordinate fact: \texttt{bias} declares
independence from \texttt{i}. Broadcasting is not a shape-compatibility
rule. It is a coordinate omission, visible in the index list.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{multiple-clauses}{%
\subsection{Multiple Clauses}\label{multiple-clauses}}

Einlang allows multiple clauses for the same tensor:

\begin{verbatim}
A[i, j] = B[i, j];
A[i, j] += C[i, j];
\end{verbatim}

Underneath:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output A (i j))}
\NormalTok{  (clause (}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{) (index B (i j)))}
\NormalTok{  (clause (+=) (}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (index A (i j)) (index C (i j)))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

One \texttt{let-decl} holds two clauses. They share axis
names---\texttt{i} and \texttt{j} mean the same thing in both.
\texttt{(=)} says ``initialize.'' \texttt{(+=)} says ``accumulate.'' The
IR makes the relationship explicit.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{coordinate-aware-functions}{%
\subsection{Coordinate-Aware
Functions}\label{coordinate-aware-functions}}

\begin{verbatim}
fn softmax[j](x: [f32; ..left, j, ..right])
    -> [f32; ..left, j, ..right] { ... }
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(define-fn softmax (coord-params j) (value-params x)}
\NormalTok{  (type-params (..left) (..right))}
\NormalTok{  (body ...))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The coordinate parameter \texttt{j} is a child of \texttt{define-fn}.
The packs \texttt{..left} and \texttt{..right} are type
parameters---they represent coordinate structure, not values.

At the call site:

\begin{verbatim}
softmax[class](logits)
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(call softmax (index logits (..batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (coord-args }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The coordinate argument \texttt{class} is transmitted separately from
the value argument \texttt{logits}. The compiler later verifies that
\texttt{class} exists on \texttt{logits} and that the coordinate
contract of \texttt{softmax} is satisfied.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{recurrence}{%
\subsection{Recurrence}\label{recurrence}}

\begin{verbatim}
let u[t in 0..T, i] = initial[i];
let u[t in 1..T, i] = u[t-1, i] + f(u[t-1, i]);
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(recurrence u (index-var }\KeywordTok{t}\NormalTok{) (var i)}
\NormalTok{  (clause (domain }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) (index initial (i)))}
\NormalTok{  (clause (domain }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ T)}
\NormalTok{    (}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (index u ((}\OperatorTok{-} \KeywordTok{t} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) i))}
\NormalTok{       (call f (index u ((}\OperatorTok{-} \KeywordTok{t} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) i))))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The time axis \texttt{t} is marked as \texttt{index-var}. Its domain is
split: \texttt{(domain\ 0)} for the initial condition,
\texttt{(domain\ 1\ T)} for the recurrence body. The backward reference
\texttt{t-1} becomes \texttt{(-\ t\ 1)} in the index position. The
compiler sees the subtraction and marks \texttt{t} as a time axis---a
coordinate with a direction.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{gradient}{%
\subsection{Gradient}\label{gradient}}

\begin{verbatim}
@loss / @w
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(gradient (}\KeywordTok{numerator}\NormalTok{ loss) (}\KeywordTok{denominator}\NormalTok{ w))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The gradient node records what it differentiates. The compiler computes
the pullback by reversing the forward graph---every forward reduction
becomes a backward broadcast, every forward broadcast becomes a backward
reduction. The shopping cart record, read in reverse.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{a-complete-program}{%
\subsection{A Complete Program}\label{a-complete-program}}

Put it all together:

\begin{verbatim}
let data = random([batch, class]);
let result = softmax[class](data);
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(program}
\NormalTok{  (let-decl (output data (batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    (call }\KeywordTok{random}\NormalTok{ (literal (batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))))}
\NormalTok{  (let-decl (output result (batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    (call softmax (index data (batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{      (coord-args }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{program} is the outermost node---the container for the entire
compilation unit. Inside are \texttt{let-decl} trees. Everything is in
parentheses. There are no exceptions.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-the-ir-preserves}{%
\subsection{What the IR Preserves}\label{what-the-ir-preserves}}

Read the softmax IR tree and notice three things.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Names are everywhere.} \texttt{i} and \texttt{class} remain
  names from start to finish. Nothing has become axis=0, axis=1. The IR
  preserves names.
\item
  \textbf{Reductions name their axis.}
  \texttt{(reduction\ max\ (class)\ ...)} and
  \texttt{(reduction\ sum\ (class)\ ...)}---the reduction operates on
  \texttt{class}, not ``axis 1.''
\item
  \textbf{Array access uses names.}
  \texttt{(index\ logits\ (i\ class))}---the indices are \texttt{i} and
  \texttt{class}, matching what the source wrote.
\end{enumerate}

This tree is the skeleton of your program. No syntactic sugar---no
colons, no equals signs, no commas. But every semantic relationship is
present. The names you wrote are still names. The coordinates you named
are still coordinates. The IR has not \emph{translated} your program. It
has \emph{said it again}, in parentheses.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{why-parentheses}{%
\subsection{Why Parentheses?}\label{why-parentheses}}

Why S-expressions? Why not a JSON AST? Why not protobufs?

Because parentheses are homoiconic: the IR is both the data structure
the compiler manipulates and the text format a human can read. A
compiler pass can traverse the tree with \texttt{car} and \texttt{cdr}.
A programmer can read the same tree in a debug log. There is no gap
between the internal representation and its printed form.

Because parentheses are uniform. Every node is
\texttt{(operator\ children...)}. There are no special cases.
\texttt{(reduction\ sum\ (class)\ ...)} looks like
\texttt{(let-decl\ (output\ C\ (i\ j))\ ...)} looks like
\texttt{(+\ (index\ A\ (i))\ (index\ B\ (i)))}. The uniformity makes
passes easy to write: every pass is a tree walk with a case for each
operator. Adding a new operator means adding one case.

Because parentheses have no ambiguity. In \texttt{x{[}i,\ j{]}}, the
comma and brackets are syntax---they must be parsed, their precedence
resolved against other operators. In \texttt{(index\ x\ (i\ j))}, the
structure is explicit: \texttt{index} is the operator, \texttt{x} is the
tensor, \texttt{(i\ j)} is the index list. No precedence to resolve. No
grammar to extend. The parentheses \emph{are} the parse tree.

The IR's job is to be the simplest possible form that preserves all
source-level information. Parenthesized prefix notation is that form. It
has been that form since Lisp discovered it in 1958. The Einlang IR does
not innovate on representation. It inherits.

\vspace{2em}

The compiler's native tongue does not perform magic. It simply restates
your Einlang program in a different notation---syntactic sugar removed,
core information preserved. Axis names remain. Reduction names remain.
Clause relationships remain. The compiler has not \emph{translated} your
program. It has \emph{said it again}, in parentheses.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Take a single line of your own tensor code. Translate it into
S-expressions by hand---parenthesize every operation, preserve every
name. What information survived? What was lost? The IR is not magic. It
is your program, said again, in a form that a machine can query.}

Now the tree exists. But the names on the tree are still just
names---they carry no range, no shape, no type. The next chapter asks:
what can the compiler \emph{derive} from those names, without running
the program?

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{you-are-the-compiler}{%
\subsection{You Are the Compiler}\label{you-are-the-compiler}}

Before we hand the tree to Chapter 13's analysis passes, let's do one
ourselves. Here is a small Einlang program:

\begin{verbatim}
let result[i, j] = sum[k](A[i, k] * B[k, j]) + bias[j];
\end{verbatim}

Translate it into IR. Don't look at the answer below. Actually do it.
Write it in parentheses.

\vspace{2em}

Done? Compare:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output result (i j))}
\NormalTok{  (}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (reduction sum (k)}
\NormalTok{       (}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (index A (i k)) (index B (k j))))}
\NormalTok{     (index bias (j))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Every construct becomes a parenthesized form. \texttt{sum{[}k{]}} →
\texttt{(reduction\ sum\ (k)\ ...)}. \texttt{A{[}i,\ k{]}} →
\texttt{(index\ A\ (i\ k))}. \texttt{+} → \texttt{(+)}. The \texttt{let}
declaration wraps the entire expression.

Now ask yourself: what can you determine about this program just by
reading the parentheses? No runtime. No data. Only the tree.

You can determine: - \texttt{result} has coordinates \texttt{(i,\ j)}.
Those are the survivor names in \texttt{let-decl}. - \texttt{k} is
consumed inside the reduction. \texttt{sum} eliminates it. -
\texttt{bias} is indexed only by \texttt{j}. It omits \texttt{i}---a
broadcast. - \texttt{A} and \texttt{B} share \texttt{k}. The
multiplication inside the sum operates on matching \texttt{k} positions.
- The addition of \texttt{bias{[}j{]}} to the reduction result requires
broadcasting \texttt{bias} along the \texttt{i} dimension---because the
reduction output has \texttt{(i,\ j)} and \texttt{bias} only has
\texttt{(j)}.

All of this is mechanically derivable from the tree. No guessing. No
shape arithmetic. Just coordinate names and their positions in index
lists. The tree preserves everything the source said---and makes it
queryable by compiler passes that only understand parentheses.

This is why the IR must preserve names. If the IR replaced \texttt{k}
with \texttt{axis=1}, the question ``which coordinate is consumed?''
would have an integer answer but no identity answer. The compiler could
still generate code---\texttt{axis=1} is all NumPy needs. But it could
not answer the \emph{check} questions: does \texttt{k} appear in both
\texttt{A} and \texttt{B}? Is \texttt{bias{[}j{]}} independent of
\texttt{i}? These questions require names, not numbers. The IR preserves
names so that the analysis passes in Chapter 13 can ask these
questions---and get answers that are checkable facts, not deduced
conventions.

Now let's test that claim with a harder case---one where the positional
IR would be silent:

\begin{verbatim}
let y[b, s] = mean[channel](x[b, channel, s]);
\end{verbatim}

IR:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output y (b s))}
\NormalTok{  (reduction mean (channel)}
\NormalTok{    (index x (b channel s))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

What can you determine from this tree? - \texttt{y} has
\texttt{(b,\ s)}. The declaration says so. - \texttt{channel} is
consumed by \texttt{mean}. It's in the reduction bracket and absent from
the output. - \texttt{x} has \texttt{(b,\ channel,\ s)}. All three are
in the index list.

Now ask: what if \texttt{x} had \texttt{(b,\ time,\ s)} instead? The IR
would be \texttt{(index\ x\ (b\ time\ s))} and the reduction bracket
would be \texttt{(reduction\ mean\ (channel)\ ...)}. The compiler would
check: does \texttt{channel} appear in \texttt{(b\ time\ s)}? No.~Error.
The name \texttt{channel} caught the mismatch. A positional IR with
\texttt{axis=1} would ask: is axis 1 valid on \texttt{x}?
Yes---\texttt{x} has 3 axes. No error. Silent consumption of the wrong
coordinate.

The tree preserves what the positional IR loses: the identity of the
consumed coordinate.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{writing-a-simple-analysis-pass}{%
\subsection{Writing a Simple Analysis
Pass}\label{writing-a-simple-analysis-pass}}

Let's write a pass together. Not in real code---in pseudocode, the way
you'd sketch it on a whiteboard. The pass checks Rule 1: Index
Existence. Every coordinate that appears in an \texttt{(index\ ...)} or
\texttt{(reduction\ ...)} must exist on the tensor being indexed.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{;; PASS: check-index-existence}
\CommentTok{;; Walk every (index T (c1 c2 ...)) node.}
\CommentTok{;; For each coordinate ci:}
\CommentTok{;;   1. Resolve T to its declaration.}
\CommentTok{;;   2. Check that ci is in T's declared coordinate list.}
\CommentTok{;;   3. If not, emit E003 with ci and T's name.}

\CommentTok{;; Example tree:}
\CommentTok{;; (index logits (batch class))  -- logits declared with (batch class): pass}
\CommentTok{;; (index logits (batch channel)) -- channel not on logits: E003}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

That's the pass. Four lines of logic. The same structure works for
\texttt{(reduction\ sum\ (k)\ (index\ A\ (i\ k)))}---\texttt{k} must
exist on every tensor indexed inside the reduction body. \texttt{i} must
exist on \texttt{A}.

Now a second pass, checking Rule 5: Coordinate Contract at call sites:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{;; PASS: check-coordinate-contract}
\CommentTok{;; Walk every (call fn ...) node.}
\CommentTok{;; For each coordinate argument ci:}
\CommentTok{;;   1. Resolve fn to its declaration.}
\CommentTok{;;   2. Get fn's coordinate parameter layout (coord-params ...).}
\CommentTok{;;   3. Bind ci to the corresponding parameter.}
\CommentTok{;;   4. Walk the value arguments. For each, check that ci exists on the tensor.}
\CommentTok{;;   5. If the call site passes ci but the tensor lacks ci, emit E006.}

\CommentTok{;; Example:}
\CommentTok{;; (call softmax (index logits (batch class)) (coord-args class))}
\CommentTok{;; softmax expects j, class is bound to j.}
\CommentTok{;; logits has (batch class): class exists. Pass.}
\CommentTok{;;}
\CommentTok{;; (call softmax (index logits (batch feature)) (coord-args class))}
\CommentTok{;; logits has (batch feature): class does not exist. E006.}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Again, four lines of logic. The pass is small because the information it
needs is in the tree. The coordinate argument \texttt{class} is a symbol
in \texttt{(coord-args\ class)}. The tensor's declared coordinates are
symbols in the declaration node. The check is symbol comparison. No
shapes. No positions. No inference. Just: ``does this name appear in
this list?''

The simplicity of the passes is the IR's justification. If the IR were a
complex data structure---nested records with optional fields, implicit
defaults, position-dependent layout---each pass would need to unpack,
normalize, and reconstruct before it could ask its one question. The
S-expression IR has no defaults, no implicit fields, no
position-dependent layout. Every fact is a symbol in a known position in
a known list. The pass asks its question. The answer is in the tree.

This is the point of homoiconicity from Section 7. The IR is both the
text the compiler reads and the data structure it queries. There is no
AST → IR translation step. There is no AST. The parse tree IS the IR.
What you wrote by hand in the ``You Are the Compiler'' exercise is
exactly what the parser produces. The passes walk what you wrote.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{why-names-must-survive-into-the-ir}{%
\subsubsection{Why Names Must Survive Into the
IR}\label{why-names-must-survive-into-the-ir}}

There is an alternative design. Translate names to integers at parse
time. \texttt{class} → \texttt{axis=1}. Then the IR has no names, only
positions. The analysis passes never see \texttt{class}. They see
\texttt{1}.

This design is simpler. It eliminates a whole category of IR nodes---no
\texttt{(reduction\ sum\ (class)\ ...)}, just
\texttt{(reduction\ sum\ 1\ ...)}. Every pass after parsing operates on
integers.

It is also wrong.

Here is why. The analysis pass that checks Rule 1 (Index Existence) must
answer: ``does this tensor have a coordinate called \texttt{class}?'' If
the IR says \texttt{(index\ logits\ (batch\ 1))} and the declaration of
\texttt{logits} is \texttt{(batch\ class)}, the pass must match
\texttt{1} against the second element of \texttt{(batch\ class)}. It
extracts position 2. It compares: \texttt{1\ ==\ 2}. They match. Rule 1
passes.

Now a refactoring changes the declaration: \texttt{logits} becomes
\texttt{(batch,\ spatial,\ class)}. The IR now says
\texttt{(index\ logits\ (batch\ 1))} and the declaration is
\texttt{(batch\ spatial\ class)}. Position 2 is \texttt{spatial}. The
pass checks: \texttt{1\ ==\ 2}. They match. Rule 1 passes. But
\texttt{1} is now \texttt{spatial}, not \texttt{class}. The check passed
because it checked a position, not an identity.

The same failure occurs for every rule. Rule 2 (Reduction Consistency):
is the reduction consuming the right coordinate? Position \texttt{1}
matches position \texttt{1} --- always. Rule 5 (Coordinate Contract):
does the call site pass the right coordinate? Position \texttt{1} at the
call site matches position \texttt{1} in the declaration --- always.

If names are translated to integers before analysis, every check reduces
to: ``is position p the same as position p?'' The answer is always yes.
The checks are vacuous.

This is the reason names must survive in the IR until analysis is
complete. The analysis rules are expressed in terms of coordinate
\emph{identities}, not coordinate \emph{positions}. \texttt{class} is an
identity. \texttt{1} is a position. An identity can be
wrong---\texttt{class} vs \texttt{channel} is a meaningful error. A
position can only be out of bounds. The analysis needs identities. The
IR preserves them. Lowering burns them into positions only after every
identity-based check has passed.

The IR is not a convenience. It is the minimum set of information that
makes the five check rules non-vacuous. If the names are gone before
analysis, the rules ask nothing. If the rules ask nothing, the compiler
is a shape checker. A shape checker catches position errors. It does not
catch identity errors. The entire book has been about identity errors.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-your-own-ir}{%
\subsubsection{Stop and Think: Your Own
IR}\label{stop-and-think-your-own-ir}}

Take the most recent tensor operation you wrote. Translate it into
S-expression IR by hand:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Write the source in Einlang:
  \texttt{let\ result{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ sum{[}k{]}(A{[}i,\ k{]}\ *\ B{[}k,\ j{]})}.
\item
  Translate:
  \texttt{(let-decl\ (output\ result\ (i\ j))\ (reduction\ sum\ (k)\ (*\ (index\ A\ (i\ k))\ (index\ B\ (k\ j)))))}.
\item
  Now ask: what can you determine from the IR alone? Survivors:
  \texttt{\{i,\ j\}}. Consumed: \texttt{\{k\}}. Shared: \texttt{k}
  appears on both \texttt{A} and \texttt{B}. Broadcasts: none --- both
  operands index all output coordinates except \texttt{k}.
\item
  Now ask: if the IR had integers instead of names ---
  \texttt{(let-decl\ (output\ result\ (0\ 1))\ (reduction\ sum\ (2)\ (*\ (index\ A\ (0\ 2))\ (index\ B\ (2\ 1)))))}
  --- could you determine which coordinate is shared? You could
  determine that axis 2 is contracted. You could not determine whether
  axis 2 is \texttt{k} or \texttt{feature} or \texttt{inner}. The
  identity is gone. The checks that depend on identity are impossible.
\end{enumerate}

The exercise takes two minutes. It shows why the IR preserves names. Not
for elegance. For correctness.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-13-the-name-in-the-mirror}{%
\section{Chapter 13 · The Name in the
Mirror}\label{chapter-13-the-name-in-the-mirror}}

\begin{quote}
``The purpose of analysis is not to add information, but to make
explicit the information that was already implicit in the names.''

--- The compiler writer's maxim
\end{quote}

\emph{Construction · Range, shape, type, and five check rules}

\vspace{2em}

The IR tree from Chapter 11 is full of names---\texttt{i},
\texttt{class}, \texttt{batch}, \texttt{sum}. But names \emph{said} and
names \emph{checked} are two different things.

Look at this tree:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output C (i j))}
\NormalTok{  (reduction sum (k)}
\NormalTok{    (}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (index A (i k)) (index B (k j)))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The names have already said: \texttt{i} and \texttt{j} survive,
\texttt{k} is consumed. But they haven't said: - Where does \texttt{i}
range? 0..100? 0..batch\_size? - What is the output shape? - What are
the types? - Do the coordinate contracts match at every call site?

The compiler must answer all four before it can generate code. This
chapter is about how it answers them---and about five check rules that
turn names from documentation into verification.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{range-analysis}{%
\subsection{Range Analysis}\label{range-analysis}}

RangeAnalysis walks the tree and finds a range source for every axis.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Array shapes.}
  \texttt{(index\ logits\ (i\ class))}---\texttt{i} appears at logits'
  0th position, so \texttt{i}`s range is logits' 0th dimension length.
\item
  \textbf{Literals.} If code says \texttt{A{[}i{]}} and \texttt{A} is
  defined with shape \texttt{(10)}, then \texttt{i}'s range is 0..10.
\item
  \textbf{Explicit declarations.}
  \texttt{(reduction\ sum\ (i)\ ...\ (domain\ 0..n))}---the range is
  given directly.
\end{enumerate}

At the outermost node, every axis has an annotation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{;; i     → (range 0 batch_size)}
\CommentTok{;; class → (range 0 num_classes)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{shape-analysis}{%
\subsection{Shape Analysis}\label{shape-analysis}}

With ranges, shapes follow mechanically. The output shape is all
declared axes minus reduction axes, each remaining axis taking its range
length.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output C (i j)) ...)}
\CommentTok{;; shape: (batch_size num_classes)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

With a reduction:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(reduction sum (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) body)}
\CommentTok{;; body has (batch n_class), reduction consumes class}
\CommentTok{;; output shape: (batch)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If the shapes don't match---if a clause declares \texttt{(class)} but
the body accesses an array requiring \texttt{k}---the compiler reports
it here. Not at runtime.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{type-propagation}{%
\subsection{Type Propagation}\label{type-propagation}}

Types flow from leaves to root:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(literal }\FloatTok{3.14}\NormalTok{)   → f64}
\NormalTok{(literal }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)      → i64}
\NormalTok{(index A (i))    → A's element }\KeywordTok{type}
\NormalTok{(}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ f64 f64)      → f64}
\NormalTok{(call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ f64)   → f64}
\NormalTok{(reduction sum ... f64) → f64}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Leaves know their types. Operators know input→output rules. Propagate
upward. If \texttt{(+\ f64\ i64)} appears, the compiler promotes i64 to
f64.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-five-check-rules}{%
\subsection{The Five Check Rules}\label{the-five-check-rules}}

Analysis culminates in five verification rules. Each catches a class of
bug that positional notation silently accepts.

\hypertarget{rule-1-index-existence}{%
\subsubsection{Rule 1: Index Existence}\label{rule-1-index-existence}}

Every name in an \texttt{(index\ T\ (axes...))} must exist in
\texttt{T}'s declared axis list.

\begin{verbatim}
// Correct
(index logits (batch class))  // logits has axes (batch class)

// Error
(index logits (batch time))   // logits has no time axis
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Catches}: renamed a dimension upstream but missed one index
site. \emph{If you have ever refactored a data pipeline and spent an
afternoon wondering why one operation silently started reading the wrong
axis, you have met this bug.}

\hypertarget{rule-2-reduction-consistency}{%
\subsubsection{Rule 2: Reduction
Consistency}\label{rule-2-reduction-consistency}}

The consumed coordinate must appear in every operand's index list within
the reduction body.

\begin{verbatim}
// Correct
(reduction sum (k)
  (* (index A (i k)) (index B (k j))))  // k appears in both

// Error
(reduction sum (k)
  (* (index A (i k)) (index B (j q))))  // k missing from B
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Catches}: reduction bracket says \texttt{k} but one index
reference uses \texttt{q} by mistake. \emph{If you have ever written
\texttt{sum{[}class{]}} but one operand's index used \texttt{batch}
instead---the result had the right shape but the wrong coordinate was
consumed---this rule would have caught it. Every chapter of this book
has used the same word: consume. A reduction consumes a coordinate. A
broadcast omits one so the backward pass can consume it. A gradient
collects over what was consumed. This rule is where that thread lands:
did you actually consume what you claimed to consume?}

\hypertarget{rule-3-broadcast-recording}{%
\subsubsection{Rule 3: Broadcast
Recording}\label{rule-3-broadcast-recording}}

For every term in an expression, compute its coordinate set. The output
coordinate set minus the term's coordinate set = the coordinates that
term broadcasts over. Record this. It is not an error check---it is the
foundation of the backward pass. What was broadcast forward becomes
reduced backward.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{;; (+ (index A (i j)) (index bias (j)))}
\CommentTok{;; A coordinates: \{i, j\}}
\CommentTok{;; bias coordinates: \{j\}}
\CommentTok{;; bias broadcasts over: \{i\}  ← recorded for gradient}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\emph{If you have ever traced a gradient that was silently summed over
the wrong dimension because a forward broadcast was implicit, you know
why this record must exist before the backward pass runs.}

\begin{verbatim}

### Rule 4: Causality Verification

In a recurrence body, every reference to the time variable must be strictly less than the declared index.
\end{verbatim}

// Correct (recurrence u (index-var t) \ldots{} (index u ((- t 1) i)))
// t-1 \textless{} t, valid

// Error (recurrence u (index-var t) \ldots{} (index u ((+ t 1) i))) //
t+1 \textgreater{} t, invalid

\begin{verbatim}

**Catches**: accidentally wrote `t+1` instead of `t-1` in a recurrence. *If you have ever implemented an RNN and watched the loss diverge because a forward reference read uninitialized memory instead of the previous hidden state, this rule catches that bug at compile time.*

### Rule 5: Coordinate Contract at Call Sites

When calling a coordinate-aware function, the coordinate arguments must exist on the value arguments, and the coordinate layout must match the function's declaration.
\end{verbatim}

// fn softmax\href{x:\%20\%5Bf32;\%20..left,\%20j,\%20..right\%5D}{j}
-\textgreater{} \ldots{}

// Correct (call softmax (index logits (batch class)) (coord-args
class)) // logits has class, softmax expects j ← class

// Error (call softmax (index logits (batch class)) (coord-args
nonexistent)) // logits has no nonexistent axis

\begin{verbatim}

**Catches**: `softmax[nonexistent](logits)`—a one-character typo caught at compile time. *If you have ever written `softmax(logits, dim=2)` and spent thirty minutes trying to understand why the output wasn't normalized along the dimension you intended, this rule would have told you immediately.*

---

## The Error Map: A Detective Walkthrough

You have seen the five rules. You have read what each catches. But reading a list of rules and applying them are different things. Let's apply them together.

Here is a broken Einlang program. It was written by a programmer who intended to implement a simple linear layer with a bias, followed by a softmax over classes. It compiles. It runs. It produces wrong results. The programmer has been debugging for two hours.
\end{verbatim}

fn
predict\href{x:\%20\%5Bf32;\%20batch,\%20in\%5D,\%20W:\%20\%5Bf32;\%20out,\%20in\%5D,\%20bias:\%20\%5Bf32;\%20out\%5D}{class}
-\textgreater{} {[}f32; batch, out{]} \{ let logits{[}batch, class{]} =
sum\href{x\%5Bbatch,\%20k\%5D\%20*\%20W\%5Bout,\%20k\%5D}{k} +
bias{[}out{]}; softmax\href{logits\%5Bbatch,\%20class\%5D}{class} \}

\begin{verbatim}

Stop. Before reading further, find the bugs yourself. There are multiple. Don't just glance—trace each coordinate. Which names appear where? Which names are missing? Which operations consume the right coordinate? Take two minutes. Write down every bug you find.

---

Done? Good. Now let's hunt together.

### The Wall

Imagine a detective's wall. Five empty slots, each labeled with one rule. Every time we find a bug, we pin it to the slot of the rule that caught it. By the end, we will see whether these five rules are enough—or whether some bugs slip through.

The slots:
\end{verbatim}

\protect\hyperlink{rule-1-index-existence}{Rule 1: Index Existence}
\protect\hyperlink{rule-2-reduction-consistency}{Rule 2: Reduction
Consistency} \protect\hyperlink{rule-3-broadcast-recording}{Rule 3:
Broadcast Recording} {[} Rule 4: Causality {]} {[} Rule 5: Coordinate
Contract {]}

\begin{verbatim}

### Round 1: Rule 1 — Index Existence

Rule 1 says: every name in an index list must exist on that tensor's declared axes. Let's check.

First line: `fn predict[class](x: [f32; batch, in], W: [f32; out, in], bias: [f32; out])`.

Parameter `x` declares axes `(batch, in)`. Parameter `W` declares axes `(out, in)`. `bias` declares axes `(out)`.

Second line: `let logits[batch, class] = sum[k](x[batch, k] * W[out, k]) + bias[out]`.

- `x[batch, k]`: `x` has `(batch, in)`. `batch` exists. `k`—does `k` exist on `x`? `x` has `in`, not `k`. **First bug.** `k` is not declared on `x`. The writer meant to use `in` but wrote `k`.

Pin it: **[Rule 1 — Index Existence]** `k` referenced on `x` but `x` declares `in`.

What about `W[out, k]`? `W` has `(out, in)`. `out` exists. `k`—same bug. `W` declares `in`, not `k`. Two pins on the same rule.

The reduction variable `k` in `sum[k]` is introduced by the reduction bracket—it is the coordinate being consumed. But it must match the coordinate that exists on the tensors inside the body. Here, `k` is a ghost coordinate—it appears in the index lists without being a declared coordinate of those tensors. The writer renamed the coordinate in their head but not in the code.

Now check `bias[out]`: `bias` declares `(out)`. `out` exists. Correct.

`softmax[class](logits[batch, class])` on line 3: `logits` was declared with `(batch, class)`. `class` exists on `logits`. Correct.

**Pins so far**: Rule 1 — two occurrences of the same rename bug.

### Round 2: Rule 2 — Reduction Consistency

Rule 2 says: the consumed coordinate must appear in every operand's index list within the reduction body.

The reduction is `sum[k]`. The body is `x[batch, k] * W[out, k]`.

Does `k` appear in every operand? `x[batch, k]` — yes, `k` is there. `W[out, k]` — yes, `k` is there. All operands reference `k`.

Syntactically, Rule 2 passes. But semantically? The reduction coordinate should be `in`—the coordinate that `x` and `W` both declare. `k` is a name the writer invented on the spot. The fact that `k` appears in both operands means Rule 2 is satisfied, but the *wrong coordinate* is being consumed. This is a near-miss: Rule 2 catches absence, not misidentification. The misidentification is caught by Rule 1 (index existence) when `k` doesn't match any declared coordinate. Together, Rules 1 and 2 cover both "the coordinate doesn't exist" and "the coordinate exists but isn't used consistently."

Now look at the output declaration: `logits[batch, class]`. The reduction consumed `k`. The surviving coordinates from the body are `batch` (from `x`) and `out` (from `W`). But the output declares `(batch, class)`. `class` appears in the output but was never in the body. `out` appears in the body but is not in the output. This is a **shape mismatch** between declared output coordinates and the coordinates that actually survive the reduction. The shape analysis from the previous section catches this—the declared coordinates don't match the body's surviving coordinates. It's not one of the five rules per se, but it falls out of the analysis the rules feed.

**Note it**: the output declaration says `class` but the body produces `out`. Caught by shape analysis.

### Round 3: Rule 3 — Broadcast Recording

Rule 3 records what each term broadcasts over. In `let logits[batch, class] = sum[k](x[batch, k] * W[out, k]) + bias[out]`:

The reduction `sum[k]` produces surviving coordinates `{batch, out}`. Then `+ bias[out]`. `bias` has coordinates `{out}`. The full output set is `{batch, out}`. `bias` is missing `batch`. So `bias` broadcasts over `batch`.

Is this correct? A bias should be independent of batch—that's the definition of a bias term. This broadcast is intentional. Rule 3 records it: `bias` broadcasts over `batch`. The backward pass will sum over `batch` to produce `d_bias[out]`. No bug here. Rule 3's job is not to flag errors—it's to record facts for the gradient. And the fact it records is correct.

### Round 4: Rule 4 — Causality

There is no recurrence in this program. No `t`, no `t-1`, no `t+1`. Rule 4 is silent. Move on.

### Round 5: Rule 5 — Coordinate Contract at Call Sites

Rule 5 says: when calling a coordinate-aware function, the coordinate argument must exist on the value argument, and the call must match the function's declared contract.

The call is `softmax[class](logits[batch, class])`. `softmax`'s declaration (from Chapter 3) is `fn softmax[j](x: [f32; ..left, j, ..right]) -> [f32; ..left, j, ..right]`. The call binds `j` to `class`. `logits` has `(batch, class)`. `class` exists on `logits`. This passes.

But now look at the function `predict` itself. Its return type is `[f32; batch, out]`. The body's last expression is `softmax[class](logits[batch, class])`, which returns `[f32; batch, class]`. The return type says `out`; the body produces `class`. **Bug.** The coordinate `out` in the return type doesn't match the coordinate `class` in the return value.

Pin it: **[Rule 5 — Coordinate Contract]** Return type declares `out` but body produces `class`.

### The Wall, After the Hunt

Let's look at our wall:
\end{verbatim}

\protect\hyperlink{rule-1-index-existence}{Rule 1: Index Existence}
\protect\hyperlink{rule-2-reduction-consistency}{Rule 2: Reduction
Consistency} \protect\hyperlink{rule-3-broadcast-recording}{Rule 3:
Broadcast Recording} ✗ k on x (meant ``in'') \textasciitilde{} k appears
in all operands ✓ bias broadcasts over batch ✗ k on W (meant ``in'')
(syntactic pass, semantic miss) (intentional, recorded)

{[} Rule 4: Causality {]} {[} Rule 5: Coordinate Contract {]} N/A (no
recurrence) ✗ Return type says ``out'', body produces ``class''

\begin{verbatim}

Four bugs pinned on the wall. Two caught by Rule 1. One caught by Rule 5. One caught by shape analysis (the output coordinate mismatch between declaration and expression). Rule 2 had a near-miss—the reduction coordinate was wrong but syntactically present. Rule 3 recorded a correct broadcast. Rule 4 was not applicable.

Now rewrite the program correctly:
\end{verbatim}

fn
predict\href{x:\%20\%5Bf32;\%20batch,\%20in\%5D,\%20W:\%20\%5Bf32;\%20class,\%20in\%5D,\%20bias:\%20\%5Bf32;\%20class\%5D}{class}
-\textgreater{} {[}f32; batch, class{]} \{ let logits{[}batch, class{]}
= sum\href{x\%5Bbatch,\%20in\%5D\%20*\%20W\%5Bclass,\%20in\%5D}{in} +
bias{[}class{]}; softmax\href{logits\%5Bbatch,\%20class\%5D}{class} \}

\begin{verbatim}

Three changes. `sum[k]` → `sum[in]`: the reduction now consumes the coordinate that both `x` and `W` declare. `W[out, k]` → `W[class, in]` (and `W`'s declaration from `out` to `class`): the output coordinate `class` now flows from `W`'s declaration through the reduction body to the output. Return type `out` → `class`: the declared return coordinate matches the body. Every coordinate flows from declaration to use. Every name matches.

### What the Wall Teaches

Every bug we found was a name that didn't match. `k` was supposed to be `in`. `class` was declared in the output declaration but came from nowhere in the body. `out` was in the return type but not in the return value. Not one of these bugs was a shape error—the shapes would have been correct in a positional version. `sum[k]` over the second axis of `(batch, in)` and second axis of `(out, in)` would produce `(batch, out)`—a valid shape. The code would run. The loss would descend. And the model would be computing a meaningless function.

Now replay the debugging session the programmer spent two hours on. In a positional framework, the first sign of trouble would be a runtime shape mismatch—maybe at the loss computation, maybe at the gradient step, maybe not at all if all shapes happened to align. The programmer would trace shapes backward, print `x.shape` and `logits.shape`, and eventually deduce that an axis was misnamed. The deduction would rely on the programmer's understanding of what each axis *should* be—an understanding not recorded anywhere in the code.

In the named version, the compiler pins the bugs before the program runs. Rule 1: `k` is not a coordinate of `x`. Rule 5: return type mismatch. The programmer sees the errors, fixes the names, and moves on.

The wall is not a metaphor. It is a design claim. The claim is: **every coordinate bug that can exist in a tensor program is caught by one of these five rules, or by the shape analysis that tracks whether declared coordinates match the coordinates that actually flow through expressions.** The five rules are not arbitrary. They are the five ways a name can be wrong: it can refer to a non-existent coordinate (Rule 1), it can fail to appear where the operation requires it (Rule 2), it can broadcast silently where it shouldn't—or worse, broadcast correctly but without the record the backward pass needs (Rule 3), it can reference the future in a recurrence (Rule 4), or it can violate the contract of a function call (Rule 5). That's it. Those are all the ways.

Every rule was born from a night someone spent staring at a tensor shape that was correct and a computation that was wrong. The night that became Rule 1: a renamed column in a CSV that silently shifted every downstream index. The night that became Rule 2: a `sum[class]` where one operand accidentally used `batch` instead, producing the right shape with the wrong reduction axis. The night that became Rule 3: a broadcast that flipped direction after a refactoring, and a gradient that silently summed over the wrong dimension. The night that became Rule 4: an RNN that read from `t+1` instead of `t-1` and converged to garbage. The night that became Rule 5: a `softmax[class]` call where `class` didn't exist on the tensor anymore, and the code ran anyway because `dim=-1` still pointed to something.

Five rules. Five nights. One wall.

---

## Five Micro-Scenarios

The wall was one program, broken in multiple ways. Now let's isolate each rule with its own scenario—one bug, one rule, one line. These are the shortest Einlang programs that trigger each check.

### Micro-Scenario 1: Index Existence (Rule 1)

A programmer refactors a data loader. The CSV column `temperature` is renamed to `temp`. The model code is updated—mostly:
\end{verbatim}

let readings{[}station, temperature{]} = load\_csv(``weather.csv''); let
anomalies{[}station{]} =
mean\href{readings\%5Bstation,\%20temperature\%5D}{temperature}; //
\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}
// ✓ temperature exists

\begin{verbatim}

But the normalization function still references the old name:
\end{verbatim}

fn
normalize\href{x:\%20\%5Bf32;\%20station,\%20temperature\%5D}{feature}
-\textgreater{} {[}f32; station, temperature{]} \{ let stats{[}station,
channel{]} = mean\href{x\%5Bstation,\%20temperature\%5D}{temperature};
// \^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{} 坐标 channel doesn't exist in output \}
// wait---the bug is subtler

\begin{verbatim}

Better example. The data pipeline changed, but one file wasn't updated:
\end{verbatim}

// file: data.ein --- updated let readings{[}station, temp, hour{]} =
load\_csv(``weather.csv'');

// file: model.ein --- NOT updated let avg{[}station, temperature{]} =
mean\href{readings\%5Bstation,\%20temperature,\%20hour\%5D}{temperature};
// \^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{}\^{} // ERROR: `temperature'
not in (station, temp, hour) // did you mean `temp'?

\begin{verbatim}

The error message names the missing coordinate and suggests the closest match. The programmer sees it, fixes `temperature` → `temp`, and the code compiles. In a positional framework, `temperature` was axis 1, `temp` is axis 1—the integer hasn't changed. The bug would compile and run. At runtime, it would silently average over the wrong thing.

**What Rule 1 catches**: a coordinate rename that the positional equivalent would silently absorb.

### Micro-Scenario 2: Reduction Consistency (Rule 2)

A programmer writes a bilinear form. Two matrices, one reduction:
\end{verbatim}

let bilinear{[}i, j{]} =
sum\href{A\%5Bi,\%20k\%5D\%20*\%20B\%5Bq,\%20j\%5D}{k}; // \^{} //
ERROR: reduction consumes `k', but `B' uses `q'

\begin{verbatim}

`k` is the reduction coordinate. It appears in `A[i, k]`. It does not appear in `B[q, j]`. The result shape would be `(i, q, j)`—three surviving coordinates from two-indexed tensors. The programmer meant `B[k, j]` but typed `q` by habit (`q` is used elsewhere in the function as a query coordinate).

Rule 2 catches this: the reduction coordinate `k` does not appear in every operand. The reduction cannot consume what isn't there. The compiler reports: *reduction coordinate `k` missing from operand `B`*.

In a positional framework: `A.shape = (n, m)`, `B.shape = (p, m)`. If `n == p`, the matmul produces a valid shape. The bug is that `B`'s first axis should contract with `A`'s second, but the shapes happen to allow a different contraction. The result has the right dimensions, the wrong values, and no error.

**What Rule 2 catches**: a coordinate in the reduction bracket that doesn't appear in every operand—a bug that shape-compatible tensors would hide.

### Micro-Scenario 3: Broadcast Recording (Rule 3)

Rule 3 is not an error check—it records facts. But the facts it records prevent errors downstream. Consider:
\end{verbatim}

let scaled{[}i, j{]} = x{[}i, j{]} * factor{[}j{]}; // factor omits i →
broadcasts over i

\begin{verbatim}

The compiler records: `factor` broadcasts over `{i}`. Now consider the gradient:
\end{verbatim}

// backward pass (automatic, from Rule 3's record) let d\_factor{[}j{]}
= sum\href{d_scaled\%5Bi,\%20j\%5D\%20*\%20x\%5Bi,\%20j\%5D}{i}; //
\^{}\^{}\^{} sum over i because factor broadcast over i forward

\begin{verbatim}

Without the record, the backward pass must infer the sum from shapes. If `factor` changes from a 1D `(j,)` to a 2D `(i, j)` during a refactoring, the shape-based inference changes silently—the backward sum might sum over different axes. With the record, the backward pass reads `broadcast over {i}` and sums over `{i}` regardless of shape.

Now the scenario where it matters. A programmer adds a time dimension to `factor`:
\end{verbatim}

// Before: factor{[}j{]} --- static per-class weight let scaled{[}i,
j{]} = x{[}i, j{]} * factor{[}j{]};

// After: factor{[}t, j{]} --- time-varying per-class weight let
scaled{[}t, i, j{]} = x{[}t, i, j{]} * factor{[}t, j{]}; // factor omits
i → broadcasts over \{i\} // factor has \{t, j\} --- t is present, i is
omitted

\begin{verbatim}

The broadcast record updates: `factor` broadcasts over `{i}` (still). The backward pass continues to sum over `i`. The time dimension `t` passes through undisturbed—because it was never in the broadcast record. The gradient comes out correct without the programmer touching the backward pass.

**What Rule 3 catches**: a backward pass that would silently change behavior when a forward broadcast's coordinate structure changes. The record survives shape changes.

### Micro-Scenario 4: Causality (Rule 4)

The simplest micro-scenario in the book. Two lines:
\end{verbatim}

let h{[}t in 1..T, d{]} = activation(h{[}t-1, d{]} * W{[}d, d{]} +
b{[}d{]}); // ✓ t-1 \textless{} t, valid backward reference

let h{[}t in 1..T, d{]} = activation(h{[}t+1, d{]} * W{[}d, d{]} +
b{[}d{]}); // ERROR: t+1 \textgreater{} t, forward reference ```

The second line is legal Python. In a positional loop:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{h }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.zeros(T, D)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ t }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, T):}
\NormalTok{    h[t] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ activation(h[t}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{@}\NormalTok{ W }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b)  }\CommentTok{# reads uninitialized memory at first iteration, IndexError at last}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The Python loop either reads garbage (if \texttt{h} is pre-allocated) or
raises an IndexError (if \texttt{t+1\ \textgreater{}=\ T}). Either way,
the error is at runtime---possibly after thousands of correct
iterations. The Einlang compiler rejects the second line at analysis
time. \texttt{t+1} references a future that has not been computed.

\textbf{What Rule 4 catches}: a forward reference in a recurrence. One
character (\texttt{+} vs \texttt{-}) changes a correct program into a
silent runtime error. Rule 4 catches it before the loop runs.

\hypertarget{micro-scenario-5-coordinate-contract-rule-5}{%
\subsubsection{Micro-Scenario 5: Coordinate Contract (Rule
5)}\label{micro-scenario-5-coordinate-contract-rule-5}}

A function declares a coordinate parameter. A call site passes the wrong
one:

\begin{verbatim}
fn softmax[j](x: [f32; ..left, j, ..right]) -> [f32; ..left, j, ..right] { ... }

let probs[batch, class] = softmax[batch](logits[batch, class]);
//                                ^^^^^
//                                ERROR: 'batch' bound to 'j', but 'batch' is in '..left' pack
//                                'j' must be a concrete coordinate, not absorbed into a variadic pack
\end{verbatim}

Wait---the error depends on how packs are resolved. The actual error:
\texttt{batch} is a concrete coordinate from \texttt{logits}. It binds
to \texttt{j}. The function's contract says \texttt{j} appears in the
return type. The call expects the return type to carry \texttt{batch}
through \texttt{j}. This compiles---and normalizes over \texttt{batch}
instead of \texttt{class}. Rule 5 catches this only if the function's
contract \emph{requires} \texttt{j} to be a specific coordinate that the
call site's tensor carries. If \texttt{batch} exists on \texttt{logits},
the check passes---because the contract checks existence, not intent.

This is the boundary from Chapter 7, revisited: names check consistency,
not correctness. But the cleaner error scenario is:

\begin{verbatim}
let probs[batch, class] = softmax[nonexistent](logits[batch, class]);
//                                ^^^^^^^^^^^
//                                ERROR: 'nonexistent' not in logits' coordinates (batch, class)
\end{verbatim}

The coordinate argument doesn't exist on the value argument. Rule 5
catches it. The compiler reports: \emph{coordinate \texttt{nonexistent}
not found on tensor \texttt{logits}; available coordinates:
\texttt{batch}, \texttt{class}}.

\textbf{What Rule 5 catches}: a coordinate argument at a call site that
doesn't match the tensor's declared coordinates. One typo in a bracket.
Caught at compile time. The positional equivalent
\texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=3)} on a 3D tensor raises a runtime
IndexError---or worse, \texttt{dim=-1} produces a valid operation on the
wrong axis, with no error at all.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-five-separated}{%
\subsubsection{The Five, Separated}\label{the-five-separated}}

Each rule catches a different shape of error. Together they cover the
space of coordinate bugs:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
\begin{minipage}[b]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Rule\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Bug shape\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Positional behavior\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\midrule
\endhead
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
1: Index Existence\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Coordinate renamed upstream, reference lingers\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Silent axis shift\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
2: Reduction Consistency\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Reduction coordinate missing from one operand\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Shape-compatible wrong contraction\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
3: Broadcast Recording\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Broadcast structure changes, backward pass drifts\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Silent gradient shape change\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
4: Causality\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Forward reference in recurrence\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Runtime garbage or IndexError\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
5: Coordinate Contract\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Wrong coordinate argument at call site\strut
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[t]{0.30\columnwidth}\raggedright
Silent normalization over wrong axis\strut
\end{minipage}\tabularnewline
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

Not every tensor bug is a coordinate bug. But every coordinate bug is
one of these five shapes. And every one of these shapes is silent in a
positional framework---because the positional framework has no place to
record the coordinate identity that would expose the mismatch.

Five rules. Five micro-scenarios. Five compiler errors that replace five
runtime silences. The wall and the micro-scenarios together are the
compiler's argument that \emph{the names are sufficient}---not
sufficient to catch every bug, but sufficient to catch every bug whose
root cause is a coordinate identity that was written in one place and
wrong in another.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{a-filled-in-tree}{%
\subsection{A Filled-In Tree}\label{a-filled-in-tree}}

Before analysis, the tree is bare parentheses with names:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(define-fn softmax (coord-params j) (value-params x)}
\NormalTok{  (type-params (..left) (..right))}
\NormalTok{  (body}
\NormalTok{    (let-decl (output e (..left j ..right))}
\NormalTok{      (call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ (}\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ (index x (..left j ..right))}
\NormalTok{                   (reduction }\KeywordTok{max}\NormalTok{ (j)}
\NormalTok{                     (index x (..left j ..right))))))}
\NormalTok{    ...))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

After analysis:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(define-fn softmax (coord-params j) (value-params x)}
\NormalTok{  (type-params (..left) (..right))}
  \CommentTok{;; j: (range 0 n_class), type: f64}
  \CommentTok{;; output shape: (..batch_size n_class ..rest)}
  \CommentTok{;; coordinate layout: (..left j ..right)}
  \CommentTok{;; five rules: all passed}
\NormalTok{  (body ...))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Every question mark is gone. Every parenthesis is annotated with the
answers the compiler derived. Range, shape, type, coordinate
layout---they flowed out of the names, and now sit beside the names.

\vspace{2em}

Names are not a convenience. They are information the compiler can
reason about. You wrote \texttt{(i\ class)} and
\texttt{(sum\ (class)\ ...)}. From that, the compiler derived shape,
range, type, and coordinate layout---and verified five rules that catch
the bugs positional notation leaves to comments.

Throughout this book, a single word has threaded through the chapters:
\textbf{consume}. A reduction consumes a coordinate---the bracket names
what disappears. A broadcast omits a coordinate---the omission records
what the value is independent of. A gradient collects over what was
broadcast and broadcasts over what was consumed. The five check rules,
taken together, are the compiler's answer to the question: \emph{did you
actually consume what you claimed to consume?} Rule 2 checks it
directly---the consumed coordinate must appear in every operand's index
list. Rule 3 records what was broadcast so the gradient can consume it.
Rule 4 checks that time steps only consume the past. The word
``consume'' is not decorative. It is the verb that links the forward
pass to the backward pass, the reduction bracket to the gradient sum,
the where clause to the filtered backward.

The tree is now complete. Every slot that needed an answer has one. It
can be safely handed to the next chapter---where the names are burned.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-14-firewood}{%
\section{Chapter 14 · Firewood}\label{chapter-14-firewood}}

\begin{quote}
``A ship in harbor is safe, but that is not what ships are built for.''

--- John A. Shedd
\end{quote}

\emph{Construction · Lowering: names become numbers}

\vspace{2em}

When Chapter 12 ended, your softmax looked like this:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output probs (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ (}\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{                  (reduction }\KeywordTok{max}\NormalTok{ (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{                    (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)))))}
\NormalTok{     (reduction sum (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{       (call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ (}\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{                    (reduction }\KeywordTok{max}\NormalTok{ (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{                      (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))))))))}
\CommentTok{;; type: f64, shape: (batch n_class)}
\CommentTok{;; i: (range 0 batch), class: (range 0 n_class)}
\CommentTok{;; five rules: all passed}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The tree is complete. It knows everything it needs to know. But it
cannot be handed to NumPy. NumPy does not understand what \texttt{class}
means. It needs \texttt{axis=1}.

Translating the analyzed tree into instructions a machine can
execute---this process is called \textbf{lowering}. This is the chapter
where names are burned.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{machines-dont-read-names}{%
\subsection{Machines Don't Read Names}\label{machines-dont-read-names}}

You wrote names. \texttt{i}, \texttt{class}, \texttt{sum}, \texttt{max}.

The machine needs integers. axis 0, axis 1. Loop ranges. Execution
strategy.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{;; (i class) → output axes: axis=0 is i, axis=1 is class}
\CommentTok{;; (reduction sum (class) ...) → reduction on axis=1}
\CommentTok{;; (index logits (i class)) → read whole logits, both axes present}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The translation rules are deterministic: - Every axis name maps to its
position in the declaration order. - Every reduction axis maps to the
integer position it occupies. - Every index list maps to a slice or
full-array access.

Lowering is the systematic translation of every name into a number.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-lowered-form}{%
\subsection{The Lowered Form}\label{the-lowered-form}}

After lowering, the tree gains execution annotations:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output probs (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ ...) (reduction sum (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) ...))}
\NormalTok{  (loops (i (range }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ batch)))}
\NormalTok{  (reduction-axes (}\KeywordTok{class} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (strategy vectorized))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{(loops\ ...)} --- the output axes to iterate. \texttt{i} needs a
loop. \texttt{class} is not here---it's consumed by the reduction.

\texttt{(reduction-axes\ ...)} --- \texttt{class} maps to axis 1. Code
generation turns this into \texttt{axis=1}.

\texttt{(strategy\ vectorized)} --- chosen automatically by the compiler
based on shapes.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{three-strategies}{%
\subsection{Three Strategies}\label{three-strategies}}

After lowering, all axis ranges are known. The compiler chooses:

\textbf{vectorized} --- hand everything to NumPy's C kernels:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(logits, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{e }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(logits }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ m)}
\ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ e }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(e, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{class} → \texttt{axis=1}. \texttt{keepdims=True} because the
analyzed shapes showed that \texttt{logits\ -\ max} requires
broadcasting.

\textbf{scalar} --- pure Python for loops. Used when arrays are jagged
or when a dependency chain prevents vectorization:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(batch):}
\NormalTok{    row_max }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(logits[i])}
\NormalTok{    row_exp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [exp(x }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ row_max) }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ x }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ logits[i]]}
\NormalTok{    row_sum }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(row_exp)}
\NormalTok{    result[i] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [e }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ row_sum }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ e }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ row_exp]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{hybrid} --- some dimensions use NumPy, others use Python loops.

The strategy is not chosen by the user. The compiler decides. You write
the einstein clause. The compiler chooses how to run it.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{matrix-multiplication-read-from-axis-sharing}{%
\subsection{Matrix Multiplication: Read from Axis
Sharing}\label{matrix-multiplication-read-from-axis-sharing}}

This is where lowering shows its elegance:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output C (i j))}
\NormalTok{  (reduction sum (k)}
\NormalTok{    (}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (index A (i k)) (index B (k j)))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Lowered:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output C (i j)) ...}
\NormalTok{  (loops (i (range }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ n)) (j (range }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ m)))}
\NormalTok{  (reduction-axes (k }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (strategy vectorized))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Code generation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ A }\OperatorTok{@}\NormalTok{ B}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{k} appears in both A and B, but not in the output axes
\texttt{(i\ j)} → a contracting dimension. The lowering recognizes this
as matrix multiplication---no user annotation, no
\texttt{np.einsum(\textquotesingle{}ik,kj-\textgreater{}ij\textquotesingle{},\ A,\ B)}.
The axis sharing pattern \textbf{is} the expression of intent.

If the same clause uses jagged arrays, lowering selects scalar strategy
instead. Same einstein clause, different execution path. The names are
the same. The strategy is the compiler's decision.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{lowering-multiple-clauses}{%
\subsection{Lowering Multiple Clauses}\label{lowering-multiple-clauses}}

Multi-clause declarations each receive their own annotations:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output A (i j))}
\NormalTok{  (clause (}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{) (index B (i j)))}
\NormalTok{  (clause (+=) (}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (index A (i j)) (index C (i j)))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

After lowering:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output A (i j))}
\NormalTok{  (clause (}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{) (index B (i j))}
\NormalTok{    (kind init)}
\NormalTok{    (loops (i (range }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ n)) (j (range }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ m))))}
\NormalTok{  (clause (+=) (}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (index A (i j)) (index C (i j)))}
\NormalTok{    (kind accum)}
\NormalTok{    (loops (i (range }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ n)) (j (range }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ m))))}
\NormalTok{  (strategy vectorized))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Generated code:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{result }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ B.copy()}
\NormalTok{result }\OperatorTok{+=}\NormalTok{ C}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{(kind\ init)} → \texttt{=}. \texttt{(kind\ accum)} →
\texttt{+=}. The lowering preserves the distinction.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-panorama-one-name-five-forms}{%
\subsection{The Panorama: One Name, Five
Forms}\label{the-panorama-one-name-five-forms}}

You have now traveled through all four parts of this book. Part I gave
you the primitives---naming, reducing, broadcasting. Part II composed
them---functions, recurrences, gradients. Part III put them next to the
notations you use every day. Part IV built the compiler that reads them.

Now stop. Don't turn the page yet. We are going to do something we
haven't done in any previous chapter: look at the same program in five
forms, simultaneously. Like a museum exhibit with a gem displayed under
five different lights, each revealing a different facet.

The program is softmax. You first saw it in Chapter 3. It has traveled
with you through every chapter since. It is the simplest non-trivial
coordinate-aware function in the language---six lines, one coordinate
parameter, one reduction. And in those six lines, the entire life cycle
of a name is visible.

Here it is. Read it slowly. Then we will unfold it.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-1-the-source}{%
\subsubsection{Form 1: The Source}\label{form-1-the-source}}

This is what you wrote. The ink on your screen. The letters you typed.

\begin{verbatim}
softmax[i class](logits[i class]) =
    exp(logits[i class] - max[class](logits[i class]))
    / sum[class](exp(logits[i class] - max[class](logits[i class])));
\end{verbatim}

Six lines. Two reductions named \texttt{{[}class{]}}. Three occurrences
of the index pattern \texttt{{[}i\ class{]}}. One coordinate parameter
\texttt{class} in the function bracket.

You read this and you see: \emph{softmax normalizes over \texttt{class},
preserves \texttt{i}.} The name \texttt{class} appears five times---in
the function bracket, in every index pattern, in both reduction
brackets. It is impossible to read this code and not know which
coordinate is being normalized.

Now look at the positional equivalent:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ softmax(logits, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(logits, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{dim, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    e }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(logits }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ m)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ e }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(e, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{dim, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{dim=-1}. That's it. That's the only record of which coordinate
is being normalized. If \texttt{logits} changes shape, \texttt{-1}
silently normalizes over whatever coordinate happens to be last.

The Einlang source records the name \texttt{class} five times. The
positional source records the position \texttt{dim=-1} once. The ratio
of recorded intent is 5:1. And yet the Einlang version is the one that
looks simpler---because the coordinate structure is in the brackets, not
in the reader's head.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-2-the-ir-tree}{%
\subsubsection{Form 2: The IR Tree}\label{form-2-the-ir-tree}}

The compiler reads your source. It strips the syntax---no more
\texttt{=}, no more \texttt{/}, no more line breaks. What remains is a
tree of parentheses. Every name you wrote is still a name.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(define-fn softmax (coord-params }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) (value-params logits)}
\NormalTok{  (type-params (i))}
\NormalTok{  (body}
\NormalTok{    (let-decl (output probs (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{      (}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (call }\KeywordTok{exp}
\NormalTok{           (}\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{              (reduction }\KeywordTok{max}\NormalTok{ (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{                (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)))))}
\NormalTok{         (reduction sum (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{           (call }\KeywordTok{exp}
\NormalTok{             (}\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{                (reduction }\KeywordTok{max}\NormalTok{ (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{                  (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))))))))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

No information lost. The structure mirrors the source exactly.
\texttt{(reduction\ max\ (class)\ ...)} --- the reduction still names
its axis. \texttt{(index\ logits\ (i\ class))} --- the index list still
names its coordinates. \texttt{(coord-params\ class)} --- the coordinate
parameter is still separate from the value parameter.

The IR is not a translation. It is a \emph{restatement}. Your source
code, spoken in the compiler's native tongue. The names are the same.
The structure is the same. Only the punctuation has changed.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-3-after-analysis}{%
\subsubsection{Form 3: After Analysis}\label{form-3-after-analysis}}

Chapter 13's analysis passes walk the IR tree. They ask: what is the
range of \texttt{i}? Where does \texttt{class} come from? Do the
reductions consume what they claim to consume? What is the output shape?
What is the type?

When the passes finish, the tree carries answers:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(define-fn softmax (coord-params }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) (value-params logits)}
\NormalTok{  (type-params (i))}
  \CommentTok{;; ── analysis annotations ──}
  \CommentTok{;; i:     (range 0 batch_size)}
  \CommentTok{;; class: (range 0 n_class), reduction axis}
  \CommentTok{;; output shape: (batch_size n_class)}
  \CommentTok{;; output type:  f64}
  \CommentTok{;; five rules:}
  \CommentTok{;;   Rule 1 ✓ — class exists on logits}
  \CommentTok{;;   Rule 2 ✓ — class appears in all operands of both reductions}
