  \CommentTok{;;   Rule 3 ✓ — broadcast recorded: (reduction max ...) omits class,}
  \CommentTok{;;              broadcasts back in subtraction}
  \CommentTok{;;   Rule 4 N/A — no recurrence}
  \CommentTok{;;   Rule 5 ✓ — coordinate contract satisfied}
\NormalTok{  (body}
\NormalTok{    (let-decl (output probs (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
      \CommentTok{;; surviving coordinates: \{i, class\}}
\NormalTok{      (}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ ...) (reduction sum (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) ...)))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Every question that can be answered from the names alone has been
answered. Range, shape, type, coordinate contract---all derived from the
names you wrote. The compiler did not need data. It did not need to run
the program. It needed the names, and the names were there.

Notice something: the positional softmax passes zero of these checks.
There is no \texttt{class} to verify. There is no coordinate contract to
check. The positional compiler sees \texttt{axis=-1} and says: ``valid
integer.'' It does not say: ``this integer refers to the coordinate you
intend to normalize over.'' The name-based compiler asks that question.
The positional compiler cannot ask it. Not because it is a worse
compiler---because it has less information.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-4-after-lowering}{%
\subsubsection{Form 4: After Lowering}\label{form-4-after-lowering}}

Now the names begin their transformation. Chapter 14's lowering pass
maps every name to a number, every coordinate to a loop or a reduction
axis.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(define-fn softmax (coord-params }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) (value-params logits)}
  \CommentTok{;; ── lowering annotations ──}
  \CommentTok{;; i     → axis=0, loop over 0..batch_size}
  \CommentTok{;; class → axis=1, reduction}
  \CommentTok{;; strategy: vectorized (all shapes known, no jagged arrays)}
  \CommentTok{;; keepdims: required for (logits - max) broadcast}
\NormalTok{  (body}
\NormalTok{    (let-decl (output probs (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
      \CommentTok{;; loops: (i (range 0 batch_size))}
      \CommentTok{;; reduction-axes: (class 1)}
      \CommentTok{;; generated call: np.max(logits, axis=1, keepdims=True)}
\NormalTok{      (}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ ...) (reduction sum (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) ...)))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{class\ →\ axis=1}. The name becomes a number. But it becomes the
right number---because the compiler mapped the declared coordinate order
to integer positions. \texttt{i} is first → axis 0. \texttt{class} is
second → axis 1. The mapping is deterministic. You can trace it by hand.

\texttt{keepdims:\ required} --- this annotation is not in the source.
The compiler deduced it. The subtraction \texttt{logits\ -\ max}
requires the max to broadcast back over \texttt{class}. The analyzed
shapes showed that \texttt{logits} has \texttt{(i\ class)} and
\texttt{max} reduces \texttt{class} to produce \texttt{(i)}. The shapes
differ. \texttt{keepdims=True} bridges the gap. The programmer didn't
write \texttt{keepdims=True}. The compiler inferred it from the
coordinate structure.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-5-the-generated-code}{%
\subsubsection{Form 5: The Generated
Code}\label{form-5-the-generated-code}}

The lowering annotations are complete. Every name has a number. Every
reduction has an axis. Every broadcast has a \texttt{keepdims}. The code
generator reads the annotations and emits NumPy:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ softmax(logits):}
\NormalTok{    m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(logits, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    e }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(logits }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ m)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ e }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(e, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This is the same NumPy code a human would write. \texttt{axis=1} came
from \texttt{class\ →\ axis\ 1}. \texttt{keepdims=True} came from the
broadcast requirement the compiler deduced. The code is clean,
vectorized, and identical to what you'd find in any well-written ML
library.

But it was not written by a human. It was generated from a source
program where \texttt{class} was a name and \texttt{max{[}class{]}} was
an expression of intent. The human wrote the names. The compiler burned
them into numbers.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-pause}{%
\subsubsection{The Pause}\label{the-pause}}

Now look at all five forms together. Not sequentially---simultaneously.
Let your eye move from the source \texttt{max{[}class{]}} to the IR
\texttt{(reduction\ max\ (class)\ ...)} to the analysis annotation
\texttt{class:\ (range\ 0\ n\_class),\ reduction\ axis} to the lowering
annotation \texttt{class\ →\ axis=1} to the generated code
\texttt{np.max(logits,\ axis=1,\ keepdims=True)}.

\begin{verbatim}
 max[class](logits[i class])                          ← what you wrote
 (reduction max (class) (index logits (i class)))     ← what the compiler sees
 class: (range 0 n_class), reduction axis, Rule 2 ✓   ← what the compiler derives
 class → axis=1, reduction, keepdims=True              ← how the name becomes a number
 np.max(logits, axis=1, keepdims=True)                 ← what executes
\end{verbatim}

Five forms. One name. The name \texttt{class} traveled through all five
without changing its identity. It was written as \texttt{class},
preserved as \texttt{class}, verified as \texttt{class}, mapped from
\texttt{class} to \texttt{axis=1}. At no point was it guesswork. At no
point did the compiler wonder ``is axis 1 really the class dimension?''
The information was in the source. The compiler carried it forward. The
number at the end is the correct number because the name at the
beginning was the correct name.

Now ask yourself: if the positional version had a bug---if
\texttt{dim=-1} was normalizing over the wrong axis---at which of the
five stages would that bug be caught?

Source: not caught. \texttt{dim=-1} is a valid integer. IR: not caught.
The IR for positional code has no names to verify. Analysis: not caught.
There is no coordinate contract to check. Lowering: not caught.
\texttt{-1} maps to the last axis correctly---it's the \emph{choice} of
the last axis that is wrong. Generated code: not caught.
\texttt{np.max(logits,\ axis=-1)} is valid NumPy.

The answer: \textbf{none of them.} The positional bug is invisible to
all five stages because the information that would expose it---the name
of the coordinate---was never written down.

The Einlang bug is caught at Form 3. Analysis checks Rule 2: does
\texttt{class} appear in every operand of the reduction? If
\texttt{class} doesn't exist on \texttt{logits}---if the tensor was
declared with a different coordinate name---Rule 1 catches it. If the
coordinate contract at the call site doesn't match the function
declaration, Rule 5 catches it. The bug surfaces before the program
runs, at the stage where names are still names and the compiler can
still reason about them.

You have now seen the complete life of a name. It is written. It is
preserved. It is verified. It is burned.

The name exists so that the number can be correct. The name is the
guarantee. The number is the execution. You cannot have the second
without the first---not if you want the number to mean what you think it
means.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{lowering-the-gradient}{%
\subsection{Lowering the Gradient}\label{lowering-the-gradient}}

The lowering pass handles the backward pass automatically. When the
analysis annotations include gradient information---which coordinates
are consumed forward, which are broadcast forward---lowering generates
both the forward code and the backward code from the same IR.

Consider a softmax gradient. Forward IR (after analysis):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(let-decl (output probs (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ (}\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{                  (reduction }\KeywordTok{max}\NormalTok{ (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)))))}
\NormalTok{     (reduction sum (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{       (call }\KeywordTok{exp}\NormalTok{ (}\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{                    (reduction }\KeywordTok{max}\NormalTok{ (}\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{) (index logits (i }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))))))))}
\CommentTok{;; reduction-axes: (class), surviving: (i class), keepdims: true}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Lowering reads the reduction axe \texttt{(class)} and maps it to
\texttt{axis=1}. The \texttt{keepdims:\ true} annotation drives
\texttt{keepdims=True} in the generated NumPy. The backward pass is
generated by applying the Inversion Rule mechanically: every forward
reduction (\texttt{max{[}class{]}}, \texttt{sum{[}class{]}}) becomes a
backward broadcast. The generated gradient code distributes the softmax
Jacobian without the programmer writing a single backward line.

A custom \texttt{@fn} rule overrides this automatic generation---but the
coordinate names in the \texttt{@fn} signature must match the primal.
The lowering pass verifies the match. If the primal declares
\texttt{softmax{[}j{]}} and the custom rule declares
\texttt{@fn\ softmax{[}k{]}}, the coordinate parameter names don't
match. Error. The names prevent the mismatch.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{firewood}{%
\subsection{Firewood}\label{firewood}}

Names are a good thing. They let you write \texttt{class} instead of
\texttt{1}. They let the compiler check coordinate alignment---by name,
not position. They make your code read like mathematical notes. They
survive the five check rules.

But names must eventually be burned. Because the CPU does not know
\texttt{class}. It knows axis 1, loop range n\_class, and floating-point
instructions.

Lowering is that fire. \texttt{(reduction\ sum\ (class)\ ...)} goes in;
\texttt{axis=1,\ reduction} comes out. \texttt{(i\ class)} goes in;
\texttt{axis=0,\ axis=1,\ output} comes out.
\texttt{(index\ logits\ (i\ class))} goes in; \texttt{keepdims=True}
comes out---because the analyzed shapes tell lowering that the division
and subtraction require broadcasting.

The names burn. The heat remains. And the heat is a computation that can
be handed directly to NumPy's C kernels.

A good abstraction is not a good painting. A good abstraction is good
firewood. Its beauty is not in its surface---but in the light the flame
casts when it burns.

\hypertarget{why-firewood}{%
\subsubsection{Why Firewood}\label{why-firewood}}

The metaphor deserves an honest look, because it has a dark side.

A painting is preserved. It hangs on the wall for a century. Firewood is
destroyed in its use. The name \texttt{class}---written, preserved,
verified across five check rules---is erased in the final step. What
comes out is \texttt{axis=1,\ keepdims=True}. The name is gone from the
generated code. Only the integer remains.

If you are debugging the generated code, you see \texttt{axis=1}. You do
not see \texttt{class}. The name that gave \texttt{axis=1} its meaning
is no longer there. You must trace it backward---to the lowering
annotations, to the analysis records, to the IR tree, to the source. The
trail exists, but it is a trail you must walk.

This is the cost of burning. The name is not in the artifact that runs.
It is in the artifact that was verified. The guarantee of correctness is
in the verification, not in the execution. The execution inherits
correctness from the name that burned. The CPU trusts the integer
because the compiler trusted the name.

Now compare this to the positional default. In \texttt{x.mean(dim=1)},
the integer \texttt{1} was always an integer. No name burned to produce
it. No verification confirmed that \texttt{1} is the right axis. The
integer is correct if the programmer guessed correctly. It is wrong if
the programmer guessed wrong. There is no trail to trace, because there
was never anything to burn.

The firewood metaphor has two sides. The dark side: the name is gone
from the execution, and the execution is what runs. The bright side: the
name was there during construction, and the construction is what
guarantees correctness. A program is not just its execution. It is the
process that produced the execution. That process is verifiable. The
positional process is not.

When you burn firewood, you don't lose the warmth. You lose the log. The
log was the vehicle for the warmth. The name was the vehicle for the
correctness. The correctness survives the burning. The name does not.
That is the trade. And it is a trade worth making, because the
alternative is an integer that was never a name---a fire that gave
warmth without ever having been lit.

\hypertarget{the-ash}{%
\subsubsection{The Ash}\label{the-ash}}

After lowering, the IR still carries both the name and the number:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{;; class → axis=1, reduction, keepdims=True}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This annotation is the ash. It is not the fire. It is not the log. It is
the record that a name was here and became a number. The code generator
reads the number. The human reader---or the debugger, or the gradient
checker---reads the annotation and sees the trail.

In a mature compiler, the ash persists. The generated code carries
source maps. \texttt{axis=1} at line 47 of the generated code
corresponds to \texttt{class} at line 3 of the source. The debugger can
show you both. Stack traces can name the coordinate that was consumed,
not just the axis that was reduced.

But even without source maps, the trail exists in the compiler's
internal records. The lowering pass writes \texttt{class\ →\ axis=1}
into the tree. The tree is preserved until code generation completes. If
a bug surfaces---a shape mismatch in the backward pass, a broadcast that
doesn't align---the compiler can report it in the language of the
source: \emph{broadcast over \texttt{class} does not match reduction
over \texttt{batch}}. The numbers are for the CPU. The names are for the
error message. The ash connects them.

A positional compiler cannot produce that error message. It can say
\emph{broadcast over axis 1 does not match reduction over axis 0}. That
message tells you positions that diverged. It does not tell you which
coordinates were intended. The ash is a record of intent. The positional
compiler has no ash because it had no firewood---no names to burn, no
trail to leave.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{stop-and-think-lower-a-function-yourself}{%
\subsection{Stop and Think: Lower a Function
Yourself}\label{stop-and-think-lower-a-function-yourself}}

Here is a small Einlang function. Your job: perform lowering by hand.

\begin{verbatim}
fn rms_norm[feature](x: [f32; batch, feature]) -> [f32; batch, feature] {
    let rms[batch] = sqrt(mean[feature](x[batch, feature] * x[batch, feature]));
    x[batch, feature] / rms[batch]
}
\end{verbatim}

The declaration order is \texttt{(batch,\ feature)}. \texttt{batch} is
first → axis 0. \texttt{feature} is second → axis 1.

Take five minutes. Write down:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Which axis does \texttt{mean{[}feature{]}} reduce over?
\item
  What does \texttt{keepdims} need to be for the division to work?
\item
  Write the equivalent NumPy.
\end{enumerate}

Don't scroll down until you've written your answers.

\vspace{2em}

Done? Compare:

\textbf{Reduction axis.} \texttt{mean{[}feature{]}} reduces over
\texttt{feature}. In declaration order, \texttt{feature} is second →
axis 1.

\textbf{keepdims.} The mean produces \texttt{rms{[}batch{]}} with shape
\texttt{(batch,)}. To divide \texttt{x{[}batch,\ feature{]}}---shape
\texttt{(batch,\ n\_feature)}---by \texttt{rms{[}batch{]}}, NumPy needs
shapes \texttt{(batch,\ n\_feature)} and \texttt{(batch,\ 1)}. Without
\texttt{keepdims=True}, mean returns \texttt{(batch,)}, and the division
broadcasts silently. With \texttt{keepdims=True}, the intent is
explicit.

\textbf{Generated NumPy:}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ rms_norm(x):}
\NormalTok{    rms }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.sqrt(np.mean(x }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ x, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{))}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ x }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ rms}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Three lines. \texttt{axis=1} came from \texttt{feature\ →\ axis\ 1}.
\texttt{keepdims=True} came from the broadcast requirement. The compiler
derived both. The programmer wrote the name. The compiler produced the
integer and the flag.

Now ask yourself: if you were writing \texttt{rms\_norm} from scratch in
pure NumPy, without this exercise, would you have remembered
\texttt{keepdims=True}? If you forgot it, the code would still run.
NumPy would broadcast \texttt{(batch,)} against
\texttt{(batch,\ n\_feature)} and produce a result. The bug would be
invisible unless you checked the output shape explicitly. The lowering
pass doesn't forget. It reads the broadcast from the coordinate
structure and emits the flag. The name \texttt{feature}---consumed by
\texttt{mean}, reconstructed by the division---tells the compiler
everything it needs to know.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-lowering-gradient-revisited}{%
\subsection{The Lowering Gradient
Revisited}\label{the-lowering-gradient-revisited}}

The firewood metaphor extends to the backward pass. The forward names
burn into forward integers. The backward pass reads the ash---the
broadcast records left by Rule 3---and generates backward integers from
them.

When \texttt{rms\_norm} is differentiated, the compiler reads:

\begin{verbatim}
Forward:  mean[feature] consumes feature → reduces axis=1
          rms[batch] omits feature → broadcasts over axis=1
Backward: sum over feature (axis=1) for d_rms[batch]
          consumes the broadcast coordinate
\end{verbatim}

The backward integer is \texttt{axis=1}---the same as the forward
integer, because the forward name \texttt{feature} mapped to axis 1. The
name is gone, but the integer it became is correct on both sides. The
ash connects forward to backward.

A positional framework also maps forward integers to backward
integers---the autodiff engine tracks which axes were reduced. The
difference is not the capability. It is the surface area for error. In a
named framework, a refactoring changes the coordinate declaration. The
lowering re-runs. The new integer flows to both forward and backward
passes. Downstream operations use names, not integers, so they are
unaffected. The name insulates downstream code from the integer's
change. In a positional framework, downstream code uses integers
directly---\texttt{axis=1} means ``the second axis,'' and after a
refactoring, the second axis may be something different. The integer
changed. The downstream code didn't. The bug surfaces silently.

The fire burned the name. The integer carries the warmth. But the
integer is local---it lives within the generated function. Code outside
the function uses names. The fire is contained. The names survive
outside the flame.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{Look at any \texttt{axis=1}}Look at any \texttt{axis=1} in your
generated or handwritten NumPy code. Trace it backward: what name did it
come from? If you cannot answer---if the integer has no recoverable
identity---you are looking at a number that was never a name. The fire
burned nothing, because there was nothing to burn. That is the
positional default.*

You have now seen the complete life cycle of a name in Einlang: it is
written in source, preserved in IR, verified by analysis, and burned in
lowering. The next chapter looks back at that life cycle and asks: what
did the names do for us---and what would have happened without them?

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-15-the-outline-of-the-name}{%
\section{Chapter 15 · The Outline of the
Name}\label{chapter-15-the-outline-of-the-name}}

\begin{quote}
``We shall not cease from exploration. And the end of all our exploring
will be to arrive where we started and know the place for the first
time.''

--- T. S. Eliot
\end{quote}

\emph{Reflection · What the names did for us}

\vspace{2em}

This book began with a bug.

A tensor had shape \texttt{(32,\ 64,\ 256)}. A programmer wrote
\texttt{x.mean(dim=1)}. The intent was ``average over channels.'' The
text said \texttt{dim=1}. Three months later, a refactoring moved
\texttt{channel} to position 2. \texttt{dim=1} now silently erased
\texttt{spatial}. The shape was still \texttt{(32,\ 256,\ 64)}. The loss
still went down. The model deployed on Tuesday. The customer complaint
arrived on Thursday.

The bug lived for three weeks because the notation had no slot for the
fact that would have caught it.

Thirteen chapters later, the slot exists. It is a bracket with a name in
it. And the bracket changes everything---not because it is syntactically
novel, but because it makes a fact checkable that was previously only
commentable.

This chapter is not a summary. It is a question: after everything we
have built, what did the names actually do for us?

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-life-of-a-name}{%
\subsection{The Life of a Name}\label{the-life-of-a-name}}

A coordinate name in Einlang lives through four stages. It is
\textbf{written} in source, as a letter between brackets:
\texttt{{[}i,\ j{]}}, \texttt{{[}class{]}}, \texttt{{[}batch{]}}. It is
\textbf{preserved} in the intermediate representation, stripped of
syntax but keeping every name intact:
\texttt{(let-decl\ (output\ C\ (i\ j))\ ...)}. It is \textbf{verified}
by analysis, where the compiler derives its range, checks its
consistency across call sites, and records which operations consume it.
And it is \textbf{burned} in lowering, translated into the integers that
machines require: \texttt{class\ →\ axis=1},
\texttt{i\ →\ loop\ 0..batch}.

Four stages. Written, preserved, verified, burned. At no point is the
name decoration. At every point, it is load-bearing.

A decoration can be omitted without consequence. You can remove the
comment \texttt{\#\ dim=1\ is\ channel} and the code still runs. You can
rename the variable \texttt{spatial\_features} to \texttt{x} and the
compiler says nothing. Decorations are for humans. The machine does not
read them.

A name in Einlang is not a decoration. The compiler reads it. The
checker verifies it. The lowering pass translates it. If you write
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)} and \texttt{logits} has no
coordinate called \texttt{class}, the compiler stops. Not at
runtime---at compile time. The name is part of the contract.

This is the difference between a comment and a coordinate. A comment
records intent. A coordinate enforces it.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-names-caught}{%
\subsection{What Names Caught}\label{what-names-caught}}

Walk back through the book and ask, at each stage: what would a
positional notation have let through?

\textbf{Chapter 1}: \texttt{x.mean(dim=1)}. The positional notation let
it through for three weeks. The named version
\texttt{mean{[}channel{]}(x)} would have broken at compile time the
moment \texttt{channel} moved to position 2, because the tensor would no
longer have a coordinate called \texttt{channel} at the position the
compiler expected. Or, more precisely: the compiler would have required
the refactoring programmer to update the coordinate declaration, and
that update would have surfaced the fact that \texttt{mean{[}channel{]}}
was still referencing the old layout.

\textbf{Chapter 2}: \texttt{A\ +\ bias}. The positional notation
broadcasts \texttt{bias} along whichever dimensions happen to be
missing. If \texttt{A} changes from \texttt{(batch,\ feature)} to
\texttt{(feature,\ batch)}, the broadcast silently flips. The named
version \texttt{out{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ A{[}i,\ j{]}\ +\ bias{[}j{]}} makes
the omission visible: \texttt{bias} has no \texttt{i}, so it broadcasts
over \texttt{i}. If \texttt{i} and \texttt{j} swap meanings upstream,
the indexing pattern breaks visibly.

\textbf{Chapter 3}: \texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=-1)}. When
\texttt{batch\_size\ ==\ num\_classes}, the square matrix test applies:
\texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=0)} and \texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=-1)}
both produce valid probability distributions. The named version
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)} does not let you silently normalize
over \texttt{batch}. The name \texttt{class} is either present on
\texttt{logits} or it isn't.

\textbf{Chapter 5}: \texttt{u{[}t,\ i{]}\ =\ u{[}t+1,\ i{]}\ +\ f(...)}.
In a positional recurrence, writing \texttt{t+1} instead of \texttt{t-1}
produces a forward reference---a read from the future. The positional
loop runs. The values are whatever was in memory. The named version
rejects it: the causality check sees \texttt{t+1\ \textgreater{}\ t} and
halts.

\textbf{Chapter 7}: The gradient of a broadcast. Forward: \texttt{bias}
omits \texttt{batch}, broadcasting over it. Backward: the gradient must
sum over \texttt{batch} to recover \texttt{bias}'s shape. In a
positional framework, this sum is implicit in the autodiff engine. If
the broadcast changes because the shape changed, the gradient sum
changes with it---silently. In the named version, the coordinate sets
tell you exactly what the gradient must sum over: \texttt{C} has
\texttt{\{i,\ j\}}, \texttt{A} has \texttt{\{i,\ k\}}, sum over
\texttt{\{j\}}. The set subtraction is checkable.

\textbf{Chapter 8}: GroupNorm's reshape chain:
\texttt{x.reshape(N,\ G,\ C//G,\ H,\ W).mean(dim=(2,3,4))}. The
positions \texttt{(2,3,4)} are only correct after the reshape. If the
reshape changes, the positions change. The named version
\texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ ..spatial{]}} names the coordinates
directly. The reshape is unnecessary because the coordinates are
separate from the start.

\textbf{Chapter 9}: Self-attention and cross-attention in PyTorch have
identical code. The difference is only in the shapes of the tensors
passed at runtime. The named version distinguishes
\texttt{self\_attention{[}seq,\ ...{]}} from
\texttt{cross\_attention{[}seq\_q,\ seq\_k,\ ...{]}} in the type
signatures. A reader can see which is which without checking runtime
shapes.

Every one of these bugs was shape-correct. Every one survived the checks
that positional frameworks perform. Every one was caught by a name.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-names-couldnt-catch}{%
\subsection{What Names Couldn't Catch}\label{what-names-couldnt-catch}}

The boundary in Chapter 6 was not an accident. Named coordinates verify
that a coordinate \emph{exists} on a tensor. They do not verify that
index arithmetic stays within bounds. \texttt{oh\ +\ kh} is
syntactically valid as long as both \texttt{oh} and \texttt{kh} are
declared coordinates. Whether \texttt{oh\ +\ kh} exceeds the input's
spatial extent is a runtime question.

This boundary is a design choice, not a limitation. The compiler checks
what can be proven from names and domains alone. Bounds checking is the
runtime's job. Semantic correctness---whether the formula means what you
think it means---is yours.

The names reduce the surface area of uncheckable facts. They do not
eliminate it. A comment that says ``channel'' can be wrong. A coordinate
named \texttt{channel} can also be wrong---you might have named the
wrong axis \texttt{channel}. But the coordinate, once named, is checked
for consistency everywhere it appears. The comment is checked nowhere.
The coordinate is a fact. The comment is a hope.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{if-the-names-had-been-there}{%
\subsection{If the Names Had Been
There}\label{if-the-names-had-been-there}}

The book has shown what names caught. But there is another way to
understand their value: replay the book's scenarios with names absent,
and watch the bugs survive.

\textbf{If Chapter 1's bug had names\ldots{}} The programmer who
refactored \texttt{channel} from position 1 to position 2 would have
updated the coordinate declaration
\texttt{{[}batch,\ channel,\ spatial{]}} to
\texttt{{[}batch,\ spatial,\ channel{]}}. The line
\texttt{mean{[}channel{]}(x)} would have compiled---because
\texttt{channel} still exists on \texttt{x}. But wait. Would the bug
have been caught? The answer depends on what else changed. If the mean
was always \texttt{mean{[}channel{]}}, and the declaration still has a
coordinate named \texttt{channel}, the reduction is still over the right
coordinate. The name protects the reduction from the layout change. The
positional version \texttt{dim=1} silently changes meaning; the named
version \texttt{mean{[}channel{]}} doesn't. This is the simplest case,
and the name handles it completely.

\textbf{If Chapter 2's broadcast had names\ldots{}} \texttt{A\ +\ bias}
becomes \texttt{A{[}i,\ j{]}\ +\ bias{[}j{]}}. The broadcast over
\texttt{i} is visible. Six months later, a programmer adds a time
dimension to \texttt{A}---it becomes \texttt{A{[}t,\ i,\ j{]}}. The
broadcast \texttt{bias{[}j{]}} now omits both \texttt{t} and \texttt{i}.
The backward pass sums over \texttt{\{t,\ i\}}. In positional NumPy, the
broadcast would silently extend to the new leading dimension. The
gradient would sum over both. The shapes would match. But would the
semantics be correct? A bias that is independent of \texttt{i} (sample)
might not be independent of \texttt{t} (time). The name \texttt{t} is
present in \texttt{A} but absent from \texttt{bias}---the omission is a
claim: bias doesn't depend on time. The claim is visible. The programmer
reviewing the diff sees \texttt{bias{[}j{]}} where \texttt{A} now has
\texttt{A{[}t,\ i,\ j{]}} and asks: \emph{should bias really be constant
across time?} The name doesn't catch the semantic error---it makes the
assumption visible so the programmer can catch it.

\textbf{If the Square Matrix Test had names\ldots{}} A classifier with
\texttt{batch\_size\ =\ num\_classes\ =\ 64}. Train loss goes down. Test
loss goes down. Model deploys. Everything works. Then a new dataset
arrives with \texttt{num\_classes\ =\ 100}. The positional code
\texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=-1)} had been normalizing over the last
axis---which was \texttt{class} by coincidence, because \texttt{batch}
and \texttt{class} were both 64 and the rows and columns of the softmax
matrix both summed to 1. The new dataset breaks the square coincidence.
The bug surfaces. With names:
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}batch,\ class{]})} normalizes over
\texttt{class} regardless of whether \texttt{batch\_size} and
\texttt{num\_classes} are equal. The Square Matrix Test is not a bug
that names catch at compile time---it is a test that names make you pass
at development time, because the name \texttt{class} in the bracket
records your intent, and your intent doesn't change when the data
changes.

\textbf{If the GroupNorm reshape chain had names\ldots{}} The positional
code:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, G, C}\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{G, H, W).mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

With names:
\texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ H,\ W{]}(x{[}batch,\ group,\ c\_in\_group,\ H,\ W{]})}.
The reshape is unnecessary because the coordinates are already separate.
The reduction names \texttt{c\_in\_group}, \texttt{H}, \texttt{W}
directly. A refactoring that changes group size computes
\texttt{c\_in\_group\ =\ C\ //\ G} from the named coordinate \texttt{C}.
The positions don't need to be recalculated because they are never
written. The names absorb the layout change.

\textbf{If the attention code had names\ldots{}} Self-attention and
cross-attention are identical in PyTorch. The difference is only in the
shapes of the query and key tensors at runtime. A programmer debugging a
cross-attention bug prints shapes: \texttt{q:\ (batch,\ seq,\ d)},
\texttt{k:\ (batch,\ seq,\ d)}. The shapes match. The code is
self-attention. The programmer expected cross-attention because the
encoder and decoder should have different sequence lengths. With names:
\texttt{self\_attention{[}seq,\ ...{]}} and
\texttt{cross\_attention{[}seq\_q,\ seq\_k,\ ...{]}} are distinct
function signatures. \texttt{seq\_q} and \texttt{seq\_k} are different
coordinate names. The function call
\texttt{cross\_attention{[}seq\_q,\ seq\_k{]}(q,\ k,\ v)} where
\texttt{q} only has \texttt{seq\_q}---not \texttt{seq\_k}---and
\texttt{k} only has \texttt{seq\_k} would have a coordinate contract
that reflects the asymmetry. The names distinguish the two attention
patterns at the call site.

\textbf{If the recurrence had names\ldots{}} A programmer writes
\texttt{h{[}t{]}\ =\ h{[}t+1{]}\ +\ f(...)}. Python runs it. The result
is either garbage (if \texttt{h} is pre-allocated zeros) or an
IndexError (if \texttt{t+1\ \textgreater{}=\ T}). With names: the
causality check rejects \texttt{t+1} because it is a forward reference.
The error is caught at compile time. This is the cleanest case: one
character changes the program from correct to wrong, and the name
\texttt{t}---combined with the recurrence domain
\texttt{t\ in\ 1..T}---gives the compiler enough information to reject
the wrong one.

\textbf{If the gradient had names\ldots{}} Forward:
\texttt{factor{[}j{]}} broadcasts over \texttt{i}. Backward:
\texttt{d\_factor{[}j{]}\ =\ sum{[}i{]}(d\_scaled{[}i,\ j{]}\ *\ x{[}i,\ j{]})}.
The coordinate \texttt{i} is the broadcast coordinate, recovered by the
backward sum. In a positional framework, if \texttt{factor} changes from
1D \texttt{(j,)} to 2D \texttt{(i,\ j)}, the backward pass changes its
sum. With names, the broadcast record says
\texttt{factor\ omits\ \{i\}}. The backward sum is over
\texttt{\{i\}}---unchanged by the shape change, because the name
\texttt{i} is still the omitted coordinate. The name stabilizes the
backward pass across shape changes.

\vspace{2em}

Six scenarios. In each, the positional code could be correct---it is
correct, in the hands of a careful programmer who tracks dimension order
and documents every axis. The question is not whether positional code
can be correct. It is whether the notation makes the correctness
checkable. In every scenario, the named code records the coordinate
identity that correctness depends on. The positional code does not. The
identity lives in the programmer's head, in a comment, or nowhere. The
names move it into the source---where the compiler and the next reader
can both check it.

The ``if'' is not hypothetical. Every one of these bugs has occurred in
production, in codebases you have used, in frameworks you have imported.
The bugs were caught---eventually---by tests, by code review, by the
programmer staring at shapes at 3 AM. The question the book has asked is
not \emph{can these bugs be caught?} It is \emph{can these bugs be
caught at compile time, by the compiler, from information already
present in the code?} The answer, for every scenario above, is yes---if
the information is in the code. And the notation determines whether it
is.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-shape-of-the-book}{%
\subsection{The Shape of the Book}\label{the-shape-of-the-book}}

A name in Einlang is not a string. It is a structural element with a
defined life cycle. The book's four parts trace that life cycle:

\textbf{Part I (Chapters 1--2)} established that a coordinate has a
name, and that the name can be used to declare what survives, what is
consumed, and what is broadcast. The megaphone model gave intuition: a
tensor speaks on some coordinates and stays silent on others.

\textbf{Part II (Chapters 3--7)} showed how names compose.
Coordinate-aware functions make the operated-on coordinate part of the
type-level contract. Recurrence makes the direction of time a syntactic
constraint. Differentiation reads the forward pass backward, with the
same names organizing both directions.

\textbf{Part III (Chapters 8--10)} put Einlang next to PyTorch and
NumPy. The same computations, two notations. The question was not
``which is better'' but ``what does each notation make visible.''

\textbf{Part IV (Chapters 11--13)} opened the compiler. The IR preserves
names. Analysis derives range, shape, and type from them. Five check
rules verify their consistency. Lowering burns them into integers. The
names that survived three parts of the book finally become numbers---but
only after they have done all their work.

\vspace{2em}

This is the outline the names leave behind. Not a list of features. Not
a syntax reference. An outline of \emph{what changes} when coordinates
have names that the compiler can read.

When you write \texttt{x.mean(dim=1)}, the \texttt{1} is a position. It
records where. It does not record what.

When you write \texttt{mean{[}channel{]}(x)}, the \texttt{channel} is a
name. It records both.

The distance between \texttt{where} and \texttt{what} is the distance
between a program that runs correctly by accident and a program that
runs correctly by construction. The names do not guarantee correctness.
They guarantee that incorrectness, when it occurs, leaves a trace that
the compiler can find and the reader can see.

\vspace{2em}

You are now holding the book that began with a bug and became a
compiler. The compiler is not the point. The language is not the point.
The point is the question you can now ask about any tensor operation in
any framework:

\textbf{Where is the name?}

If the name is in a comment, it can rot. If the name is in the code, it
can be checked. If the name is nowhere, the intent exists only in your
head---and your head will not be there at 3 AM when the model deployed
on Tuesday produces a customer complaint on Thursday.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-the-names-did-for-you}{%
\subsection{What the Names Did for
You}\label{what-the-names-did-for-you}}

Before this book, you read tensor operations as arithmetic.
\texttt{x.mean(dim=1)} was ``reduce axis 1.'' The operation was
complete. The text contained everything the compiler needed.

After this book, you read \texttt{x.mean(dim=1)} and see an absence. The
axis has no name. The coordinate it erases has no identity. The code
records \emph{where} but not \emph{what}. The operation is incomplete.
The text tells the compiler what to execute. It does not tell the reader
what was intended.

This shift---from seeing operations as complete to seeing them as
underspecified---is the coordinate habit. It is not a skill. It is not a
technique. It is a change in what you notice when you look at a tensor
operation. Every person who reads this book starts at a different point
and ends at a different point. But everyone ends by seeing the gaps.

The gaps were always there. You just didn't have a name for what was
missing.

\hypertarget{the-readers-review}{%
\subsection{The Reader's Review}\label{the-readers-review}}

This chapter has traced the outline the names leave behind. But an
outline is not a review. A review is not something you read---it is
something you do.

So before you close this book, do the review. Not by rereading. By
answering. The following questions walk through every chapter, but they
don't summarize. They ask you to notice what has changed in how you
read.

Take your time. There is no rush. The book will still be here when you
finish.

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapter 1.} You read this line on page one:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

At the time, you saw a tensor operation. \texttt{dim=1} was a position.
Now read it again. What do you see?

Do you see the absent name? The coordinate that \texttt{1} refers
to---is it \texttt{channel}, \texttt{feature}, \texttt{class}? The code
doesn't say. Three months ago, before this book, you might not have
noticed the absence. Now the absence is the loudest thing on the line.

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapter 2.} You wrote
\texttt{out{[}i,\ j{]}\ =\ A{[}i,\ j{]}\ +\ bias{[}j{]}}. The omission
was the point: \texttt{bias} has no \texttt{i}, so it broadcasts.

Now read \texttt{A\ +\ bias} in NumPy. What do you see? Do you see the
broadcast that the code doesn't name? Do you see the coordinate that
\texttt{bias} silently copies along? Do you know, from the code alone,
whether the broadcast is intentional or accidental?

If you can answer the third question, you have learned the broadcast
self-audit. If you cannot---if the code gives you no information to
answer with---you have learned why the self-audit is necessary.

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapter 3.} The Square Matrix Test. Read this:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{softmax(logits, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When \texttt{batch\_size\ ==\ num\_classes}, \texttt{dim=0} and
\texttt{dim=-1} both produce valid probability distributions. The code
is correct either way. The \emph{program} is correct only one way.

Now read \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)}. Does the name
\texttt{class} appear on \texttt{logits}? If not, it's an error. If yes,
it's checked at every call site. The Square Matrix Test is not a trick.
It is a fact: when extents coincide, only names differ. If you don't
write the names, you cannot tell the difference.

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapter 4.} GroupNorm. Read this PyTorch line:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.reshape(N, G, C}\OperatorTok{//}\NormalTok{G, H, W).mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{dim=(2,3,4)} means ``reduce over c\_in\_group, H, W.'' But only
because the reshape put them at those positions. If the reshape changes,
the tuple changes.

Now read
\texttt{mean{[}c\_in\_group,\ H,\ W{]}(x{[}..batch,\ group,\ c\_in\_group,\ H,\ W{]})}.
The bracket names the reduced coordinates. If the layout changes, the
names don't. Which line would you rather be responsible for maintaining
six months from now, at 11 PM, during an incident?

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapter 5.} Causality. \texttt{u{[}t+1,\ i{]}} on the right-hand
side of a recurrence with declaration \texttt{u{[}t\ in\ 1..T,\ i{]}}.
Before this book, would you have caught it? Would the compiler? In
Python, the loop runs. In Einlang, the compiler halts. The difference is
whether time has a direction in your notation.

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapter 6.} The boundary. \texttt{oh\ +\ kh} is valid.
\texttt{oh\ +\ 1000} is valid too---even if \texttt{oh\ +\ 1000}
overflows the input. The compiler checks that \texttt{oh} and
\texttt{kh} are coordinates. It does not check that their sum is in
bounds.

This boundary is a design choice. Why is it here? Because the coordinate
system guarantees that \texttt{oh} and \texttt{kh} are the right
\emph{kind} of thing---spatial indices. It does not guarantee that they
are the right \emph{value}. Bounds checking is the runtime's job.
Semantic correctness is yours. The names reduce the surface area of
uncheckable facts. They do not eliminate it.

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapter 7.} The gradient. Read
\texttt{dA{[}i,\ k{]}\ =\ sum{[}j{]}(dC{[}i,\ j{]}\ *\ B{[}k,\ j{]})}.
You derived this yourself in Chapter 7, from the coordinate sets alone.
Now read \texttt{dA\ =\ dC\ @\ B.T}. Do you know which axes are being
contracted? Do you know why the transpose is there? If you had to
re-derive it from the positional code, could you?

The coordinate accounting gave you set subtraction: \texttt{C} has
\texttt{\{i,\ j\}}, \texttt{A} has \texttt{\{i,\ k\}}, sum over
\texttt{\{j\}}. The positional code gives you \texttt{dC\ @\ B.T}. Both
produce the same result. Only one explains itself.

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapters 8--10.} The comparisons. You saw LayerNorm, RMSNorm,
GroupNorm, attention, and the heat equation in two notations. Each time,
the positional code was correct. Each time, the named code made
different facts visible.

After reading all three comparison chapters, answer this: which bug
would you rather debug at 3 AM---a \texttt{dim=-1} that should have been
\texttt{dim=-2}, or a \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}} where \texttt{class}
doesn't exist? The first is silent. The second is a compiler error. The
compiler cannot prevent all bugs. But it can prevent the ones where the
notation records enough information for the compiler to check.

\vspace{2em}

\textbf{Chapters 11--13.} The compiler. You watched a name travel from
source through IR through analysis through lowering to generated code.
Five forms, one name. \texttt{class} survived every stage. It was
verified at the stage where it was still a name---where the compiler
could ask ``does this name exist on this tensor?'' and get a yes/no
answer. Then it was burned into \texttt{axis=1}---after all checks had
passed.

The positional compiler could not have asked that question, because the
positional compiler never knew the name. The name was in the
programmer's head. The compiler burned a number that was already a
number. No verification occurred.

\vspace{2em}

Now close your eyes. Or look away from the page. Ask yourself one
question:

\textbf{If you had to explain to a colleague why named coordinates
matter---not with arguments, but by showing them a single page from this
book---which page would you choose?}

Your answer to that question is your review. It is not the same as mine.
It doesn't need to be. The book is not a doctrine. It is a lens. What
you see through it depends on what you brought to it.

\vspace{2em}

Now read this line:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{x }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ x.mean(dim}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

What appears in your mind?

Does a coordinate name surface---\texttt{channel}, \texttt{feature},
\texttt{class}? Does a question arise: which coordinate is position 1,
and how do I know? Does the gap between the number and the identity feel
wider than it did seventeen chapters ago?

If the answer to any of these is yes, the book has done its work. Not by
converting you to Einlang. Not by persuading you to rename every
dimension in your codebase. By changing what you notice when you read a
tensor operation. The coordinate habit is that change.

\vspace{2em}

Before you turn the final page, one last exercise. Open a terminal.
Navigate to your most recent project. Pick the first \texttt{.py} file
that contains a tensor operation. Find the first \texttt{dim=},
\texttt{axis=}, or \texttt{permute} call. Read it. Ask: if the dimension
order changed tomorrow, would this line still be correct?

If you can answer with confidence---because a comment records the
coordinate name, because an einops string names the dimensions, because
the variable naming convention is consistent---you have applied the
habit. If you cannot answer with confidence---because the number records
only position, not identity---you have found a ghost. You now know its
name. And you know that naming it is the first step toward making it
visible.

The ghost has been there since before you opened this book. The
difference is that now you can see it. And what you can see, you can
name. And what you can name, you can check.

Turn the page.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-16-the-life-of-a-name}{%
\section{Chapter 16 · The Life of a
Name}\label{chapter-16-the-life-of-a-name}}

\begin{quote}
``It is written. It is preserved. It is verified. It is burned. At no
point is it decoration.''

--- From Chapter 14
\end{quote}

\emph{Reflection · One coordinate name tracked through five forms, from
source to execution}

\vspace{2em}

This chapter is a single exercise, performed slowly.

Take the name \texttt{class}. You first wrote it in Chapter 3, inside a
softmax bracket: \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits)}. It was one letter
among many---five characters, lowercase, easy to type, easy to forget.

But that name did not stay in Chapter 3. It traveled. Through the IR,
through the analysis passes, through the lowering engine, through the
code generator. At each stage, it was read. At each stage, it was used.
At each stage, the compiler asked it questions---what is your range?
where do you appear? are you consumed or preserved?---and the answers
determined whether the program compiled or failed.

This chapter traces that journey. Not as a technical description. As a
walk. You and I, following a single name from the moment it was typed to
the moment it became a number, stopping at each form to see what it
became and what it enabled.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-0-before-the-name}{%
\subsection{Form 0: Before the Name}\label{form-0-before-the-name}}

Before \texttt{class} was a coordinate, it was a position. In a PyTorch
program, the softmax looked like this:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{probs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.softmax(logits, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{-1} is the last axis. If \texttt{logits} has shape
\texttt{(batch,\ class)}, \texttt{-1} is \texttt{class}. If
\texttt{logits} has shape \texttt{(batch,\ feature)}, \texttt{-1} is
\texttt{feature}. If a refactoring changes the layout, \texttt{-1}
silently changes meaning.

The positional form records \emph{where}. It does not record
\emph{what}. The information that would distinguish \texttt{class} from
\texttt{feature} is not in the code. It is in the programmer's head, in
the variable name \texttt{logits}, in the documentation string that may
or may not exist.

Now the programmer writes a different line:

\begin{verbatim}
softmax[class](logits[batch, class])
\end{verbatim}

The name \texttt{class} appears twice: once in the function bracket,
once in the index pattern. It is a string of five characters. It is also
a coordinate identity---a claim that the tensor \texttt{logits} has a
dimension called \texttt{class}, and that \texttt{softmax} will
normalize over it.

This is Form 0. The name as written. What happens next is what this
chapter is about.

But pause on Form 0 a moment longer. It is the easiest form to overlook,
because it looks like the surface syntax you have been reading for
sixteen chapters. But it is also the most important form---because it is
where the name first enters the system. Every later stage depends on
what happened here. If the name is wrong here, no later stage can fix
it. If the name is missing here, no later stage can supply it.

\hypertarget{what-form-0-requires}{%
\subsubsection{What Form 0 Requires}\label{what-form-0-requires}}

Form 0 looks like a single line:
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}batch,\ class{]})}. But to write
that line, the programmer must already know four things:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{That \texttt{logits} has a coordinate called \texttt{class}.}
  This means the coordinate was declared upstream---in the data loader,
  in the model definition, in some \texttt{let} binding that named the
  axes of \texttt{logits}. The name is not invented at the call site. It
  is cited.
\item
  \textbf{That \texttt{softmax} consumes that coordinate.} The function
  bracket \texttt{{[}class{]}} is the programmer's declaration: ``I am
  passing \texttt{class} as the coordinate argument to
  \texttt{softmax}.'' The compiler will check that \texttt{class}
  exists. The programmer must know it exists before writing it.
\item
  \textbf{That \texttt{class} is the right coordinate to normalize
  over.} This is not a fact the compiler can check. It is a fact the
  programmer must decide. The slot for the name is in the syntax. The
  decision of which name to put in the slot is the programmer's. The
  compiler will check consistency. It cannot check correctness.
\item
  \textbf{That no other coordinate in \texttt{logits} should be
  normalized over.} The function bracket chooses one coordinate. The
  choice excludes others. If \texttt{logits} has
  \texttt{(batch,\ class)} and the programmer writes
  \texttt{softmax{[}class{]}}, they are claiming: \emph{batch is not the
  normalization axis.} The bracket records the choice. The omission
  records the rejection.
\end{enumerate}

These four facts are present in the programmer's mind when they type
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}batch,\ class{]})}. In the
positional version \texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=-1)}, only one of these
facts is recorded: ``normalize over the last axis.'' The other
three---that \texttt{class} exists, that it is the intended
normalization axis, that \texttt{batch} is not---are absent. They live
in the programmer's head. They are not in the source.

Form 0 is the moment where intent becomes text. The text is
sparse---five characters for the name, two brackets to hold it. But the
intent it records is rich. The name \texttt{class} is a fact. The
bracket \texttt{{[}...{]}} is a claim: ``this coordinate is the one the
function operates on.'' Every later form verifies that claim. But the
claim itself is made here, in Form 0, by the programmer pressing five
keys.

\hypertarget{the-counterfactual}{%
\subsubsection{The Counterfactual}\label{the-counterfactual}}

What would Form 0 look like if names were absent? The programmer writes:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{probs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ torch.softmax(logits, dim}\OperatorTok{=-}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

What facts are in this line? \texttt{softmax} normalizes over the last
axis. What is the last axis? Whatever \texttt{logits.shape{[}-1{]}} is.
What should it be? The programmer knows. The code doesn't.

The programmer could add a comment: \texttt{\#\ dim=-1\ is\ class}. Now
the fact is in the source---but it is in a comment, not in a bracket.
The comment can rot. The bracket can't. A year later, \texttt{logits}
changes shape. The comment still says \texttt{\#\ dim=-1\ is\ class}.
The code now normalizes over \texttt{feature}. The comment is wrong. The
bracket would have been wrong too---but the bracket is checked. The
compiler would have said: \texttt{class} not found on \texttt{logits}.
The comment is silent. The code runs. The bug survives.

Form 0 is the only form where a name is a comment. The difference is
that the comment is in a bracket that the compiler reads. The bracket
turns a hope into a check. The name \texttt{class} in
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}} is the same five characters as
\texttt{\#\ dim=-1\ is\ class}. The difference is not the characters. It
is who reads them.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-1-the-parse-tree}{%
\subsection{Form 1: The Parse Tree}\label{form-1-the-parse-tree}}

The compiler reads the source. The parser turns characters into tokens,
tokens into a tree. The name \texttt{class} survives this transformation
unchanged:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(call softmax}
\NormalTok{  (index logits (batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (coord-args }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The parser does not know what \texttt{class} means. It knows that
\texttt{class} appears in two places: as a coordinate argument in
\texttt{coord-args}, and as an element in the index list of
\texttt{logits}. It records both appearances faithfully.

At this stage, \texttt{class} is a symbol in a tree. It has no range, no
type, no positional mapping. It is a name and nothing more. But it is in
the right place: the \texttt{coord-args} slot says ``this name will be
bound to the function's coordinate parameter,'' and the index list says
``this tensor is indexed by this name.''

The parser's job is to recognize structure. It recognized that
\texttt{softmax{[}class{]}(logits{[}batch,\ class{]})} is a function
call with a coordinate argument and a value argument. It placed
\texttt{class} in the correct slots. The structure is now queryable by
every subsequent pass.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-2-after-name-resolution}{%
\subsection{Form 2: After Name
Resolution}\label{form-2-after-name-resolution}}

Name resolution connects every occurrence of \texttt{class} to its
definition.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(call softmax}
\NormalTok{  (index logits (batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))   }\CommentTok{;; class → defined on logits, 2nd axis}
\NormalTok{  (coord-args }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))             }\CommentTok{;; class → bound to softmax's j parameter}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The resolver asks: where is \texttt{class} defined? It finds the
definition on \texttt{logits}---the tensor was declared with coordinates
\texttt{(batch,\ class)}. It finds the binding in \texttt{softmax}'s
signature---\texttt{fn\ softmax{[}j{]}(x:\ {[}f32;\ ..left,\ j,\ ..right{]})},
and \texttt{class} is bound to \texttt{j}.

Two occurrences. Two resolutions. The first says: \texttt{class} is the
second coordinate of \texttt{logits}. The second says: \texttt{class} is
the coordinate argument to \texttt{softmax}, bound to parameter
\texttt{j}. If either resolution failed---if \texttt{logits} had no
\texttt{class}, or if \texttt{softmax} expected no coordinate
parameter---the compiler would stop here.

This is the first moment the name becomes load-bearing. Before
resolution, \texttt{class} was just characters. After resolution,
\texttt{class} is a reference---a pointer to a definition. If the
definition doesn't exist, the reference is dangling. The compiler
reports it. The programmer fixes it. The name has done its first job.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-3-after-analysis-1}{%
\subsection{Form 3: After Analysis}\label{form-3-after-analysis-1}}

Chapter 13's analysis passes walk the resolved tree. They derive range,
shape, and type. They apply the five check rules. And they annotate
every node with what they find.

After analysis, \texttt{class} carries annotations:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(call softmax}
\NormalTok{  (index logits (batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (coord-args }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\CommentTok{;; ── analysis ──}
\CommentTok{;; class: (range 0 n_class), reduction axis}
\CommentTok{;; Rule 1 ✓ — class exists on logits}
\CommentTok{;; Rule 2 ✓ — class appears in all operands of max[class] and sum[class]}
\CommentTok{;; Rule 5 ✓ — class bound to j, contract satisfied}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The name \texttt{class} now has a range: \texttt{0..n\_class}. The
compiler derived this from \texttt{logits}'s shape declaration. It knows
that \texttt{class} spans \texttt{n\_class} elements. It knows that
\texttt{class} is consumed by reductions inside \texttt{softmax}. It
knows that the coordinate contract at the call site is satisfied.

Three check marks. Three verifications that the name is used
consistently. If any had failed---if \texttt{class} didn't exist, or
wasn't consumed by the right reductions, or didn't match the function's
contract---the compiler would have reported an error. The name enabled
the check. Without the name, there is nothing to check.

Compare to \texttt{dim=-1}. The positional compiler sees \texttt{-1} and
says: ``valid integer.'' It does not ask whether \texttt{-1} is the
right integer. It does not know that \texttt{-1} is supposed to mean
\texttt{class}. The name-based compiler asks and answers. The positional
compiler cannot ask.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-4-after-lowering-1}{%
\subsection{Form 4: After Lowering}\label{form-4-after-lowering-1}}

Chapter 14's lowering pass maps names to numbers. \texttt{class}---the
word you typed---becomes \texttt{axis=1}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{(call softmax}
\NormalTok{  (index logits (batch }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{  (coord-args }\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{))}
\CommentTok{;; ── lowering ──}
\CommentTok{;; batch → axis=0, loop over 0..batch_size}
\CommentTok{;; class → axis=1, reduction}
\CommentTok{;; keepdims=True (inferred from broadcast requirement)}
\CommentTok{;; strategy: vectorized}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Why \texttt{axis=1}? Because the declaration order is
\texttt{(batch,\ class)}. \texttt{batch} is first → axis 0.
\texttt{class} is second → axis 1. The mapping is deterministic. You can
verify it by hand.

Why \texttt{reduction}? Because \texttt{softmax} contains
\texttt{max{[}class{]}} and \texttt{sum{[}class{]}}. Both consume
\texttt{class}. The lowering pass reads the reduction annotations from
analysis and emits the reduction axis.

Why \texttt{keepdims=True}? Because the subtraction
\texttt{logits\ -\ max} requires \texttt{max} to broadcast back over
\texttt{class}. The analyzed shapes showed the broadcast requirement.
The lowering pass inferred the \texttt{keepdims} from it. The programmer
didn't write \texttt{keepdims=True}. The compiler derived it.

Why \texttt{vectorized}? Because all ranges are known at compile time.
The shapes are concrete. NumPy's C kernels can handle the entire
operation. If arrays were jagged, the strategy would be \texttt{scalar}.
The compiler chooses.

Every annotation on \texttt{class} from Form 3 has been translated into
an execution decision. The range became a loop bound. The reduction
annotation became \texttt{axis=1}. The broadcast requirement became
\texttt{keepdims=True}. The name \texttt{class} is still in the
tree---it hasn't been erased---but it is now accompanied by the numbers
that will replace it.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{form-5-the-generated-code-1}{%
\subsection{Form 5: The Generated
Code}\label{form-5-the-generated-code-1}}

The code generator reads the lowered tree and emits Python:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ softmax(logits):}
\NormalTok{    m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(logits, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    e }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.exp(logits }\OperatorTok{-}\NormalTok{ m)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ e }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{sum}\NormalTok{(e, axis}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, keepdims}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{class} is gone. In its place is \texttt{axis=1}. The name has
been burned. The number remains.

But---and this is the point of the entire journey---the number is
\textbf{correct} because the name was \textbf{verified}. \texttt{axis=1}
is not a guess. It is not a convention. It is not ``the last axis,
whatever that happens to be.'' It is the axis that was named
\texttt{class} in the source, preserved through the IR, verified by
analysis, and mapped to 1 by lowering.

The number inherits the correctness of the name. If the name was
right---if the programmer correctly identified \texttt{class} as the
normalization coordinate---then \texttt{axis=1} is right. If the name
was wrong---if the programmer wrote \texttt{class} but should have
written \texttt{feature}---then \texttt{axis=1} is wrong. But the error
is a name error, caught by the checks in Form 3 (does \texttt{feature}
exist on this tensor? no → error). The name-based pipeline catches name
errors. The positional pipeline catches nothing.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-five-forms-side-by-side}{%
\subsection{The Five Forms, Side by
Side}\label{the-five-forms-side-by-side}}

\begin{verbatim}
Form 0 (Source):       softmax[class](logits[batch, class])
Form 1 (Parse):        (call softmax (index logits (batch class)) (coord-args class))
Form 2 (Resolved):     class → defined on logits, bound to softmax's j
Form 3 (Analyzed):     class: range 0..n_class, reduction, Rules 1/2/5 ✓
Form 4 (Lowered):      class → axis=1, reduction, keepdims=True, vectorized
Form 5 (Code):         np.max(logits, axis=1, keepdims=True)
\end{verbatim}

Five forms. One name. Five stages of increasing concreteness. At each
stage, the name did work that a number could not have done.

A number can be an axis. A number cannot be checked for existence. A
number cannot be verified against a function's coordinate contract. A
number cannot tell the lowering pass whether it's a reduction axis or a
loop axis---both are just integers. The name distinguishes. The name
enables. The name is burned only after all its work is done.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{what-the-name-enabled}{%
\subsection{What the Name Enabled}\label{what-the-name-enabled}}

Let's be precise about what \texttt{class} made possible at each stage.

\textbf{Form 1 (Parse):} The name \texttt{class} in the
\texttt{coord-args} position told the parser: this is a coordinate
argument, not a value argument. The parser placed it in the correct AST
slot. A positional \texttt{dim=-1} would be a value
argument---indistinguishable from any other integer argument until
semantic analysis. The name disambiguates at the syntax level.

\textbf{Form 2 (Resolution):} The resolver connected \texttt{class} in
the call to \texttt{j} in the function signature. It verified that
\texttt{logits} carries a coordinate called \texttt{class}. These are
identity checks---do the names match? Positional resolution connects
\texttt{-1} to the last axis. That connection is always valid,
regardless of whether the last axis is the intended one.

\textbf{Form 3 (Analysis):} The analysis passes derived \texttt{class}'s
range from \texttt{logits}'s declaration. They checked that
\texttt{class} appears in every operand of the reductions inside
\texttt{softmax}. They verified that the coordinate contract at the call
site is satisfied. These are semantic checks, enabled by the name's
presence in the tree.

\textbf{Form 4 (Lowering):} The lowering pass mapped \texttt{class} to
\texttt{axis=1} using the declaration order. It inferred
\texttt{keepdims=True} from the broadcast requirement in the
subtraction. These are engineering decisions, made deterministically
from the analyzed annotations.

\textbf{Form 5 (Code):} The generated code is correct because every
decision that produced it was verified. The integer \texttt{1} is not
arbitrary. It is the integer that corresponds to the coordinate named
\texttt{class}, which was checked for existence, verified for
consistency, and mapped deterministically to its position.

The name enabled every check. Remove the name, and every check
collapses. The positional pipeline has only one check: ``is
\texttt{axis=1} a valid integer?'' It is. Always. The check is vacuous.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-names-life-and-the-programmers}{%
\subsection{The Name's Life and the
Programmer's}\label{the-names-life-and-the-programmers}}

Now step back. Not the compiler's perspective---yours.

You wrote \texttt{class}. Five characters. You pressed the keys. And
because you wrote them, the compiler asked questions that it could not
have asked otherwise. Because the compiler asked, bugs that would have
survived to runtime were caught at compile time. Because they were
caught, you didn't spend three hours at 3 AM tracing a shape mismatch
backward through twelve layers.

The name's life is the compiler's work. But the name's value is your
time.

Every \texttt{dim=-1} in your codebase is a name that was never written.
Every one of them is a question the compiler couldn't ask. Every one of
them is a potential 3 AM.

The name \texttt{class} took five keystrokes to write. It enabled five
stages of verification. The ratio is not merely favorable. It is the
difference between a program that is correct by construction and a
program that is correct by coincidence.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{when-the-name-is-wrong}{%
\subsection{When the Name Is Wrong}\label{when-the-name-is-wrong}}

The chapter has traced \texttt{class} through a journey where every
check passed. But what happens when the name is wrong?

Suppose the programmer writes
\texttt{softmax{[}batch{]}(logits{[}batch,\ class{]})}. \texttt{batch}
is the coordinate argument. \texttt{softmax} expects \texttt{j}---a
coordinate it will consume in reductions and reconstruct in the output.
\texttt{batch} is bound to \texttt{j}.

Form 1 (Parse): passes. \texttt{batch} is a valid symbol in the
\texttt{coord-args} position.

Form 2 (Resolution): passes. \texttt{batch} exists on \texttt{logits}
(the declaration says \texttt{{[}batch,\ class{]}}). \texttt{batch} is
bound to \texttt{j} in \texttt{softmax}'s signature.

Form 3 (Analysis): Rule 5---Coordinate Contract. \texttt{softmax}'s
signature says \texttt{j} appears in \texttt{..left,\ j,\ ..right}. The
packs are bound: \texttt{..left} is empty. \texttt{..right} is
\texttt{{[}class{]}}. The return type is
\texttt{{[}f32;\ batch,\ class{]}}. But wait---\texttt{j} is
\texttt{batch}, and \texttt{softmax} consumes \texttt{j} in a reduction.
The reduction \texttt{sum{[}j{]}} consumes \texttt{batch}. The function
contracts \texttt{j} and then reconstructs it. But \texttt{batch} was
never supposed to be contracted---it was supposed to pass through in
\texttt{..left}. The error is that \texttt{batch} was used as the
coordinate parameter when it should have been a pack.

What error does the compiler emit? It depends on how the contract is
checked. If the compiler checks that the coordinate parameter \texttt{j}
is distinct from the pack coordinates, it would catch this:
\texttt{batch} is both the coordinate parameter AND a pack coordinate?
No---packs are bound at the call site. \texttt{batch} is a concrete
coordinate from the argument \texttt{logits{[}batch,\ class{]}}. When
\texttt{softmax{[}batch{]}} is called, \texttt{batch} is bound to
\texttt{j}. Then \texttt{j} is consumed inside \texttt{softmax}. The
function returns \texttt{{[}f32;\ batch,\ class{]}}---but \texttt{batch}
was consumed and reconstructed, which means the function's semantics
are: ``normalize over batch.'' The code compiles. It produces a valid
probability distribution---over the batch dimension, not the class
dimension.

The name was wrong. The check passed. The program is incorrect.

This is the boundary from Chapter 7 and Chapter 15, restated for the
final time: \textbf{names check consistency, not correctness.}
\texttt{softmax{[}batch{]}} is internally consistent---every reduction,
broadcast, and gradient aligns over \texttt{batch}. The error is that
the programmer wanted \texttt{class}. The compiler cannot read the
programmer's mind. It can only verify the contract the programmer wrote.

But the name \texttt{batch} is visible. When the next programmer reads
\texttt{softmax{[}batch{]}(logits)}, they see the error
immediately---``why are we normalizing over batch?'' The name makes the
error readable. The positional equivalent
\texttt{softmax(logits,\ dim=0)} hides the error behind a position
number. The reader sees \texttt{dim=0} and must reconstruct whether axis
0 is batch or class. The reconstruction may be wrong. The name
\texttt{batch} in \texttt{softmax{[}batch{]}} carries its meaning with
it.

A wrong name is a visible error. A missing name is an invisible one.
This has been the book's refrain since Chapter 1. The refrain survives
the final chapter because it is the one claim that every chapter has
tested and none has falsified.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{burned-not-lost}{%
\subsection{Burned, Not Lost}\label{burned-not-lost}}

The name is burned. \texttt{class} became \texttt{axis=1}. The word is
not in the generated code. The CPU will never read it.

But the name is not lost. It is in the source code, where you can read
it. It is in the compiler's analysis log, where the checks are recorded.
It is in the error message, if a future refactoring breaks the contract.
It is in the lowering annotations, documenting why \texttt{axis=1} was
chosen.

The name burned. The heat remains. And the heat is a program whose axes
are correct---not because the programmer memorized the dimension order,
but because the compiler verified the coordinate names.

This is what the seventeen chapters of this book have been building
toward. Not a language. Not a compiler. A guarantee: \textbf{if you
write the name, the compiler will check it. If the compiler checks it,
the execution will match the intent. If the intent changes, the name
changes---and the compiler will tell you everywhere the old name was
used.}

The guarantee is not perfect. Names can be wrong---you can name the
wrong coordinate \texttt{class}. But a wrong name is a visible error. A
missing name is an invisible one. The book has argued that the second
kind is more dangerous, because it compounds silently. The name
\texttt{class}---burned, verified, gone---is the answer to that
argument. It was here. It did its work. The code is correct because it
was.

\vspace{2em}

\emph{You first wrote \texttt{class} in Chapter 3. It has now traveled
through every chapter of this book---through primitives, combinations,
comparisons, construction, and reflection. It was written, preserved,
verified, and burned. Close your eyes. Trace its journey in your mind.
The path you trace is the coordinate habit.}

\vspace{2em}

This chapter traced one name through five forms. But the name was not
the point. The point was what each form made possible.

The parse tree made the name queryable by compiler passes. The resolved
tree made it verifiable against its definition. The analyzed tree made
its consistency checkable across the whole program. The lowered tree
made it mappable to a position number. The generated code made it
executable.

At each stage, the name enabled a question that could not have been
asked without it. ``Does this tensor have a coordinate called
\texttt{class}?'' → Form 1. ``Is \texttt{class} bound to the right
parameter?'' → Form 2. ``Is \texttt{class} consumed consistently by
every reduction?'' → Form 3. ``What position does \texttt{class} map
to?'' → Form 4. ``Is that position the correct one?'' → Form 5.

Five questions. Five answers. One name. The name was the key that
unlocked each question. Without it, the compiler asks only: ``is this a
valid integer?'' That question has one answer: yes, always. The
positional pipeline is a pipeline of one question. The named pipeline is
a pipeline of five. The difference is not the number of questions. It is
what the questions verify.

The next time you write \texttt{dim=-1}, remember: the positional
compiler asked one question of that argument (``is -1 a valid
integer?''). The named compiler would have asked five---existence,
binding, consistency, position, and correctness of that position. The
four unasked questions are the gaps where bugs live.

\newpage

\hypertarget{chapter-17-the-complete-picture}{%
\section{Chapter 17 · The Complete
Picture}\label{chapter-17-the-complete-picture}}

\begin{quote}
``The purpose of computing is insight, not numbers.''

--- Richard Hamming
\end{quote}

\emph{Reference · Thought map and syntax at a glance}

\vspace{2em}

This chapter contains no new syntax. It is a map of territory already
explored.

The preceding fourteen chapters introduced Einlang's grammar piece by
piece, each piece arriving when the concept it served had earned its
introduction. The result is a working knowledge of the language, but the
pieces are scattered across chapters. This chapter assembles them into
one place, organized by category rather than by pedagogical necessity.

Think of it as the view from the summit. You climbed the mountain one
trail at a time. Now you can see the whole range.

But before I show you my map, draw your own.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{build-your-own-map}{%
\subsection{Build Your Own Map}\label{build-your-own-map}}

Take out a piece of paper. Or open a blank document. At the top, write:
\texttt{dim=1\ bug}.

Now draw arrows downward. Each arrow is a question the bug forced us to
ask. \emph{``Which coordinate did I just erase?''} → naming.
\emph{``Which coordinate is being reduced?''} → reduction bracket.
\emph{``Why can't the compiler check this?''} → coordinate contracts.

Don't look at the next section yet. Draw from memory. What were the big
ideas? How do they connect? Which chapters depend on which?

Five minutes. Go.

\vspace{2em}

Done? Good. Now look at your map and ask three questions:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Which arrow did you forget?} Everyone forgets at least one.
  The arrow you forgot connects two ideas you hadn't realized were
  dependent on each other. That connection is the thing you haven't
  fully internalized yet.
\item
  \textbf{Which arrow did you draw but can't explain why it exists?} You
  remembered that A depends on B, but you can't articulate the
  dependency. That arrow is a memory, not an understanding. Go back to
  the chapter where that arrow was first drawn and reread the
  transition.
\item
  \textbf{Which idea has the most arrows pointing to it?} That idea is
  the load-bearing concept. In this book, it is almost certainly ``a
  coordinate has a name.'' Everything else depends on it. If you had to
  explain the book in one sentence, that concept would be in it.
\end{enumerate}

The map you drew is not the final answer. It is a snapshot of your
understanding at this moment. A month from now, draw it again. The
arrows will have moved. Some will have disappeared---their dependencies
now obvious. Others will have appeared---connections you didn't see the
first time.

Learning is not the accumulation of facts. It is the continuous
redrawing of the map.

\vspace{2em}

\hypertarget{the-thought-map-one-version}{%
\subsection{The Thought Map (One
Version)}\label{the-thought-map-one-version}}

Here is one version of the map. It is not the only version. Compare it
to yours. Where do they agree? Where do they differ? The differences are
not errors---they are perspectives.

Before the syntax reference, a map of how the ideas connect. Each arrow
is a dependency: the idea at the tail must be understood before the idea
at the head.

\begin{verbatim}
dim=1 bug (Prologue)
    │
    ▼
A coordinate has a name, a domain, a position (Ch1)
    │
    ├──► Permutation: names survive position changes (Ch1)
    │
    ├──► Reduction: the consumed coordinate is named (Ch2)
    │       │
    │       └──► Broadcasting: the omitted coordinate is visible (Ch2)
    │               │
    │               └──► Inversion Rule: broadcast ↔ reduction dual (Ch2, Ch7)
    │
    ├──► Coordinate-aware functions: names as type-level contracts (Ch3)
    │       │
    │       ├──► Square Matrix Test: when extents equal, only names differ (Ch3)
    │       │
    │       ├──► Pack polymorphism: ..batch absorbs unknown leading dims (Ch4)
    │       │
    │       └──► Normalization skeleton: one pattern, four functions (Ch4)
    │
    ├──► Recurrence: time as a directional coordinate (Ch5)
    │       │
    │       └──► Causality constraint: t-1 valid, t+1 rejected (Ch5)
    │
    ├──► Complex terrain: splits, arithmetic, disambiguation (Ch6)
    │
    ├──► Differentiation: the pullback reads the forward pass backward (Ch7)
    │       │
    │       └──► @fn: custom derivative rules carry coordinate contracts (Ch7)
    │
    ├──► Comparisons: same computation, two notations (Ch8–10)
    │       │
    │       ├──► Normalization: GroupNorm reshape chain vs named groups (Ch8)
    │       ├──► Attention: identical PyTorch, distinct Einlang signatures (Ch9)
    │       └──► Physics: integer field indices vs named field coordinates (Ch10)
    │
    └──► Compiler construction (Ch11–13)
            │
